### THE
# SENTIMENTS
### OF
# PHILO JUDAEUS <note>original: "Philo Judæus," a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher from Alexandria who lived during the time of Christ and the Apostles.</note>
### CONCERNING THE
## LOGOS <note>original: "$\Lambda$O$\Gamma$O$\Sigma$," a Greek term meaning "Word," "Reason," or "Plan." In this context, it refers to the divine intellect or the "Word of God" as described in the Gospel of John.</note>, OR WORD OF GOD;
### TOGETHER WITH
## LARGE EXTRACTS FROM HIS WRITINGS
## COMPARED WITH THE SCRIPTURES
### ON MANY OTHER
## PARTICULAR AND ESSENTIAL DOCTRINES
## OF THE CHRISTIAN RELIGION.
### *By* JACOB BRYANT.

---

->CAMBRIDGE,<-
->PRINTED BY JOHN BURGES, PRINTER TO THE<-
->UNIVERSITY;<-
->AND SOLD BY J. DEIGHTON, CAMBRIDGE;<-
->MESSRS. CADELL & DAVIES, P. ELMSLEY,<-
->AND T. PAYNE, LONDON.<-
->1797.<- <note>original: "MDCCXCVII"</note>

<summary>This title page introduces Jacob Bryant's study of the writings of Philo Judaeus, specifically examining how Philo's concept of the "Logos" (Word) aligns with Christian theology and scripture. Published in 1797, the work seeks to harmonize Hellenistic Jewish philosophy with the essential doctrines of Christianity.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Logos, Word of God, Jacob Bryant, Christian Doctrine, 18th-century theology, Hellenistic Judaism, Cambridge University Press</keywords>

# ->PREFACE<-

A variety of prejudices prevail in the world regarding the Christian religion—some against it in general, and others against its specific doctrines. Therefore, it is very providential <note>meaning occurring through divine foresight or intervention</note> that we have, on the other hand, an equal variety of evidence to counteract this evil and remedy its consequences. 

The early <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>the influential Christian theologians and writers of the first few centuries AD</note> show plainly what the doctrine and faith of the Church were in their time. Our own nation has also produced excellent writers who have vindicated <note>defended or justified</note> our holy religion and maintained its doctrines. Yet the common argument is still raised that those who have embraced a system will naturally support it, and those who profit from the "image" will certainly uphold the shrine <note>An allusion to Acts 19:24-27, suggesting that people defend religious institutions only because they benefit from them financially or socially.</note>.

<vocab>Christian Religion, early Church Fathers, Church Doctrine, Faith, Holy Religion, common argument</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces the preface by noting that while Christianity faces many prejudices, historical evidence from the Church Fathers and modern scholars exists to defend it, despite critics' claims of bias.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, Church Fathers, apologetics, religious prejudice, historical evidence</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous page, where he discusses using external, non-Christian sources to validate Christian doctrine. The text begins mid-sentence, referring to the "assent" (agreement) of a specific philosopher.</meta>

vi PREFACE.

...the agreement of someone who took not the slightest notice of their Author and probably held him in contempt.

It must be admitted that this Platonic philosopher was generally inclined toward mystery and intellectual complexity <note>original: "refinement"; in this context, it refers to overly intricate or abstract reasoning</note>. However, regarding the doctrines <note>original: "Articles"</note> that he borrowed from Christianity, he is perfectly clear and precise, and his testimony is undeniably valid. Therefore, great benefit can be gained from his evidence. Some very learned and clever writers have recently noted this and have proven it so thoroughly that any further investigation <note>original: "prosecution"</note> of the subject might seem unnecessary. 

But since I believe there is still an unexplored path left open, I have ventured to follow its direction. I am striving, if possible, to add to the benefits provided by those learned writers.

<summary>The author explains that while the unnamed Platonic philosopher was often obscure, his clear references to Christian doctrines provide valid historical evidence. The author intends to build upon the work of previous scholars by exploring a new line of reasoning regarding this evidence.</summary>

<keywords>Platonism, Christian doctrine, historical evidence, apologetics, Platonic philosophy, religious scholarship</keywords>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE. vii</header>

As the most fascinating and important point <note>original: "Article"</note> that Philo discusses is the nature of the <term>LOGOS</term> <note>original: "Logos"; a Greek term meaning "word," "reason," or "plan," used in theology to describe the divine intellect or the second person of the Trinity</note>, or <term>DIVINE WORD</term>, I will begin with his evidence on that subject <note>original: "head"</note>. I will only first provide an introduction to the ideas of the first religious innovators <note>Likely referring to early Christian or Gnostic thinkers who were contemporary with Philo</note> who lived during his time or followed immediately after.

---

I cannot conclude this Preface without offering my sincerest thanks to the Gentlemen of the Syndicate at Cambridge <note>The Syndicate is a governing committee at the University of Cambridge, in this case overseeing the University Press</note> for their repeated kindness in allowing this treatise, following a previous one, to be printed at the university press.

->J. B.<-

->CYPENHAM, January 3, 1797.<-

...the agreement <note>original: "assent"</note> of one who has paid no attention to their author and likely held him in contempt.

It must be admitted that this Platonic philosopher <note>Referring to Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who blended Greek philosophy with Jewish scripture</note> was generally inclined toward <note>original: "addicted to"</note> mystery and complex reasoning <note>original: "refinement"</note>. Great benefits can therefore be gained from his evidence. Some very learned and clever writers have recently noted this and have proved it so well that any further investigation <note>original: "prosecution"</note> of the subject might seem unnecessary. Yet, because I believe there is still an unexplored path, I have dared to follow its direction, striving, if possible, to build upon the insights provided by those learned writers.

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

As the most fascinating and significant topic <note>original: "curious and interesting Article"</note> upon which Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Hebrew Bible with Greek philosophy.</note> focuses is the nature of the **Logos**, or **Divine Word**, I shall begin with his evidence on that subject. First, however, I will introduce the ideas of the early religious innovators <note>Likely referring to early Gnostics or Christian thinkers who adapted Philo's concepts.</note> who lived during his time or followed immediately after him.

---

I cannot conclude this Preface without returning my sincerest thanks to the gentlemen of the <term>Syndicate</term> <note>The governing body responsible for managing the affairs of the Cambridge University Press.</note> at Cambridge for their ongoing kindness in permitting this treatise, following a previous one, to be printed at the University Press.

->J. B.<- <note>Jacob Bryant (1715–1804) was a prominent English scholar and mythographer known for his works on ancient history and theology.</note>

Cippenham <note>original: "Cypenham"</note>, January 3, 1797.

# PHILO JUDAEUS <note>Philo Judaeus (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a prominent Hellenistic Jewish philosopher from Alexandria. His writings attempted to harmonize the Hebrew Scriptures with Greek philosophy, particularly Platonism.</note>

---

## PART I.

### PREFACE <note>original: "PROÆM," a term for an introductory statement or prologue.</note>

Our Savior, while he was upon earth, gave an account of himself and of his mission, and displayed the high character which he bore; and this, after his death, was confirmed by his Apostles. From these evidences, we find that he was the Son of God—both God and man—and consequently possessed of two natures, human and divine. Accordingly, just before his ascension, he gave his last mandate <note>original: "mandate," meaning a formal command or commission.</note> to his Apostles: that they should go and instruct all nations—

<summary>This introductory page establishes the theological premise of the work, asserting the dual nature of Christ (divine and human) as confirmed by the Apostles and his final command to spread his teachings to all nations.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Christology, dual nature of Christ, Apostles, Great Commission, Savior, mission</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the Great Commission from the previous page, completing the quotation from the Gospel of Matthew. The catchword "OF" at the bottom suggests the next page begins with a discussion of the nature of God or a specific group.</meta>

<margin>¹ Matthew 28:19.</margin>
...baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit.¹ Therefore, we find that he introduced himself—and consequently ranked himself—alongside God, the Creator and Lord of the universe. This is a level of eminence <note>original: "eminence," meaning a position of superior rank or status.</note> which no man, nor any created being, no matter how high or excellent, could have dared to assume. At the same time, we are told that he was born of a virgin and appeared in the humble form of a man. We are taught and commanded to believe that he was perfectly God and perfectly man <note>This refers to the "hypostatic union," the Christian doctrine that Jesus Christ is both fully divine and fully human.</note>.

Pious and learned men have used these arguments, and many others to the same effect, to confirm the faith of the world in general; therefore, it may seem unnecessary to present them again. However, I have been obliged to repeat what has been said before, as many people have fallen away from the faith. It is my purpose to show the way they have deviated from the truth and the extremes into which they have fallen.

<vocab>theology, divinity of Christ, Trinity, incarnation, Matthew 28:19, Father, Son, Holy Spirit</vocab>

<summary>The author completes the Great Commission and asserts Christ’s dual nature as both fully divine and fully human. He explains that he is repeating these established doctrines to address the rise of religious deviation and apostasy.</summary>
<keywords>Matthew 28:19, Divinity of Christ, Holy Spirit, Trinity, Incarnation, religious deviation, Christology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of Christology, shifting from the established doctrine of Christ's dual nature (fully God and fully man) to a historical analysis of how different groups have deviated from this belief. It sets up a contrast between modern skeptics and ancient heretics.</meta>

## ON THE CONTRADICTION AND INCONSISTENCY THAT HAS PREVAILED.

From this, we can see how fickle <note>original: "wayward"</note> and eccentric people are at different times, and how inconsistent they are in their opinions. Today, we attempt to set aside the divinity of Christ; he is reduced by some to the status of an angel, by others he is respected as a prophet, and by others he is ranked as a mere man. 

But it was not this way in ancient times, during the first ages of Christianity. Many of those who broke away <note>original: "seceded"</note> from the early <note>original: "infant"</note> church strayed in the opposite direction. They acknowledged his divine nature but denied his human nature. Because the miracles of Christ were so well-attested <note>meaning confirmed by evidence or witnesses</note> and so wonderfully displayed at the same time, they could not believe that they were the work of a man. Some of these people lived in the days of the Apostles themselves, and others in the following century; therefore, they had no doubt about these works <note>original: "operations," referring to the miracles performed</note>. However, they perverted <note>original: "abused," in the sense of misusing or being deceived in their reasoning</note> their faith and would not allow that these miracles were performed by any human power.

<vocab>Divinity of Christ, Christianity, Apostles, miracles, human power, fickle, eccentric</vocab>

<summary>The author highlights the inconsistency of religious deviation, noting that while modern skeptics often deny Christ's divinity, ancient groups (such as the Gnostics or Docetists) were so overwhelmed by the evidence of his miracles that they denied his humanity instead.</summary>
<keywords>Christology, divinity of Christ, humanity of Christ, early church, miracles, Apostles, religious history, heresy</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's historical analysis of Christology. While the previous page noted that ancient heretics denied Christ’s humanity because they were so impressed by his miracles, this page argues that Jesus anticipated this error by frequently calling himself the "Son of man."</meta>

Thus, Christ’s humanity <note>original: "manhood"</note> was denied then, just as his divinity is denied now.

This falling away <note>original: "falling off"</note> was not unforeseen, and it seems our Savior acted continually to prevent it <note>original: "obviated," meaning to anticipate and prevent a problem</note>. Although he declared to the world that he was the Son of God and came from his Father, he also strongly and repeatedly emphasized <note>original: "inculcates"</note> that he was also the Son of man. Accordingly, it is said:

—¹ *The Son of man goes.* 
² *The Son of man shall come in his glory.* 
³ *The Son of man shall be three days and three nights in the heart of the earth.* 
⁴ *The Son of man must be lifted up. The Son of man must suffer.* 
⁵ *The Son of man is delivered into the hands of wicked men.* 
⁶ *The foxes have holes, and the birds of the air have nests; but the Son of man has nowhere to lay his head.* 
⁷ *Judas, do you betray the Son of man with a kiss?*

It is remarkable that after the death of 
our

our Savior, the Apostles never use these terms, nor do they call him the **Son of man** anymore. Since he had now been received into glory and become the Lord of life, they speak of him in a different way. Nevertheless, whenever the opportunity arises, they never fail to emphasize <note>original: "insist upon"</note> his human nature. It is, therefore, strongly reinforced <note>original: "inculcated"</note> that:

—² *Christ came in the flesh.* 
³ *God was revealed <note>original: "manifested"</note> in the flesh.* 
⁴ *The Word was made flesh.* 
⁵ *Christ suffered in the flesh.* 
⁶ *Christ was put to death in the flesh.* 
⁷ *In him (Christ) dwells all the fullness of the Godhead <note>original: "Godhead," referring to his divine nature or essence</note> in bodily form.*

### CONCERNING THE FIRST INDIVIDUALS WHO ABANDONED CHURCH DOCTRINE

One of the first of those who broke away from the Gospel truth in this manner was Nicolaus. His followers were called "Nicolaitans" <note>original: "Nicolaitæ"</note> after him and were later absorbed into the diverse <note>original: "multifarious"</note> group known as the Gnostics. He lived at the same time as the Apostles and is mentioned as an apostate <term>apostate: a person who renounces their religious belief</term> by Saint $^3$ John. He pioneered this false doctrine by claiming that Christ did not suffer, nor did he ever appear...

$^2$ 1 Corinthians 15:12, 13.
$^3$ Revelation 2:6 and 15. The doctrine of the Nicolaitans—*which thing I hate*.

appear in the flesh—*who denied that Christ had come in the flesh* <note>original: "Qui Christum negarent in carne venisse"</note>. Another person, Menander, a disciple of Simon Magus, together with Saturninus, insisted that Christ was not gifted with any human substance; and though he might seem to suffer upon the cross, yet he never suffered, or at least only in appearance. In consequence of this, he denied any final resurrection—*that Christ was not in the substance of a body, and that he only seemed to suffer as a phantasm; that the resurrection of the flesh would in no way occur* <note>original: "Christum in substantia corporis non fuisse: et phantasmate tantum quasi passum fuisse. Resurrectionem carnis nullo modo futuram."</note>. These were followed by Basilides, who like them affirmed that the appearance of Christ was ideal, and that he was a mere **phantasm** <term>phantasm: a ghost-like appearance or illusion; in this context, the belief that Christ only appeared to be human</term>; consequently that he had no human frame, nor

nor did he ever suffer. According to him, Simon—the same man who carried the cross of Christ—was substituted in his place and suffered for him <note>original: "that Simon was crucified in his place" (vice ipsius Simonem crucifixum esse)</note>.

The Valentinians of the second century held similar views about our Savior. They maintained that Christ received nothing from the Virgin Mary; instead, his body was a heavenly substance which he brought with him from above.

This was likewise the opinion of the heretics called Marcionites: that Christ did not take on real flesh, but only a **phantom** <term>phantom: here used to mean a body that was an optical illusion rather than physical matter</term> <note>original: "phantastikēn"</note> body; and that he did not truly suffer, even though he appeared to suffer <note>original: "Christum non veram, sed φανταστικὴν solum carnem induisse; nec revera passum esse, cum pati visus fuerit"</note>. Because of this, they would not admit that he was born of the Virgin. In this, they agreed with the Gnostics. For the doctrine which they supported was that Christ

Cerdo, who lived during the same period, held a similar opinion: that ² Christ never appeared in the flesh, nor was he the son of the Virgin; and that he showed himself only as a mental image <note>original: "mere ideal appearance"</note>, without anything physical <note>original: "real"</note>.

### COROLLARY.

It may seem extraordinary that such an opinion should have taken hold so very early; yet it is most certain that it did. The reason for its prevalence may, I think, be easily discovered. It arose from a cause which—though contrary to the evidence of the Gospel and designed to injure the truth rather than to promote it—nevertheless indirectly and ultimately intended to honor the divine character of our Savior. For it arose from a high regard for his virtues and excellence, and a firm belief

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of why early heretical groups, overwhelmed by the magnitude of Christ's miracles, concluded that he could not possibly be human.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>

...of the miracles he performed, which were never disputed in those early days or for a long time afterward. These virtues and works were so amazing—and so far beyond what could be expected from a mere human—that these people would not admit there was anything human in either his character or his actions. Therefore, they accepted the truth of his works but attributed everything to Christ as God, without acknowledging his humanity. For they could not imagine that such sublime and heavenly qualities, or such supernatural powers, could belong to any son of Adam <note>An archaic phrase referring to any human being, as a descendant of the biblical first man.</note>. 

Thus they were mistaken, through a misunderstanding of Christ’s true nature. They acted more excusably, however, than many people during the time of our Savior and in later periods, who attributed his miracles either to magic or to the powers of darkness <note>A reference to those who claimed Jesus performed miracles by the power of demons or Beelzebub.</note>. We find that both groups acknowledged these wonderful works; they saw and were certain that no mortal could perform them without help. But they erred in opposite extremes: one by denying the intervention of the Deity <note>original: "Deity"; meaning God.</note>, and the other by denying the involvement of a man. Yet

<meta>catchword: we</meta>

<summary>This page explains that some early thinkers denied Christ's humanity because his miracles were so extraordinary they believed he must be entirely divine. The author contrasts these heretics with those who attributed Christ's powers to evil, noting that both acknowledged the miracles' supernatural origin but misunderstood his dual nature.</summary>

We find that each of these groups contributed indirectly toward the preservation of these Gospel truths. Even when these truths were misunderstood and misapplied, they were acknowledged in those days by the worst enemies of the Gospel. Christ’s supernatural powers were accepted, even though his humanity was sometimes denied; the fact that his divine power was acknowledged was a point of great importance.

own¹ nation and community. For this reason, he was sent as an ambassador to Rome for a special mission in the fourth year of the Emperor Caligula. This was in the year of Christ 42. Therefore, if we assume Philo was about forty years old at this time, he must have been a contemporary of our Savior throughout his entire life, and nearly the same age as many of his Apostles and Disciples. If he were older than I have stated, as some believe, he still would have been a contemporary of Christ throughout his life; for he survived him and, as there is reason to believe, lived many years after him. His thoughts upon this subject are very accurate and sublime—the kind that would do honor to a Christian. 

But although the Jews in his time expected the Messiah Prince and flattered themselves that he would arise from among their brothers and exalt their nation, Philo suppresses every thought to this purpose. This prejudice was the great obstacle to his becoming a Christian, even though he must have been convinced of the miracles of our Savior, as well as the holiness <note>original: "sanctity"</note> of his conduct, his goodness, and his wisdom. 

He must likewise have known many of the first converts <note>original: "proselytes"</note>, who were very numerous at Alexandria, and he was probably not unacquainted with some of the Apostles. But despite these advantages, he could not bring himself to believe that ***the Word could be made flesh***. A suffering Messiah and Christ crucified were beyond his comprehension. ## ON PHILO’S OPINION REGARDING THE LOGOS REPRESENTED AS THE HIGH PRIEST.

<margin>¹ See also Joshua 20:6.</margin>

Yet, even though the Scriptures had declared that this High Priest was the Messiah appointed to come into the world—and our Savior, through his teaching and miracles, had proved himself to be that Messiah—Philo shows by his silence that he could not accept that opinion. He could not accept a crucified Savior. It was a stumbling block in the way of truth that continually hindered him. ## ON THE OPINION OF PHILO CONCERNING THE LOGOS BEING REPRESENTED AS HIGH PRIEST.

### ON THE OPINION OF PHILO REGARDING THE LOGOS BEING REPRESENTED AS HIGH PRIEST.

Such was the opinion of Philo Judaeus <note>original: Philo Judeus; a Jewish philosopher from Alexandria who lived during the 1st century AD</note> regarding the Logos, or Word of God. He maintains the Logos's divine nature and his origin from God. Yet, although the Scriptures had declared that this High Priest was the Messiah appointed to come into the world, and our Savior by his doctrine and miracles had proved himself to be that Messiah, Philo shows by his silence that he could not accept <note>original: accede to</note> that opinion. He could not admit to a crucified Savior. It was a stumbling block in the way of truth that continually hindered him. 

Therefore, when he comes to his fourth question in *On Fugitives* <term>original Latin title: de Profugis</term> (p. 561), where it is stated from ¹ Numbers 35:25–28 that the guilty person who fled for his crimes to a city of refuge should remain there to

He had undoubtedly conversed with Christians at Alexandria¹ and also at Rome, when he visited that city on two different occasions. He was likely familiar with them in Jerusalem as well, for this seems to have been his place of residence in the early part of his life. He calls it the "Holy City" <note>original Greek: ἱερα πολις</note> and says, "This city, as I have said, is my native land; it is the metropolis not only of the country of Judea, but of most others." <note>original Greek: Αυτη, καθαπερ εφην, εμη μεν εϛι πατρις, Μητροπολις δε ου μιας χωρας Ιουδαιας, αλλα και των πλειϛων.</note>² *This city (Jerusalem) was the place of my birth: which city is the metropolis not of Judea only, but of many other regions.* <note>Philo was historically born in Alexandria; here he likely refers to Jerusalem as his "mother city" or ancestral home in a spiritual and cultural sense.</note> He seems to have visited this city at the times of the Passover in obedience to the law, and probably during other festivals.³ From his intercourse with—

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding Philo Judaeus. It suggests that Philo's sophisticated understanding of the "Word of God" (Logos) likely came from his contact with early Christians, yet he remained silent about Jesus himself.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

Through his interactions with the Christians, he obtained this improved knowledge concerning the Word of God <note>The "Word of God" refers to the *Logos*, a central concept in both Philo's Jewish philosophy and early Christian theology (as seen in the Gospel of John).</note>, whom he calls the Son of God, his first-born. He described the Word's divine nature far more accurately than any of the Platonists before him, any of the Alexandrian school after him, or even any of his own Jewish nation in ancient times. 

However, the idea of a crucified Christ seems to have been so contrary to his preconceived notions that he never mentions him or even alludes to him. This is despite the fact that Philo lived during Christ's lifetime and must have been well-acquainted with the history of his holy life, his teachings, and all his miraculous works. 

There is something extraordinary in his silence which is worth noting. For since he had so often taken great pains to define what the Word of God was, we should naturally expect that he would have also shown what it was not. And as our Savior declared to all that he was the Son of God, the first-born of the world, who came down from heaven to give his life as a ransom for many, and was identified by Saint Paul as the High Priest <note>In the Book of Hebrews, Saint Paul (or the author of the text) identifies Jesus as the great High Priest, a title Philo also uses for the Logos.</note> mentioned by

<summary>The author notes the contradiction in Philo’s writings: while he describes the "Word of God" in terms nearly identical to Christian doctrine, he completely ignores the historical person of Jesus, likely because he could not accept the concept of a suffering and crucified Messiah.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Logos, Word of God, Son of God, Christ crucified, Alexandrian school, Saint Paul, High Priest, theology</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Jewish philosopher Philo Judaeus and his relationship to early Christianity. It argues that while Philo never explicitly mentions Jesus, he was likely aware of him and influenced by Christian ideas during his time in Alexandria.</meta>

<page-num>( 27 )</page-num>

the Prophets; it is extraordinary that he does not try to counter <note>original: "obviate this notion"</note> this idea. The reputation and the claims of Christ, the Messiah, were well known among the Jews in the time of Philo. They could be no secret at Alexandria, which was the residence of several thousand of his fellow countrymen and which was so near to Judea—especially as Saint Mark preached the Gospel there very early, generally supposed to be in 49 AD <note>original: "A. C. 49," meaning *Anno Christi* (Year of Christ).</note>. 

Yet he never attempts to set aside these claims, nor does he ever speak of Christ, or of Jesus the Messiah. His prejudices would not allow him to acknowledge Christ in the flesh, and at the same time, he must have been affected by his holy life and miracles, for *these things were not done in secret* <note>original: "these things were not done in a corner." This is a reference to Acts 26:26, meaning the events were public and well-known.</note>. He had certainly examined this matter in his own mind and was brought so far toward the truth that he confessed it was easier to conceive of a Deity partaking of human nature than a man partaking of divinity—¹ *For God would sooner [pass] into a man, than [a man] into...* <note>original Greek: Θαττον γαρ αν εις ανθρωπον Θεον, η εις... (Thātton gar an eis anthrōpon Theon, ē eis...). From Philo's treatise *On the Virtues*.</note>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Jewish philosopher Philo Judaeus and his internal conflict regarding the divinity of Christ. The previous page ended with a partial quote from Philo suggesting that it is easier for God to become man than for a man to become God.</meta>

<page-num>( 28 )</page-num>

...than a man could change into God <note>original Greek: η εις Θεον ανθρωπον μεταβαλειν (*ē eis Theon anthrōpon metaballein*)</note>. He was, as he confesses, facing great doubts and difficulties; and, as he could not agree <note>original: "accede"</note>, he kept a respectful <note>original: "awful," meaning full of awe or reverence</note> distance, maintaining a religious silence. What he could not bring himself to admit due to human frailty, he was neither able nor willing to deny. He seems to have occupied a fearful middle ground <note>original: "medium"</note>, which was also the case for Josephus and many of the Jews at that time.

From the extracts produced above, we may learn the opinion of Philo and others of his nation concerning the divinity of the second Person of the Trinity—the *Logos*, or Word of God. In his writings, we find this doctrine more developed <note>original: "improved"</note> and more precisely defined than it had ever been presented before the coming of Christ.

Although I have introduced this learned writer alongside Basilides, the Gnostics, and others of the first and second centuries, he differs from them significantly in one respect. They agree with him in not allowing that

### CONCERNING THE AGE OF PHILO

Since I have suggested that such excellent knowledge could not have been obtained by this early Jew except through access to some of the early Christians, it will be proper to show that the era <note>original: "æra"</note> of his life corresponds with the first proclamation <note>original: "promulgation"</note> of the Gospel. This is denied by the learned editor of his works,¹ who maintains that he was born many years before Christ and could not have had any correspondence with Saint Mark and the first Christians of Alexandria. I shall now examine the reasons he gives for this.

***

¹ Dr. Thomas Mangey <note>an 18th-century English clergyman and scholar famous for his 1742 edition of Philo's works, which remained the standard for over a century</note>.

<summary>The author argues that Philo must have had contact with the apostles to develop his doctrines and begins to address chronological objections raised by the scholar Thomas Mangey regarding Philo's lifespan.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Dr. Thomas Mangey's chronological argument. The author disputes the claim that Philo was already an old man during his embassy to Caligula in 40 AD, arguing instead that Philo was describing his age at the time he wrote the account, many years later.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

He tells us that many have attempted to determine the time of Philo's birth; however, we have no definite information regarding this. He says that some, who seem to come closest to the truth, estimate it to have been about thirty years before the birth of our Savior <note>Jesus Christ</note>. This was the opinion of Basnage <note>Jacques Basnage (1653–1723), a noted French Protestant theologian and historian</note>, and the editor of Philo agrees with him, giving the following reason for his conclusion:

¹ "For he himself, in the fourth year of Caligula <note>reigned 37–41 AD; the Latin text uses 'Caii' for Gaius Caligula</note>, the year 793 from the founding of the city <note>793 A.U.C. corresponds to 40 AD</note>, testifies more than once that he was an old man and advanced in age." <note>original Latin: "Ille enim ipse anno Caii quarto, urbis conditæ 793, se senem et ætate provectiorem plus una vice testatur."</note>

This is a major mistake, and I wonder how the editor could possibly have fallen into it. At the beginning of his ² treatise, where he provides an account of his embassy, Philo undoubtedly speaks of himself as being old. But by this, he meant he was old at the time of his *writing*, not at the time of the embassy to Caligula, which probably took place twenty years or more earlier <note>original: "antecedent"</note>. This is obvious to anyone who examines the treatise, and I won—

¹ *Preface*, p. ii. <note>original: Præfatio</note>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 31, finishing the word "wonder" and further explaining why Philo’s account of his embassy to Caligula was written long after the event occurred.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

der, how it could be mistaken. We find in the account provided a most severe verbal attack <note>original: "invective"</note> against the emperor; one that no Jew, nor any person of any country, would have dared to utter [while he was alive]. The Jewish nation had lived in great dread of Caligula, who had threatened to install his own statue within the Temple at Jerusalem. Following their repeated protests, he became hostile toward the entire nation and showed a particular dislike toward the Jews of Alexandria, a city he intended to visit. To turn away his anger, it was decided to send an embassy to Rome. Philo and several others were employed for this purpose, and he met with the emperor at Rome and at Puteoli <note>a major port city near modern-day Naples</note>; however, he met with no success. On the contrary, he was ¹ insulted, put in chains, and barely escaped with his life. Consequently, several years later, he wrote the treatise *On the Embassy* <note>original Latin: *De Legatione*</note>:

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of Philo's writings, specifically questioning the date and circumstances under which he wrote his critique of the Emperor Caligula.</meta>

in which he accuses Caligula of ¹ folly, madness, cruelty, and every vice. His editor supposes that he produced this work in the very year of his embassy—at the very crisis when the Jewish nation was under such apprehension. However, this could not have been the case. No person would have published such a satire during the reign of Caligula, nor for some time afterward. Therefore, nothing concerning Philo's age can be gathered from these facts <note>original: "data"</note>.

Eusebius, however, mentions a tradition that Philo read this treatise before the Emperor Claudius and the entire Senate, and that it was received with great applause.² This is undoubtedly a Jewish fabrication <note>original: "figment"</note>. On such an occasion, the author would have included some compliments to the reigning prince, as well as to the assembly before whom he stood.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that Philo's writings against Caligula were written much later than traditionally assumed, noting that Philo speaks harshly of the Romans and mentions events that occurred after the deaths of Caligula and Claudius.</meta>

But not a single word to this effect appears, nor are they <note>meaning the Roman Senate</note> ever mentioned. Furthermore, he speaks of the Romans in general with great bitterness and accuses them of cruelty and a lack of generosity <note>original: "illiberality"</note> toward him and his people. How can we imagine that the Senate would have permitted such a harsh attack <note>original: "invective"</note>, or that they would have even listened to either satire or praise <note>original: "encomium"</note> from a Jew? 

But what settles the matter beyond any doubt is that Philo himself shows the work could not have been written during the reign of Claudius, let alone Caligula. Throughout his work, he repeatedly implies that the former emperor, Claudius, had been dead for some time. One of the primary enemies of the Jews was an Egyptian named Helicon, who used every possible trick to make them hateful to the emperor. $^1$ *But this Helicon*, says Philo, *was eventually removed—being executed by Claudius Caesar for some other*

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that Philo’s writings were composed long after the events they describe. It specifically focuses on internal evidence from Philo's "On the Embassy" (De Legatione) and "Against Flaccus" to prove they were written after the deaths of the emperors Caligula and Claudius.</meta>

*other base actions, of which he had been guilty.*

*“But these things happened afterward,”* Philo says, *“that is, after my embassy to Rome.”* This statement appears in the very treatise *On the Embassy* <note>original: "de Legatione"</note>. He plainly suggests here that the reign of Claudius was already over when he wrote this document; consequently, Caligula must have been dead for several years. Similar evidence is found in his oration *Against Flaccus*$^1$. But why should I even mention that oration? In the very treatise mentioned above$^2$—the one we are currently discussing—it is apparent on almost every page that Caligula was already dead. The entire character description given is of a person who has passed away. 

We must not, therefore, take it for granted that an event and the historical record of that event are necessarily from the same date. One may have occurred many years after the other. Therefore, the treatise could not have been written until the reign of Nero—and probably even later, when the Caesarean <note>The Julio-Claudian dynasty, the first five Roman emperors related to Julius Caesar.</note> family was extinct, and no offense could be

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against dating Philo's life based on vague chronological phrases. It argues that "not long ago" is a relative term depending on the context of the subject matter, using the discovery of America as a comparison.</meta>

<page-num>( 37 )</page-num>

ago, and "*not long since*," signifies a very unlimited space of time. This can only be determined by the author, for it depends entirely upon his mode of judging and the boundary points <note>original: "termini"</note> to which he silently alludes. If a person were writing about migrations and discoveries, he might say that it is not long since America was discovered; yet it has been known for over three centuries. Nothing, therefore, can be precisely concluded from the words above, nor can the year of a man’s life be determined by such evidence. The very words—*as they say* <note>original Greek: *Ὥσπερ φασιν*</note>—seem to suggest much uncertainty and imply that the event was by no means recent. In short, we can prove this from the author’s own account of himself and his writings, in the face of which all such guesses must vanish.

When, therefore, the birth of Philo is moved back to the time of Julius Caesar, it is an unjustifiable leap backward in time <note>original: "unwarrantable anticipation"</note>. There is reason to think that he was nearly the same age as the Apostles, with some of whom he is said

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Philo of Alexandria was a contemporary of the early Christian leaders, placing his life late enough to have potentially interacted with the Apostles and their writings.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

to have conversed. As we have seen, he was alive as late as the reign of Nero. Since he lived in Alexandria, he certainly would have known Saint Mark, who was the first bishop in that city,¹ and arrived there (as the Editor acknowledges²) around the year 48 or 49 AD. 

And, if Saint Mark’s Gospel was published in that year—or even earlier, as many believe—then Philo would have had the opportunity to read it as well. Moreover, since he was living during the reign of the Emperor Nero, it is likely that he spoke with some of Christ's disciples as well as the Apostles, and was likely familiar with the writings of the other Evangelists. His location and the timing of his life support this theory <note>original: "conjecture"</note>, and, above all, the truths which he revealed in his writings confirm it.

The Editor is of a different opinion, which he expresses in the following manner:

Although this date (64 AD) is accepted regarding the publication <note>original: "promulgation"</note> of the Gospel, everything I have argued may still be true. For at this period (64 AD), Philo—far from being nearly a hundred years old—was probably no older than many of the Apostles. If we suppose him to have been forty or forty-two years old when

...could not have been a stranger to Christ and Christianity. Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD) was a historian of the early Christian Church.</note>, therefore, very rightly observes that Philo must have had in mind some of the first preachers of the Gospel and the doctrines transmitted by the Apostles themselves when he wrote these things. But this is not precise enough: for these truths were not merely transmitted to him. He lived during the time of the Evangelists and Apostles, and he obtained his knowledge from them directly, as if from the fountainhead.

We may judge that he held a favorable opinion of the Gospel from his silence: for although he was a Jew and—as one might consequently suppose—not a friend to Christianity, he never speaks against it, even when many opportunities arise. Furthermore, we have seen that he borrows many essential truths that could not have been obtained from any non-Christian <note>original: "unconverted"</note> members of his own nation. At the same time, it should be noted that although he lived among Christians and was acquainted with their doctrines, he never mentions them by name; nor does he ever take notice of Saint Mark, who presided over the church at Alexandria during Philo's lifetime.

<meta>catchword: Yet</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Philo's silence regarding Christianity, combined with his use of Christian-like doctrines, suggests he was a contemporary who respected the faith even if he did not explicitly join it.</summary>

Yet Philo was so indebted to them <note>The author refers here to the early Christians and Apostles mentioned on the previous page.</note> that we can find the opinions of the Apostles and the doctrines of Christ himself regarding this essential article of our faith within his writings. It is clear that he had many opportunities to gain such information. For even if he was born before Christ—as the Editor believes—it is very evident from his own testimony that he outlived him. In the treatise we are currently examining, he mentions the death of the Emperor Claudius <note>Claudius died in 54 AD, roughly twenty years after the death of Jesus.</note>, as I have already shown.

He was therefore alive during the entire time our Savior resided on earth, and he survived him by several years. This shows how much opportunity there was for him to gather information, which he clearly used to his advantage. He was a Jew and a follower of Plato. ### THE ACCOUNT OF THE LOGOS, OR WORD OF GOD, IN SAINT JOHN

We have seen that the heretics mentioned above would not admit that Christ came to earth in a human state; and Philo, through his specific silence, seems to think that He did not appear at all. I have shown that this denial of our Savior in the flesh was of very dangerous consequence. It rendered the sufferings of Christ—His death and passion, along with all the blessings that were to follow—useless <note>original: "abortive"</note> and ineffective.

### SAINT JOHN'S GOSPEL, CHAPTER 1

> 1. In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God.

---

^1 Our version <note>The King James Version</note> translates the word *autou* <note>original Greek: "αυτȣ," a pronoun meaning "him" or "his"</note> as *him*, and this is the proper meaning. But I have used the word *it* because several people have translated the original in this manner in order to get rid of a difficulty that embarrassed them. They wanted to prove that by the *Word* was meant *no Person*. But the text is so plain that even if you translate the word either way, a *Person* must still be signified; the truth cannot be evaded by this tactic <note>original: "artifice"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 51, completing the sentence that no human can understand divine mysteries through their own efforts alone. It transitions into a discussion on the dual nature of Christ—his visible humanity and his hidden divinity.</meta>

...mysteries no man can arrive at by himself.

If Christ had been merely a man upon earth, there could have been no such mystery; consequently, there would have been no difficulty in obtaining an immediate knowledge of him. Accordingly, as a man, he was known to everyone around him. But in addition to this, he had in his nature something heavenly and superior—his divinity, which is known only to the Father. Therefore, it is not to be discovered by man, who can only know it through divine revelation.

Regarding the divinity of our Savior, there is one passage in Saint Paul so plain and decisive <note>original: "determinate"</note> that I should think every reasonable person must necessarily agree with it. The Apostle is mentioning his zeal and best wishes for some of the converts <note>original: "proselytes"</note> to the Gospel, and adds—"That their hearts might be encouraged, being knit together in love, and attaining to all the riches of the full assurance of understanding, to the knowledge of the mystery of God, both of the Father and of Christ." <note>original Greek: Ἱνα παρακληθωσιν αἱ καρδιαι αυτων, συμβιϐασθεντων εν αγαπη, και εις παντα πλȣτον της πληροφοριας της συνεσεως, εις επιγνωσιν τȣ μυστηριȣ τȣ Θεȣ, και Πατρος, και τȣ Χριϛȣ.</note>¹ *That their hearts might be comforted, being knit together in love, and unto all riches of...*

...the full assurance of understanding, to the knowledge <note>original: "acknowledgement"</note> of the mystery of God, and of the Father, and of Christ. This latter part is not properly translated here, nor in the Roman version <note>original: "Roman version," referring to the Latin Vulgate</note>. Consequently, the intended meaning <note>original: "purport"</note> of the Apostle’s information is largely lost. The Greek words—<note>original Greek: "την επιγνωσιν τȣ μυστηριȣ τȣ Θεȣ, και Πατρος και τȣ Χριστȣ"</note>—should be translated as—*to the knowledge of the mystery of God,* BOTH OF THE FATHER AND OF THE <gloss>Christ;</gloss> SON; or more fully—BOTH OF GOD THE FATHER, AND OF GOD THE <gloss>Christ, or Messiah.</gloss> SON. 

This is the true meaning of these significant terms, if there is any certainty in language; and I believe that, upon careful consideration, it cannot be disputed <note>original: "controverted"</note>. The divinity of our Savior is clearly established <note>original: "ascertained"</note> here, and his connection with God is quite rightly called a mystery, for it was a truth that could not be discovered by man. *Flesh and blood could not have revealed it.* In this passage, Christ is not only mentioned alongside God, but *as* God—*that Christ, who is over all, God blessed forever.*¹ <note>original Greek: "Θεος ευλογητος εις τȣς αιωνας"</note>.

## OF THE THIRD PERSON.

<margin>?</margin>If the divinity of our Savior <note>original: "Saviour"</note> is satisfactorily proved, and we are assured of the second Person, the Son of God, then the third follows as a matter of course and must be admitted. When our Savior gave his last command to his disciples and ordered them to teach all nations, baptizing them in the *name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note>,*† we cannot suppose that on such a solemn occasion, after mentioning two Persons, he would then mention a third alongside them—in exactly the same manner—who was a mere mode <term>mode: a philosophical term for a manifestation or quality of a being, rather than a distinct individual person</term> or attribute, and specifically an attribute of one of those other persons. The sacred writers would never wish to perplex the world, much less to mislead those to whom they addressed themselves. Therefore, if the Holy Spirit did not exist as a Person, they would not have described him as a cooperating agent, nor as an agent at all. Yet they speak of the Holy Spirit as the *Paraclete* <term>Paraclete: derived from the Greek "paraklētos," meaning an advocate, helper, or counselor</term>, or

† Matthew 28:19.

<meta>catchword: Com-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the Holy Spirit must be a distinct Person of the Trinity because Jesus ranks the Spirit equally with the Father and the Son in the baptismal command, rather than describing the Spirit as a mere attribute.</summary>

## OF THE THIRD PERSON

If the divinity of our Savior is satisfactorily proved, and we are assured of the second Person, the Son of God, the third follows naturally and must be accepted. When our Savior gave his last command to his disciples and ordered them to teach all nations, baptizing them in the *name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit*, <gloss>† Matthew 28:19.</gloss> we cannot suppose that, on such a solemn occasion, after mentioning two Persons, he would mention a third alongside them—and in exactly the same manner—that was a mere mode or attribute <note>original: "mode, or attribute"; in theology, this refers to the idea that the Holy Spirit is just a "way" God acts or a quality he possesses, rather than a distinct Person</note>, and specifically an attribute of one of those persons. <margin>2.</margin> The sacred writers would never wish to confuse the world, much less to mislead those to whom they addressed themselves. Therefore, if the *Holy Spirit* did not exist as a Person, they would not have described him as a cooperating agent, or an agent at all. Yet they speak of the Holy Spirit as the *Paraclete* <term>original: "Paraclete"; a term from the Greek *paraklētos*, meaning an advocate, counselor, or helper</term>, or

->† Matthew 28:19.<-

<meta>catchword: Com-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the Holy Spirit must be a distinct divine Person because he is listed alongside the Father and Son in the baptismal command, rather than being described as a mere attribute of God.</summary>

We must labor in the field to enjoy the harvest. A pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> poet has delivered this great truth in a very expressive manner:

->—— The Father himself willed that the way of cultivation <-
> should not be easy; he first stirred the fields by skill, sharpening mortal hearts with cares, nor did he allow his realms to grow sluggish in heavy lethargy. <note>original Latin: "Pater ipse colendi / Haud facilem esse viam voluit: primusque per artem / Movit agros, curis acuens mortalia corda: / Nec torpere gravi passus sua regna veterno." A similar labor of the mind, with a similar exercise of our faculties, is required <note>original: "requisite"</note> in order to obtain knowledge, both human and divine. And this is the very purpose of that Being who confers the blessing. We must seek to find, and knock to have it opened. From discoveries made in this way, we learn how many hidden <note>original: "latent"</note> truths are to be found in the Scriptures. And when these are observed upon examination, they afford more inward satisfaction and are more conducive to faith than if they were superficial and self-evident. They likewise increase our regard for the Scriptures. For the more we discover of hidden <note>original: "latent"</note> design and wisdom in an object, the greater will be our veneration and the stronger our faith.

<meta>This page transitions from the author's philosophical arguments about discovering divine truth to a historical critique of shifting views on Christ's nature. From the previous discussion <note>original: "foregoing disquisition"</note>, we can sadly observe how stubborn and changeable <note>original: "perverse and fickle"</note> human minds are, and what contradictions and inconsistencies appear in their various opinions. Many of the primary innovators <note>original: "principal innovators"; referring to early religious dissenters or heretics</note> in the first era of the church refused to believe that Christ took on human form <note>original: "came in the flesh"</note> and denied his humanity. In the present age, it has become popular <note>original: "a fashion"</note> to deny his divinity. We find that many insist he was merely a man, subject to all the weaknesses of the descendants of Adam. Because of this, his roles as the Son of God, and as God himself—as the Angel of the Covenant, the Intercessor, Mediator, and Redeemer—are completely rejected, and his miraculous birth is considered a myth <note>original: "fable"</note>.

To the evidence of the Apostles, we may add the authority of the Church Fathers who followed them. Appealing to them after such superior evidence, however, may seem...

unnecessary. But as it has been thought by some that the writers in the first eras <note>original: "æras"</note> of Christianity have nothing to say regarding this matter, I will proceed somewhat further and show that they afford much information <note>original: "intelligence"</note> upon these subjects, and information of the greatest importance <note>original: "consequence"</note>.

In response to this, it has been argued that if any doctrine is not to be found in the apostolic writings, no authority of the Church Fathers can give it official approval <note>original: "sanction"</note>. This is very true. But if a person, through human weakness <note>original: "frailty"</note> and misconception, should imagine that any article of faith was of doubtful meaning <note>original: "purport"</note> or shrouded in obscurity, then the evidence of those who had conversed with the Apostles and their immediate disciples must carry weight. 

Furthermore, those of the second century, though they came later, are still early enough to have their opinions admitted—especially if they are in total agreement <note>original: "unanimous"</note> and wrote before any different ideas <note>original: "notion"</note> had taken hold. <page-num>61</page-num>

These writers were found to have lived at such a great distance from one another that, if any error had crept into the church in one region so early, it could not have spread so quickly to another, much less to all of them. The church in Alexandria had little communication with the church in Carthage and was separated even further from the church in Lyons. Similarly, the converts <note>original: "proselytes"</note> in Lyons had very little contact <note>original: "correspondence"</note> with those in Edessa, Antioch, and Samaria. 

Therefore, the total agreement <note>original: "unanimity"</note> of these writers—who were otherwise so disconnected—demonstrates the truth of the doctrine. If any further proof is needed, they certainly provide it. The evidence of the Church Fathers will confirm <note>original: "ascertain"</note> this truth. While this evidence has been quoted to varying degrees by various authors using different arrangements, I will nevertheless venture to present it again, and in great detail <note>original: "at large"</note>. I will also add what seems to have been previously omitted and present the whole matter in the clearest and most accurate light to the very best of my ability.

### JUSTIN MARTYR.

He says that the Christians were considered <note>original: "reputed"</note> atheists, and he admits <note>original: "confesses"</note> that they were indeed so in regard to the gods of the pagans <note>original: "Gentiles"</note>—

<margin>¹ First Apology <note>original: "Apologia prima"</note>, p. 47. B.</margin>

But we are not atheists in respect to the most true God, the Father of all righteousness, self-control <note>original: "σωφροσυνης"</note>, and every other virtue, who is entirely free from evil. We reverence and worship both Him and His Son, who came from Him and taught us these things, along with the army <note>original: "στρατον"</note> of the other good angels who follow Him and are made like Him. We also worship the prophetic Spirit, honoring them in reason and in truth, and freely sharing these teachings with anyone who wishes to learn, just as we ourselves were taught. <note>original: "αλλ’ ουχι του αληθεστατου, και Πατρος δικαιοσυνης και σωφροσυνης και των αλλων αρετων, ανεπιμικτου τε κακιας, Θεου. Αλλ’ εκεινον τε, και τον παρ’ αυτου υιον ελθοντα και διδαξαντα ημας ταυτα, και τον των αλλων επομενων, και εξομοιουμενων, αγαθων Αγγελων στρατον. Πνευμα τε το προφητικον σεβομεθα, και προσκυνουμεν, λογω και αληθεια τιμωντες, και παντι βουλομενω μαθειν, ως εδιδαχθημεν, αφθονως παραδιδοντες."</note>

## ATHENAGORAS.

Athenagoras <note>Athenagoras of Athens (c. 133–190 AD) was a Christian apologist who wrote to the Roman Emperors to defend Christianity.</note> complains of the same unjust accusation <note>the accusation of atheism, commonly leveled against Christians because they refused to worship the Roman gods</note>. He says: 

*"How must anyone be astonished when he hears us accused of atheism—we who acknowledge God the Father and God the Son, together with the Holy Spirit; and who maintain their power as united, while also recognizing their distinction in terms of personality and order."* 

I am providing what I believe is the true meaning, which sometimes can only be expressed through a paraphrase <term>periphrasis: a roundabout way of speaking or a paraphrase</term>. The original Greek is as follows: 

"Who then would not be at a loss to hear people called atheists who speak of God the Father, and of God the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, and who demonstrate both their power in unity and their distinction in order?" <note>original: "Τις ουν ουκ αν απορησαι, λε- γοντας Θεον Πατερα, και υἱον Θεον, και πνευμα ἁγιον, δεικνυντας αυτων και την εν τη ένωσει δυναμιν, και την εν τη ταξει διαιρεσιν, ακουσας Αθεους καλε- μενος;"</note> ¹ 

He had previously said: 

"The Son of God is the Word <term>Logos: The Greek word 'Logos' implies both 'word' and 'reason' or 'divine logic'</term> of the Father, in both form and power. For by him and through him all things were made, the Father and the Son being one." <note>original: "εσιν ὁ υἱος του Θεου Λογος του Πατρος εν ιδεᾳ και ενεργειᾳ. Προς αυτου γαρ και δι' αυτου παντα εγενετο, ἑνος οντος του Πατρος και του υἱου."</note> ² 

*The Son of God*

*...is the Word of the Father, identical in both conception <note>original: "comprehension"; here meaning essence or what is contained within the divine nature</note> and operation. For through him and by him all things were created, the Son and the Father being one.* He then mentions that it is the duty of humanity to consider this mystery: 

"What is the unity of the Son <note>original: "pais," meaning child or servant, a term used here for the Son of God</note> with the Father; what is the communion of the Father with the Son; what is the Spirit; and what is the union of such great beings and the distinction between those united: the Spirit, the Son, and the Father?" <note>original: "Τις ἡ τε παιδος προς τον Πατερα ἑνοτης, τις ἡ τε Πατρος προς τον υἱον κοινωνια, τι το Πνευμα, τις ἡ των τοσουτων ἑνωσις, και διαιρεσις ἑνομενων, τε Πνευματος, τε Παιδος, τε Πατρος"</note> 

*He asks what that unity is between the Son and the Father, and what is the communion of the Father with the Son. He also asks us to search for what the Spirit is, and to consider the union of these wonderful Beings and their distinction when united: the Holy Spirit, the Son, and the Father.*

->THEOPHILUS.<- <note>This heading, appearing as a catchword, signals that the text will transition to the writings of Theophilus of Antioch on the following page.</note>

<page-num>( 66 )</page-num>

# THEOPHILUS OF ANTIOCH.

He mentions that the Word <note>The "Word" or *Logos* refers to the second person of the Trinity, Jesus Christ.</note> proceeded from God before the worlds <note>original: "worlds"; referring to the ages or the universe</note> began, and that all things were created by him. "God used this Word as a helper for the things created by Him, and through Him He made all things."¹ <note>original: "Τουτον τον Λογον εσχεν ὑπεργον των ὑπ’ αυτου γεγενημενων, και δι’ αυτου τα παντα πεποιηκεν."</note> This learned Father was, like many others, too much influenced <note>original: "tinctured"</note> by Platonic philosophy and by a degree of mysticism that began to prevail in the church very early; yet he speaks effectively to the point. "And the three days [of creation]... are types of the Trinity: of God, His Word, and His Wisdom."² <note>original: "Και αἱ Τρεις ἡμεραι . . . . τυποι εισιν της Τριαδος, του Θεου, και του Λογου αυτου, και της Σοφιας αυτου." Theophilus is the first known author to use the Greek word *Trias* (Trinity) to describe the Godhead.</note> Though he is in some places far too imaginative <note>original: "fanciful"</note> in his illustrations, he nonetheless provides us with the perspective of the church in his time regarding this great article of religion. He wrote around the middle of the second century.

# TATIAN <note>original: "Tatianus"</note>

This learned Father lived slightly before Athenagoras and was a disciple of Justin Martyr; it was during Justin's time that he became a convert to Christianity. He speaks of the Word <note>The *Logos*, referring to Jesus Christ as the second person of the Trinity.</note> as "the first-born work of the Father" <note>original Greek: "εργον πρωτοτοκον τ8 Πατρος"; translated in the text as "the first instance of the productive power of God."</note>. This was brought about by a partitioning of the divine nature, but without any separation <note>original Greek: "Γεγονε δε κατα μερισμον, 8 κατ’ αποκοπην"; meaning the Word comes from the Father like one torch lighting another without diminishing the first.</note>.¹ "We know that He was the head and origin of all things <note>original Greek: "Τ8τον ισμεν τ8 Κοσμ8 την αρχην"</note>." "The Word, before the formation of man, was the creator of the angels in heaven <note>original Greek: "Ὁ μεν 8ν Λογος προ της των ανδρων κατασκευης Αγγελων δημι8ργος γινεται"</note>." "The Word, which was made in the image of God <note>original Greek: "Ὁ Λογος, κατ’ εικονα Θε8 γεγονως"</note>..."²

-> # IRENÆUS <-

He is said to have been born in the reign of Trajan <note>Roman Emperor, reigned 98–117 AD.</note>, towards the beginning of the second century. Some think that he was not quite so early. It is certain that he had been a disciple of Pothinus, and also of Polycarp, who had been a disciple of Saint John <note>The Apostle John.</note>. He succeeded the former <note>Pothinus, the first bishop of Lyons</note> as bishop of Lyons, where he afterwards suffered martyrdom.

He mentions the unity of the Holy Spirit with Christ, and at the same time their unity with the Father — "their unity with one another at once, and their unity with the Father" <note>original Greek: "την προς αλληλους αμα, και την προς τον Πατερα ενωσιν"</note>.¹ He speaks of Christ as the Son of God — "Our Lord alone is truly the Teacher, and truly the good Son of God... the Word of God the Father" <note>original Latin: "Solus vere Magister Dominus noster, et bonus vere Filius Dei . . . Verbum Dei Patris"</note>;² and says that he always was with the Father — "The Son of the Father... who is with the Father from the beginning" <note>original Latin: "Filius Patris . . . qui ab initio est cum Patre"</note>.³

—always one and the same God <note>original Latin: "semper eundem Deum"</note>¹—God, and Lord, and Eternal King, and only-begotten <note>original Latin: "Deus, et Dominus, et Rex æternus, et unigenitus"</note>.² Very numerous are the proofs for this purpose which may be obtained from this respectable writer. ### CLEMENT OF ROME AND CLEMENT OF
### ALEXANDRIA

<margin>* First Epistle to the Corinthians, Section 46, page 174.</margin>

Clement of Rome <note>original: "Clemens Romanus"</note> lived during the first century and was a contemporary of Saint Peter and other original disciples. He has passed down a brief but very comprehensive account of his faith—undoubtedly the same faith he received from his great teachers. "Do we not have one God, one Christ, and one Spirit, whose divine influence is poured out upon us?" <note>original Greek: "Οὐχ᾽ ἕνα Θεον εχομεν, και ἕνα Χριςον, και ἕν πνευμα . . . το εκχυθεν εφ᾽ ἡμας;"</note>

Clement of Alexandria followed the figures mentioned above in time and preserved the same doctrines. He speaks of Christ as the Word <note>original: "Logos," a term referring to Christ as the divine reason or mind of God</note> and says: "For the image of God is His Word; and the Divine Word <note>The original text translates the Greek "Θειος Λογος" as "Holy Word," though "Divine Word" is more literal.</note> is the genuine offspring of the—" <note>original Greek: "Ἡ μεν γαρ τοῦ Θεοῦ εικων ὁ Λογος αὐτοῦ, και υἱος τοῦ Νοῦ γνησιος ὁ Θειος Λογος"</note>

# TERTULLIAN:

## AND THE OPINIONS OF THE ANCIENT PHILOSOPHERS.

Tertullian lived later than any of the writers already mentioned and is believed to have been converted to Christianity around the year 196 AD. He supports their testimony and shows that the same doctrine which prevailed in the East and West, and at Rome, was also maintained at Carthage <note>A major city in modern-day Tunisia and a center of early Christian thought</note>, where he was born. This is demonstrated in the account he gives of this mystery: "What has proceeded from God is God, and the Son of God, and both are one; so too, from Spirit comes Spirit, and from God, God." <note>original Latin: "Quod de Deo profectum est, Deus est, et Dei Filius, et Unus ambo: ita et de Spiritu Spiritus, et de Deo Deus."</note>³

"—He (Christ), having received the gift from the Father, poured out the Holy Spirit—the third name of the Divinity, and the third degree of Majesty... and the leader into all truth, which is in the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit, according to the Christian mystery." <note>original Latin: "— Hic (Christus) acceptum a Patre munus effudit Spiritum Sanctum, tertium nomen Divinitatis, et tertium gradum Majestatis . . . et deductorem omnis veritatis, quae est in Patre, et Filio, et Spiritu Sancto secundum Christianum sacramentum."</note>¹

These truths, he says, were to some degree known to the Greek <note>original: "Grecian"</note> philosophers. "Among your own Wise Men also, it is clear that the *Logos* <note>The Greek term *Logos* (Λογον) can mean "Word," "Reason," or "Discourse"; in Christian theology, it refers to the second person of the Trinity</note>—that is, the Word and the Reason—is seen as the architect of the universe." <note>original Latin/Greek: "Apud vestros quoque Sapientes, Λογον, id est, Sermonem, atque Rationem, constat artificem videri universitatis."</note>² They certainly were not totally ignorant of this truth. But they refined it and introduced *Matter* as part of the Trinity <note>original: "Trias"</note> and as something eternal. From the account given by Diogenes Laertius <note>A 3rd-century biographer of the Greek philosophers</note> regarding Plato, one would imagine that he allowed only two first principles.³ "He declared the two principles of all things to be God and Matter, which he also calls Mind and the Cause." <note>original Greek: "Δυο δε των παντων απεφηνεν αρχας, Θεον και Ὑλην, ὁν και Νυν προσαγορευει, και Αιτιον."</note> *Plato declared that the two principles of all things were God and Matter, which he styles the Mind and the efficient Cause.* But others give a better account of

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the philosophical "triad" of Plato and the belief among early Christian scholars that Greek philosophy was secretly derived from ancient Hebrew scriptures.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>

Justin Martyr says that this philosopher’s *Triad* <note>original: "Trias" — a set of three things or persons; here, it refers to the Platonic trinity</note> consists of — $^1$ God, Matter, and Form — *God, Matter, and Image or Resemblance*, which is the same as the *Idea*. In most of these examples, as I mentioned before, *Matter* is presented as a *first principle* <term>first principle</term> <note>A fundamental, self-evident truth or substance from which other things are derived</note> and as being *eternal*.

The doctrines we have been considering were not Plato's own discoveries; rather, they were ORIGINAL TRUTHS, $^2$ which he modified and misapplied in his writings. He himself suggests, in fact, that they were not his own — "foreigners have spoken them" <note>original Greek: ὁι βαρβαροι ειρηκασι. The term "barbarians" was used by the Greeks to refer to anyone who did not speak Greek, specifically the Hebrews in this context</note>. Let us then investigate the source from which he obtained them. Now, there was no nation that he had access to, and from which he could have acquired these ideas, except for the Jews. It is

---

$^1$ "For Plato once said that there are three principles of the universe: God, Matter, and Form." <note>original Greek: Ὁ γȣν Πλατων ποτε μεν τρεις αρχας τȣ παντος ειναι λεγει, Θεον, και Ὑλην, και Ειδος</note> *Exhortation to the Greeks <note>original Latin: "Cohort."</note>, p. 12.*

$^2$ "Plato the philosopher benefited from the writings of Moses." <note>original Greek: Πλατων δε ὁ φιλοσοφος εκ των Μωσεως — ωφεληθεις</note> *Clement of Alexandria, Stromata, Book 1, Volume 1, p. 419. See also p. 60, 176, 355.* "Do you think that in saying these things, Plato followed the doctrines of the Hebrews?" <note>original Greek: Αρα σοι δοκει ταυτα λεγων ὁ Πλατων τοις Ἑβραιων επηκολȣθηκεναι δογμασι;</note> *Eusebius, Preparation for the Gospel <note>original Latin: "Præp. Evang."</note>, Book 11, p. 534 and 540.*

<summary>This page discusses Justin Martyr’s interpretation of Plato's philosophical triad (God, Matter, and Form) and argues that Plato derived his "original truths" from the ancient Hebrews rather than his own invention.</summary>
<keywords>Justin Martyr, Plato, Triad, Matter, first principle, Hebrew influence, Clement of Alexandria, Eusebius, Greek philosophy, religious history</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Plato's philosophical concepts, specifically his ideas about a secondary divine intellect, were derived from the theology of the Hellenistic Jews.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>

It is true that even these people [the Jews] did not have a perfect and exact knowledge of these doctrines; yet they had enough knowledge to improve upon paganism <note>original: "heathenism"</note> and to instruct the "divine" Plato. 

And while he says that what he called *Intellect* <note>original Greek: Εννοημα (Ennoema)</note>, foreigners <note>original Greek: ὁι Βαρβαροι (hoi Barbaroi)</note> styled "the Word" <note>original Greek: Λογος (Logos)</note>—meaning Reason—none but the Hellenistic Jews <term>Hellenistic Jews</term> <note>Greek-speaking Jews living outside of Israel, particularly in Alexandria, Egypt</note> could have given it this name. For I believe Plato's meaning, when he mentions—"which the foreigners called the Word" <note>original Greek: ὁπερ ὁι Βαρβαροι Λογον ειρηκασι</note>—is that the people to whom he refers expressed it literally by this name. 

No people but the Jews had any knowledge of a second Divine Being of such a high rank and character; and none but the Jews in Egypt could have translated it in this manner as *the Word* <note>original Greek: Λογος (Logos)</note>. That they translated it in this way can be seen in the Greek version of the Bible <note>The Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Scriptures</note>. For although this version was not produced until after the time of Plato, we may reasonably conclude that the term *Word*, as a Person in Scripture, was previously expressed this way by the Hellenistic Jews in those regions.
