<meta>This page continues the discussion of how ancient philosophers, specifically the Platonists, adopted concepts of a Trinity from earlier sources, identifying these principles with terms like "the Word."</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>

### THEIR EVIDENCE OF GREAT CONSEQUENCE.
<sig>3</sig>

The Platonists and other philosophers who accepted these truths were not uniform in their descriptions, nor consistent in their explanations. However, when they speak of a *Trinity* <note>original: "Trias"</note>, which consisted of three *ruling principles* <note>original Greek: αρχαι (archai)</note>; and when they describe the first as *God* <note>original Greek: Θεος (Theos)</note>, the second as *Reason* <note>original Greek: Νυς (Nous)</note>—the same as the *Word* <note>original Greek: Λογος (Logos)</note> or divine *Word*—and when they add a third co-existing Being which they style *Divine Wisdom* <note>original Greek: Σοφια (Sophia)</note> and the *Soul of the world* <note>original Greek: ψυχη τε Κοσμου (psyche te Kosmou)</note>, they adopt articles of great consequence. 

We know all too well how such a belief is opposed and denied in our own times as impious, idolatrous, and contrary to both religion and reason. Yet the people I have been speaking of embraced these ideas as soon as they had any knowledge of them, and they saw their suitability and truth. And who were they who gave this approval to the

<meta>catchword: doctrines</meta>

<summary>This page explores how Platonists and other ancient philosophers identified a Trinity of divine principles—God, Reason (the Word), and Divine Wisdom (the Soul of the World). The author argues that despite modern rejection of these ideas as irrational, ancient thinkers recognized them as profoundly true and significant.</summary>

<keywords>Platonists, Trinity, Logos, Soul of the World, Divine Wisdom, Greek philosophy, Theology, ruling principles</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the concept of a Trinity was not only accepted by ancient philosophers like Socrates and Plato but was also a foundational, universally recognized doctrine of the early Christian Church.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>

...doctrines mentioned above? Some of the wisest men the world has ever seen—those who worked hardest to achieve true knowledge <note>original: "science"</note> and made the greatest progress toward virtue and the knowledge of God. We have no examples of human power, without divine assistance, reaching so far. They saw a logical fit and appropriateness in these ideas, even where people deny them today. Of this famous group, I will mention only four: Socrates, Plato, Aristotle, and Zeno. If Philo Judaeus <note>A Jewish philosopher from Alexandria who sought to harmonize Greek philosophy with the Hebrew Bible</note> went further than they did, it was because he drew from a more abundant source of divine knowledge provided by a later revelation.

We have seen that from the time this revelation began—that is, from the time of the Apostles to the end of the second century—a Sacred Trinity <note>original: "Trias"</note> was universally accepted in every region where a Christian church was established. From this, I conclude that even if an error of this kind had appeared so early, it could not have spread so equally across so many remote parts of the world. And I go even further, and am persuaded...

<summary>The author argues that the concept of the Trinity was recognized by great ancient philosophers and universally accepted by the early Christian Church, suggesting its widespread adoption proves it was a divine truth rather than a local error.</summary>

<keywords>Socrates, Plato, Aristotle, Zeno, Philo Judaeus, Sacred Trias, Trinity, early Church, revelation, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the doctrine of the Trinity is not a human invention but a divine truth, as evidenced by its widespread acceptance and its compatibility with the logic of the greatest ancient philosophers.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>

persuaded, that this doctrine is so far from the natural ideas of humanity <note>original: "so little obvious to the notions of mankind"</note> that it could scarcely have been devised by human imagination. Even if it had been invented, as I said before, it could not have spread so widely throughout the world. 

It has now endured for ages. We accept and uphold it not because of any individual or biased opinion, but because it is accompanied and confirmed by divine authority <note>original: "sanction"</note>. Furthermore, it has the consistent approval <note>original: "suffrage"</note> of the wisest men, who have also passed it down to us. 

We can be certain that there is nothing in it contrary to reason, seeing as those great masters of logic among the Greeks so readily adopted it. Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE), a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher</note> went far beyond them, as he had better access to information. Although he was no friend to Christianity, he admitted most of the primary doctrines regarding the two natures <note>original: "characters," referring to the divine and human natures of Christ</note> of our Savior. And although he misapplied them in some ways, he clearly acknowledges and defends them. He was followed by those who belonged to the School of Alexandria in succession, such as Plotinus and Porphyry <note>Neoplatonist philosophers who, while not Christian, shared certain metaphysical views on the nature of the divine</note>,

<summary>The author argues that the doctrine of the Trinity is a divine truth rather than a human invention, citing its long history and its acceptance by both Greek philosophers and the School of Alexandria.</summary>

<keywords>doctrine, Christianity, Savior, Philo of Alexandria, School of Alexandria, Plotinus, Porphyry, divine sanction, logic, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that even philosophers and scholars who were hostile to Christianity acknowledged the divine nature of the Logos (the Word), suggesting that such truths must have come from the Gospel rather than earlier, imperfect philosophical systems.</meta>

<page-num>( 79 )</page-num>

and Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Greek Neoplatonist philosopher who was famously critical of Christian doctrine.</note>. They were men of great learning and bitter enemies of Christianity; yet they maintained the doctrines mentioned above. They borrowed these ideas not from Plato or Pythagoras—whose knowledge of such things was limited, imperfect, and different <note>original: "dissimilar"</note>—but from the Gospel itself, where alone they were found in such abundance. Even though these concepts are beyond human understanding <note>original: "apprehension"</note>, these thinkers accepted them because they believed them to be divine truths and consistent with reason.

These doctrines have likewise been upheld by some of the most learned among the Jews, despite how relentless <note>original: "implacable"</note> their opposition to Christianity might be. They accepted the doctrine of the Messiah and spoke of him as the Word of God and as God, existing before the angels and before the creation of the world. This is evident from their <term>Targums</term> <note>Aramaic translations or paraphrases of the Hebrew Bible produced from the Second Temple period onward.</note> and other <term>Rabbinical writings</term>, of which Pietro Galatino <note>Pietro Galatino (c. 1460–1540) was an Italian scholar and friar who wrote on Hebrew and Christian theology.</note> gives an extensive account.

From this we learn that those mysterious truths concerning the Second Person <note>The Second Person of the Trinity, or Jesus Christ as the Eternal Word.</note>, which in these times are rejected by many Christians as impious and idolatrous, were accepted by the very people who were the greatest enemies of idolatry and who always showed themselves to be the most hostile toward Christianity and the Gospel. They upheld these truths as being, once made known, consistent with reason and supported by the authority of the Scriptures.

## PARTICULAR DOCTRINES MAINTAINED BY PHILO.

### FROM HIS OWN WORDS.

#### OF THE IMPURITY OF OUR NATURE.

For the Divine Oracle <note>original: "το Λογιον" (to Logion); Philo often uses this term to refer to the Word of God or sacred scripture.</note> says that the Tabernacle <note>The sacred portable dwelling of God used by the Israelites; here Philo uses it to symbolize God's presence in the world.</note> was established in the midst of our impurity, so that we might have a means of being purified—washing away and getting rid of the things that defile our wretched life, which is full of disgrace.<sup>1</sup> <note>original Greek: "Της γαρ ακαθαρσιας ἡμων εν μεσῳ φησι την σκηνην ἱδρυσασθαι το Λογιον, ἱνα εχωμεν ᾧ καθαρθησομεθα, εκνιψαμενοι και απολεσαμενοι τα καταρρυπαινοντα ἡμων τον αθλιον, και δυσκλειας γεμοντα, βιον."</note>

For the Scripture informs us that it pleased God to establish the tabernacle of His oracle in the middle of our impurities, so that we might have an opportunity to be cleansed by washing away all those impurities <note>original: "sordes"; a Latin term meaning filth, dregs, or foul matter.</note> with which the wretched and base nature of man overflows.

The author seems here to allude to a passage by Saint Paul, who mentions the High Priest who is seated at the right hand of the throne of the Majesty in the Heavens—*A Minister of the sanctuary, and of the true tabernacle, which the Lord established, and not man.*$^1$ . . . *Which consisted only of food and drinks, and various washings, and physical regulations;*$^2$ <note>original: "carnal ordinances"; refers to religious laws focused on outward physical rituals.</note> and which in the Book of Wisdom is called—*a resemblance of the Holy Tabernacle, which you have prepared from the beginning.*$^3$

# ON PROPITIATION

He speaks of <term>propitiation</term> <note>the act of appeasing or making amends to God for sin</note>, which he styles, like Saint John, a "means of appeasement" <note>original Greek: ἱλασμός (hilasmos)</note>; and he seems to have some faint allusions, not only to original sin, but also to our renovation through the Word of God <note>original Greek: Λόγος (Logos)</note>.

"This is remission, this is the complete freedom of the soul: when it shakes off the error in which it was bewildered, and anchors itself in a nature free from error, returning to those inheritances which it received when it breathed a bright air and flourished in the pursuit of what is beautiful. For then the Sacred Word, admiring its struggles, honored it by giving it an exceptional prize—an immortal inheritance and a place among the incorruptible." <note>original Greek: Τοῦτ’ ἔστιν ἄφεσις, τοῦτ’ ἐλευθερία παντελὴς ψυχῆς, ὃν ἐπλανήθη τε πλάνον ἀποσειομένης, καὶ πρὸς τὴν ἀπλανῆ φύσιν μεθορμιζομένης, καὶ ἐπὶ τοὺς κλήρους ἐπανιούσης, οὓς ἔλαχεν, ἡνίκα λαμπρὸν ἔπνει, καὶ τοῖς περὶ τῶν καλῶν πόνοις ἤνθει. Τότε γὰρ αὐτὴν τῶν ἄθλων ἀγάμενος ὁ Ἱερὸς Λόγος ἐτίμησε, γέρας ἐξαίρετον δούς, κλῆρον ἀθάνατον, τὴν ἐν ἀφθάρτῳ γένει τάξιν.</note>²

### ON A MEDIATOR AND FAITH.

An account is given in Numbers 16:46 of a terrifying plague that followed the rebellion of Korah, when fourteen thousand seven hundred of the people were destroyed, and even more were in danger. But

*Moses said to Aaron, "Take a censer and put fire in it from off the altar, add incense, and go quickly to the congregation and make atonement for them." And he* (*Aaron*, the High Priest) *stood between the dead and the living, and the plague was stopped.* Philo saw clearly that this intercession by Aaron was a symbolic representation <note>original: "type"</note> of the mediation of the Great High Priest, whom he recognizes as the *Logos*, or *Word of God.*¹ Accordingly, in another passage, he provides a very specific description of the Divine Word and his mediatorial power, which he derives from the history mentioned above.

To the Archangel and most ancient Word, the Father who created all things gave a special gift: that, standing as a boundary, he might distinguish the creation from its Maker. This same Word is both a petitioner <note>original Greek: ἱκέτης (hiketēs)</note> on behalf of the mortal who is always suffering under corruption, and an ambassador <note>original Greek: πρεσβευτής (presbeutēs)</note> of the Ruler to the subject. He rejoices in this gift and, taking pride in it, he describes it by saying: "And I stood between the Lord and you; being neither uncreated like God, nor created like you, but in the middle of the extremes, acting as a bond for both—" <note>original Greek: Τῷ δὲ Ἀρχαγγέλῳ καὶ πρεσβυτάτῳ Λόγῳ δωρεὰν ἐξαίρετον ἔδωκεν ὁ τὰ ὅλα γεννήσας πατὴρ...</note>

The Great Father of all gave this extraordinary gift to that most ancient and principal of all angels, His Holy Word: that he should stand midway and separate the creature from the Creator. This Word (the *Logos*) is accordingly the intercessor for mortal man, who is always prone to corruption; and he is appointed as a messenger and ambassador <note>original: "legate"</note> from the Supreme Lord to his subjects. This office is highly pleasing to him, and he shows the dignity of it when he is introduced saying (regarding the rebellion of Korah), "It was I who stood as a mediator between you and the Lord." For he is not unbegotten like God, nor created like man, but a medium between the extremes,

We are then entitled to be the children of the Most High; and our sins are forgiven through him—the Angel, who is the Word, just like a physician of evils <note>original Greek: τον Αγγελον, ὁς εστι Λογος, ὡσπερ Ιατρον κακων</note>¹—*that Angel, the Word of God, who is the physician and healer of all our evils.* For we cannot please God by ourselves: even our best actions are not truly acceptable—not even virtue itself, without divine favor, is sufficient of itself to be beneficial <note>original Greek: μηδε την αρετην, ανευ θειας επιφροσυνης, ἱκανην εξ ἑαυτης ωφελειν ειναι</note>²—*Even virtue itself without the divine sanction can have no merit, nor advantage.* All these good things are accomplished by the Word, styled the great High Priest, the Son of God, the Divine Word <note>original Greek: τον Λογον Θειον</note>. And he himself is the intercessor for that which is mortal <note>original Greek: Ὁ δε αυτος ἱκετης του θνητου</note>. *And he is the Intercessor for mortal man.* Hence he is mentioned as the Middle One <note>original Greek: Μεσος</note>, and the Boundary <note>original Greek: Μεθοριος</note>, *the Mediator*, and as *bordering upon both*; by whom we are made sons of God <note>original Greek: υἱοι Θεου ανθρωποι</note>—*sons of God*; and heirs of divine things <note>original Greek: και θειων πραγματων κληρονομοι</note>—*and heirs of heavenly advantages.*

# ON FREEDOM GRANTED BY THE DEATH OF THE HIGH PRIEST.

I have mentioned that Philo was very much perplexed <note>original: "embarrassed," meaning troubled by a difficult problem</note> by that part of the Law which stated that a person guilty of manslaughter should flee to an appointed city of refuge and not be released <note>original: "acquitted"</note> until the death of the High Priest.

The fourth and remaining point of those proposed was the time limit for the return of the fugitives—the death of the High Priest—which caused me much difficulty in the literal reading <note>original Greek: Τεταρτον και λοιπον ην των προταθεντων, ἡ προθεσμια της των πεφευγοτων καθοδου, του Αρχιερεως ὁ θανατος, πολλην εν τω ρητω μοι παρεχουσα δυσκολιαν</note>. For an unequal punishment is ordained by law against those who have done the same things, if indeed some will remain in exile for a longer time, and others for a shorter time. For some High Priests are very long-lived, while others live only a very short time. Some are appointed when they are young, and others when they are old. And of those convicted of unintentional homicide, some were exiled at the beginning of a priesthood, while others were exiled when the priest was already about to die <note>original Greek: Ανισος γαρ ἡ τιμωρια κατα των τα αυτα δρασαντων νομοθετειται, ειγε ὁι μεν πλειω χρονον αποδρασονται, ὁι δε ελαττω. Μακροβιωτατοι γαρ, ὁι δε ολιγοχρονιωτατοι των Αρχιερεων εισι. Και ὁι μεν νεοι, ὁι δε πρεσβυται καθισανται. Και των εαλωκοτων επ’ ακουσιω φονω, ὁι μεν εν αρχη της ἱερωσυνης εφυγαδευθησαν, ὁι δ’ ηδη μελλοντος τελευταν του ἱερωμενου</note>.*—The fourth remaining article of these propositions is the ordinance concerning the return of those who had fled for refuge, which 
->was<-

<page-num>( 92 )</page-num>

*The release* was to be at the death of the *High Priest*. *The consideration of this has given me a great deal of trouble regarding its meaning. For the punishment appointed is not equally distributed; as some must have been confined for a longer, and some for a shorter, period; and as some of the* High Priests *held office for a longer, others for a shorter, time; and some arrive at that dignity when young, others when old. And those who were accidentally guilty of bloodshed must have sought this shelter, some at the beginning of the priesthood, and others when the* High Priest *was near his death. These were the difficulties which gave* Philo *so much uneasiness, and which for a time he could not solve. But he says that at last he perceived it to be symbolic* <note>original: "typical," referring to a "type" or religious symbol</note>, *and a mystery.* 

"For I maintain," he says, "that the High Priest is not a man, but the Divine Word <note>original Greek: Λογον θειον (Logon theion)</note>, who has no part in any wrongdoing, whether intentional or unintentional... because, I believe, he was born of parents who are immortal and perfectly pure: God as his Father, who is the Father of all things; and Wisdom <note>original Greek: Σοφιας (Sophias)</note> as his Mother, through whom the universe was brought into being <note>original Greek: δι’ ἡς τα ὁλα ηλθεν εις γενεσιν (di’ hēs ta hola ēlthen eis genesin)—literally "through whom the whole came into existence"</note>."¹ I maintain then,

that the *High Priest* alluded to is not a man, but the *Sacred Logos*, the *Word of God*, who is incapable of either intentional or unintentional sin. I therefore conclude that he was born of *parents* who are the most pure and incorruptible: having for his *Father*, *God*, who is the *Creator* <note>original: "Author"</note> of all things; and *Divine Wisdom* for his *Mother*, through whom all things came into being. "And because his head is anointed with oil" <note>original Greek: Και διοτι την κεφαλην κεχριται ελαιῳ (Kai dioti tēn kephalēn kechritai elaiō)</note>¹—*and for this reason, he is said to have his head anointed with oil.* Who would think that, when he saw so much, he would not have seen more? How could he have failed to perceive the *Messiah Christ*, the anointed of God so often mentioned in the *Prophets*, who is clearly pointed out here? He concludes:

"These things I have not spoken without a purpose, but to show that the most natural appointed time for the return of fugitives is the death of the High Priest" <note>original Greek: Ταυτα δ' ουκ απο σκοπου μοι λελεκται, αλλ' ὑπερ του διδαξαι, ότι φυσικωτατη προθεσμια καθοδου φυγαδων, ὁ του Αρχιερεως εστι θανατος (Tauta d' ouk apo skopou moi lelektai, all' hyper tou didaxai, hoti physikōtatē prothesmia kathodou phygadōn, ho tou Archiereōs esti thanatos)</note>²—*These things I have not mentioned without good reason: but to show that the natural and appointed return of the fugitives*

...was the death of the High Priest, whom he calls the *most Holy Word* <note>original Greek: ἱερώτατος Λόγος (hierōtatos Logos)</note> of God. However, he treats this death as an allegory, and by over-refining his interpretation, he ruins the entire meaning.

### THE SOURCE FROM WHICH HE BORROWED HIS OPINIONS.

These were the opinions of Philo Judeus <note>Also known as Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who lived in Egypt during the 1st century AD.</note> on these very interesting subjects. We have seen the numerous and significant reasons he provided for his views. The only question is: where could he have obtained them? Little of this nature could have been derived from Plato and his disciples. Furthermore, many of these truths could not have been obtained by him even from his fellow Jews and their sacred writings, except for a few hints. These truths have since been made obvious and clear by a later revelation <note>The author is referring to the New Testament and the teachings of Jesus.</note>. Therefore, there was no source he could have possibly consulted except for the Apostles, the Evangelists, and other disciples of Christ.

<meta>catchword: It</meta>

<summary>The author concludes that Philo of Alexandria's complex theology regarding the "Holy Word" (Logos) could not have originated from Greek philosophy or Jewish tradition alone, suggesting instead that Philo must have been influenced by early Christian teachings.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judeus, Logos, Holy Word, allegory, Plato, Jewish scriptures, New Testament, Apostles, Christian influence, theology</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that Philo Judaeus (Philo of Alexandria) was a contemporary of Jesus and the Apostles, suggesting he was influenced by early Christian writings.</meta>

<page-num>( 95 )</page-num>

It has been claimed that he <note>Philo of Alexandria</note> was born much too early to have been taught by them <note>the Apostles and Evangelists</note>. However, this claim is made without sufficient evidence. First, we do not know the exact date of his birth. Second, we are certain from his own testimony that he traveled as an ambassador from Alexandria to Rome in the fourth year of the Emperor Caligula’s reign, which was the year 42 AD <note>original: "Christian Æra 42"</note>. If he were of a mature age at the time of this embassy, he must have been a contemporary <note>original: "coæval"</note> of our Savior <note>Jesus Christ</note>.* Since he lived into the reign of Nero <note>Nero reigned from 54–68 AD</note>, we can be certain that he outlived Christ by many years. In any case, he must have been an adult during the ministry of Christ and, consequently, saw many of his followers for many years afterward. Judging by his doctrines, he was likely acquainted with the Gospels and the Epistles <note>letters</note> of Saint Paul.

---

* Even if he were born some years before the birth of Christ, as some have argued, he still would have been a contemporary of Christ during his entire time on earth, for he certainly outlived him by many years.

<summary>The author argues that Philo of Alexandria was a contemporary of Jesus Christ and the Apostles, citing Philo's 42 AD embassy to Rome as proof that he was alive and active during the early spread of Christianity.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Chronology, Caligula, Nero, contemporary, Apostles, Gospels, Saint Paul, Alexandria, Christian Era</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Philo of Alexandria’s religious writings were influenced by early Christian leaders, specifically Saint Paul and Saint Mark.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

Paul and Saint Mark, the first bishop of Alexandria, could not have been unknown to him <note>Philo of Alexandria</note>. I believe these facts are largely evident from the internal evidence found in the doctrines he has passed down. There is only one alternative: he must have either borrowed these truths from the source mentioned <note>the Apostles and early Christians</note>, or he must have received them through divine inspiration. However, he seems to have no claim to such a great privilege <note>original: "indulgence," meaning the direct favor of divine inspiration</note>. Consequently, he must have received them from the Apostles and the first converts <note>original: "proselytes"</note> to the Gospel. The very words in which those doctrines are delivered show this to be true.

### INFERENCES.

It was not only Philo who took advantage of these teachings; he simply set an early example. Soon, the pagan world at large was improved by the doctrines of Christianity. The wisest philosophers saw its superiority. And although they held the common people in contempt,

<summary>The author argues that Philo of Alexandria must have learned his doctrines from the Apostles or Saint Mark, as he had no claim to divine inspiration himself. This influence on Philo is presented as the beginning of a broader trend where pagan philosophers recognized the superiority of Christian teaching.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Saint Paul, Saint Mark, Alexandria, divine inspiration, Apostles, Gospel, pagan philosophers, early Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that pagan philosophers, though often hostile to Christianity, were nonetheless deeply influenced by its doctrines and ethics, which were spread in part by the writings of Philo of Alexandria.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<sig>G</sig>

from among whom it <note>the Gospel/Christianity</note> proceeded, and they could not be reconciled to its great Author; yet they borrowed their ethics from Him and enriched their philosophy with truths unknown before. Many of them admitted the very mysteries <note>the fundamental, often supernatural doctrines of the Christian faith</note>: and though they tried to explain them each according to his particular imagination <note>original: "fancy"</note>; yet, I say, they were admitted, and not thought reprehensible, nor contrary to reason. Theodoret <note>a 5th-century Christian bishop and historian</note> takes notice, in his *Cure of the Greek Maladies*, Book 6 <note>original: "Θεραπευτ. 6."</note>, that Plutarch, Numenius, and Plotinus borrowed from the sacred writings. To these might be added Epictetus, Antoninus, Porphyrius, Maximus Tyrius, Hierocles, Proclus, with many more. The emperor Julian, that bitter enemy, copied largely from the system which he tried to ruin. The "lump of leaven" mentioned by our Savior <note>a reference to the Parable of the Leaven in Matthew 13:33, symbolizing the quiet, transformative spread of the Gospel</note> began very soon to operate upon the mass in which it was enclosed, and continues to this day in its operation. The good effects of Christianity are felt far beyond its limits and will in time be universal. That they are so extensive at present is owing in no small degree to the writings of Philo.

<header>FURTHER INFERENCES.</header>

If these arguments, which I have drawn <note>original: "deduced"</note> from Philo, are true—as I believe they are—we have, as a consequence, the earliest and most decisive evidence in favor of the doctrine with which we are concerned. We can be certain <note>original: "certified"</note>, if any proof is needed <note>original: "wanting"</note>, regarding the opinion of the first Christians and Apostles with respect to this mystery <note>the nature of the Logos or the Trinity</note>. The testimony <note>original: "attestation"</note> of the Church Fathers <note>the influential theologians and writers of the early Christian church</note> is highly valid and cannot be set aside. But this evidence from Philo is even more powerful and convincing, as it is earlier. 

And though it may not come from an enemy, it certainly does not come from a friend <note>Philo was a Jewish philosopher, not a Christian, making his agreement with Christian concepts seen as more objective</note>; so there could have been no prejudice or partiality in favor of the points <note>original: "articles"</note> mentioned. It is certain that the words of the Apostles, and especially those of our Savior, do not need any outside evidence to be authenticated. But when, in these times, the meaning <note>original: "purport"</note> of those words is disputed, and the very doctrines

[doc]trines denied; and when the opinions of the Church Fathers <note>the early influential theologians of the Christian church</note> are considered either too late or too vague <note>original: "indeterminate"</note>, then this earlier evidence from an outsider <note>original: "Alien"; referring to Philo, a Jewish philosopher who was not a Christian</note> must carry weight. For he could not have obtained the truths he put forward <note>original: "advanced"</note> without assistance. Human wisdom could not have arrived at such knowledge on its own. 

He was, therefore, indebted to others for this information <note>original: "intelligence"</note>, and I have shown to whom he was indebted—or rather, he has provided plain proof of it himself. He drew from a noble source, and the examples <note>original: "samples"</note> he has produced show that they come from the *well of wisdom*, the *spring of living waters*, and the *fountain of life*. He drew from it only partially; yet, what he has provided is of great benefit <note>original: "advantage"</note>.

### THE MISAPPLICATION OF THESE GREAT
### TRUTHS BY PHILO.

I have observed that Philo speaks only of the divinity of the *Logos*, or Word of God. He deliberately <note>original: "industriously"</note> avoids mentioning the Messiah, the "Anointed" of God, whom the Apostles and Evangelists call Christ. Although he silently <note>original: "tacitly"</note> denies the humanity of our Savior, he nonetheless takes almost every attribute belonging to Christ—the Son of Man—and applies them to the *Logos* in his pre-existing <note>original: "prior"</note> and heavenly state. 

However, these attributes are not consistent with the *Logos* as Philo describes him <note>original: "with this part of his character"</note>. He therefore improperly <note>original: "unduly"</note> assigns <note>original: "adjudges"</note> to the *Logos*—as depicted in his writings—the various roles<sup>1</sup> of High Priest to make an atonement, and of Messenger<sup>2</sup> from the Deity to declare God's <note>original: "his"</note> will to humanity.

---

<sup>1</sup> Philo. Vol 1. p. 653. This is the world in which the High Priest is his first-born divine Word <note>original: "ὅδε ὁ κόσμος, ἐν ᾧ καὶ ἀρχιερεὺς, ὁ πρωτόγονος αυτȣ θεῖος Λόγος"</note>.

<sup>2</sup> Ibid. Vol 1. p. 501. A messenger from the ruler to the subject <note>original: "Πρεσβευτης τȣ ἡγεμόνος πρὸς τὸ ὑπήκοον"</note>.

If we restore these attributes to the **Messiah**, to whom they truly belong, the opinions of **Philo** will be found in most instances to be very similar to those of the **Apostles**—and often exactly the same.

->### ON THE PASSAGE IN PHILO'S *ON THE CONFUSION*<-
->### *OF TONGUES*, VOL. 1, P. 427, L. 3.<-

I have mentioned that Philo speaks of the Logos, or *Word of God*, as sometimes *bearing the likeness of man*—"the man according to the image" <note>original Greek: "ὁ κατ' εικονα ανθρωπος"</note>.* The learned editor <note>This refers to Thomas Mangey, who published an edition of Philo's works in 1742.</note> thinks that this is not the correct reading. In its place, he substitutes "he in whose image man is" <note>original Greek: "οὗ κατ' εικονα ανθρωπος"</note>. He does this because he imagines that Philo never could have allowed that any such likeness existed between the Word and humanity. 

But in this, I think he is mistaken. In the first place, we have the authority of Eusebius for this reading, who quotes the entire passage; a part of which is "the man according to the image" <note>original Greek: "ὁ κατ' εικονα ανθρωπος"</note>.^2^ In the next place, the reading is justified by the context, which would be damaged by the editor's alteration. Philo is mentioning several characters and titles of the *first-begotten Son* <note>original Greek: "πρωτογονος υἱος"</note>—

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>The author disputes a textual correction made by a previous editor regarding Philo's description of the Logos, arguing that Philo did indeed characterize the Word of God as "the man according to the image."</summary>

<sig>G 4</sig>

of *God*: such as the — $^1$ *great Archangel of many names* <note>original Greek: "Αρχάγγελος πολυώνυμος"</note> — *The Great Archangel under different appellations*: — the *beginning* <note>original Greek: "ἀρχὴ"</note>, the *name of God* <note>original Greek: "ὄνομα Θεοῦ"</note> (Jehovah), the *Word* <note>original Greek: "Λόγος"</note>, the *man according to the image* <note>original Greek: "ὁ κατ' εἰκόνα ἄνθρωπος"</note>, and $^2$ the *one who sees Israel* <note>original Greek: "ὁρῶν Ἰσραὴλ"</note> — *The beginning or Creator of all things*, *the name of God* (Jehovah), *the Word of God*, *the likeness of man*, *and the Guardian of Israel*. If we rearrange this passage as our editor <note>Thomas Mangey</note> advises, an article is introduced in a most improper place and in a most unnecessary manner. The discussion relates to the different names and attributes of God's First-born; the idea of man's likeness to Christ has no place or connection here. The words are to be considered as relating to one of the titles and attributes of the Son of God. The Logos in the Scriptures is described as appearing to Abraham and to other Patriarchs in a human form; and to this, I imagine, Philo alludes when he says, *the man according to the image* <note>original Greek: "ὁ κατ' εἰκόνα ἄνθρωπος"</note>. ### SOME FURTHER SPECIFIC DOCTRINES

### BORROWED BY PHILO
<note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>

### FROM THE APOSTOLIC WRITINGS,

### BUT MISAPPLIED.

<sig>A</sig>

the support of $^1$Origen, who tells us that Philo wrote *concerning the fact that God is not described as being like a man* <note>original Greek: "περι του μη ὡς ανθρωπον λεγεσθαι ειναι τον Θεον"</note>. Philo, on the page mentioned previously (Vol. 1, p. 427), says that *man was born in the image of the Word* <note>original: "after the image"</note>. Therefore, there must have been a mutual likeness, however partial it might be.$^2$

---

$^1$ Origen, *Commentary on Matthew* <note>original Latin: "in Matt."</note>, p. 477.

$^2$ Philo says there are two parts of the Scriptures which mention this—one stating that *God is not like a man* <note>original Greek: "εν μεν, ὅτι ουκ ὡς ανθρωπος ὁ Θεος"</note>, and the other that *he is like a man* <note>original Greek: "ἑτερον δὲ, ὅτι ὡς ανθρωπος"</note>. *That God is Unchangeable* <note>original Latin: "Quod Deus sit Immutabilis"</note>, Vol. 1, pp. 282, 283. And *On Dreams [Sent by God]* <note>original Latin: "Quod a Deo [mittantur somnia]"</note>, p. 656.

->I.<-

->THE LOGOS IS<-

**THE** Son of God—The upright Word <term>Logos: A Greek term meaning "Word," "Reason," or "Plan." In theology, it refers to the divine intellect or the second person of the Trinity.</term> of the Son of God <note>original Greek: Τον ορθον Λογον Θεȣ Υιου</note>—of a divine nature.<sup>1</sup> The Son of God the Father <note>original Greek: Πατρος (Θεȣ) Υιος</note>.<sup>2</sup>

## VII.

The instrument through whom the world was made. His Word, whom God used as an instrument for making the world. <note>original Greek: Ὁ Λογος αυτου, ᾧ καθαπερ οργανῳ προσχρησαμενος (ὁ Θεος) εκοσμοποιει.</note><gloss>²</gloss>

It was the divine Word by whom all things were ordered and arranged—The divine Word, who ordered these things. <note>original Greek: Τον Θειον Λογον τον ταυτα διακοσμησαντα.</note><gloss>³</gloss>

<margin>John 1:3</margin> All things were made through him (the Word), and without him nothing was made that has been made. <note>original Greek: Παντα δι' αυτου (του Λογου) εγενετο, και χωρις αυτου εγενετο ουδε ἑν, ὁ γεγονεν.</note>
*All things were made by him (the Word), and without him was not anything made that was made.*

## XIV.

### The Eternal Word — <note>original Greek: ὁ αἲδιος Λογος (ho aidios Logos)</note>¹

---

<margin>John 12:34.</margin> Christ remains forever. <note>original Greek: Χριςος μενει εις τον αιωνα.</note>
*Christ remains forever.*

<margin>2 Timothy 1:9.</margin> —before the world began. <note>original Greek: προ χρονων αιωνιων.</note>
*Who was—before the world began.*

## XVI.

He supports the World. For the Word <term>Logos</term> of the Self-Existent Being, who is the bond of all things . . . holds all the parts together and binds them fast <note>original Greek: ὁ τε γαρ του Οντος Λογος, δεσμος ων των ἁπαντων . . . . . συνεχει τα μερη παντα, και σφιγγει (ho te gar tou Ontos Logos, desmos ōn tōn hapantōn . . . synechei ta merē panta, kai sphingei)</note>¹— *The Logos is the connecting power by which all things are united. . . . . He makes all the various parts of the universe unite, and he preserves them in that union.*

The divine Word surrounds and has filled all things <note>original Greek: Ὁ θειος Λογος περιεχει τα ὁλα, και πεπληρωκεν (Ho theios Logos periechei ta hola, kai peplērōken)</note>.² *The divine Word surrounds and upholds all things, and has brought them to perfection.*

---

## XVII.

The Word <term>Logos</term> is nearest to God without any separation; being, as it were, fixed and founded upon the only true existing Deity, with nothing coming between to disturb that unity. The Divine Word... of all things perceived by the mind, he is the most ancient and the closest, with no boundary or distance coming between, being established upon the Only One, who truly exists. <note>original Greek: ὁ Λογος Θειος. . . . των νοητων ἁπαξ-ἁπαντων ὁ πρεσβυτατος, ὁ εγγυτάτω, μηδενος οντος μεθοριου διαστηματος, του μονου, ὁ εστιν αψευδῶς, αφιδρυμενος (ho Logos Theios . . . tōn noētōn hapax-hapantōn ho presbytatos, ho engytatō, mēdenos ontos methoriou diastēmatos, tou monou, ho estin apseudōs, aphidrymenos)</note>¹

### XIX.

->Of the Word <term>Logos</term> presiding over the imperfect, and God only over the perfect and wise.<-

For the Word <term>Logos</term> may be considered the God of those of us who are imperfect; but for the wise and perfect, the First and Chief must be looked upon as God.¹ <note>original Greek: Ουτος γαρ (ὁ Λογος) ἡμων των ατελων αν ειη Θεος, των δε σοφων και τελειων ὁ Πρωτος (Houtos gar (ho Logos) hēmōn tōn atelōn an eiē Theos, tōn de sophōn kai teleiōn ho Prōtos)</note>

Philo was not consistent when he made this distinction. He did not consider that God’s mercy is not limited, but "is over all his works." Furthermore, regarding the Word, he admits (as will soon be shown) that he distributes his heavenly assistance equally to everyone who seeks it. And far from his goodness being restricted only to the imperfect, Philo stated just before—

The soul of those who are more pure is nourished by the full influence of the Word <term>Logos</term>.² <note>original Greek: τρεφεται δε των μεν τελειοτερων ἡ ψυχη ὁλῳ τῳ Λογῳ (trephetai de tōn men teleioterōn hē psychē holō tō Logō)</note>

---

¹ *Allegorical Interpretation* <note>original title: De Legum Allegoria</note>, Volume 1, page 128, line 43.
² Ibid. <note>meaning: in the same place/work</note>, page 122, line 6.

<summary>This page critiques the philosopher Philo for inconsistently suggesting that the Word (Logos) serves as a "God" for the imperfect while the "First God" serves the perfect, noting that Philo elsewhere acknowledges the Word's influence extends to all.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Logos, Word of God, spiritual perfection, divine mercy, Allegorical Interpretation, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Philo Judaeus’s distinction between the "perfect" and "imperfect." It suggests that Philo's idea—that the Word (Logos) is only for the weak—may have been a misunderstanding of Christian teachings about Christ’s mission to sinners.</meta>

<page-num>( 124 )</page-num>

He was probably led to form this judgment—that only the weak and simple are entrusted to the Word <term>Logos</term>—from the Christian doctrines with which he had become acquainted but did not perfectly understand. The Word <term>Logos</term>, in the character of Christ, did extend his saving help particularly to those who were in a state of spiritual weakness <note>original: "debility"</note>, but not to the exclusion of others.

---

<margin>Matthew 11:5</margin> The poor have the Gospel preached to them. <note>original Greek: πτωχοι ευαγγελιζονται (ptōchoi euaggelizontai)</note>

<margin>Luke 5:32</margin> I did not come to call the righteous, but sinners to repentance. <note>original Greek: Ουκ εληλυθα καλεσαι δικαιους, αλλ’ αμαρτωλους εις μετανοιαν (Ouk elēlytha kalesai dikaious, all’ hamartōlous eis metanoian)</note>

<margin>1 Timothy 1:15</margin> This is a faithful saying... that Christ Jesus came into the world to save sinners. <note>original Greek: Πιστος ὁ Λογος ... ὁτι Χριστος Ιησους ηλθεν εις τον κοσμον αμαρτωλους σωσαι (Pistos ho Logos ... hoti Christos Iēsous ēlthen eis ton kosmon hamartōlous sōsai)</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Philo Judaeus’s conception of the Word (Logos). While the previous page addressed Philo's view that the Logos helps the weak, this section transitions to the idea that the Logos is a universal source of eternal life and wisdom for all who seek it.</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>

### XX.

The Word <term>Logos</term> is the fountain of wisdom, to which everyone should go with the greatest diligence. By drinking from that sacred spring, they may obtain everlasting life instead of death.

"He encourages the person who is capable of running swiftly to strive without pausing for breath toward the supreme divine Word <term>Logos</term>, who is the fountain of wisdom; so that, by drawing from that stream, he may find eternal life as his prize instead of death." <note>original Greek: Προτρεπει δε εν τον μεν ωκυδρομειν ἱκανον, συντεινειν απνευσι προς τον ανωτατω Λογον θειον, ὁς σοφιας εστι πηγη, ἱνα αρυσάμενος τε ναματος, αντι θανατου ζωην Αϊδιον αθλον εὑρηται.</note>

*It is of the greatest importance to every person who is able to run the course set before them to strive without ceasing to approach the divine Word <term>Logos</term>, the Word of God above, who is the fountain of all wisdom. By drinking deeply of that sacred spring, they may hereafter be rewarded with everlasting life instead of death.*¹

This I quote at length, as it is so very remarkable.

"The utterance of God and the divine Word <term>Logos</term>, from which all forms of instruction and wisdom flow perpetually." <note>original Greek: Ῥημα Θεου και Λογον θειον, αφ᾿ οὗ πασαι παιδειαι και σοφιαι ῥεουσιν αενναοι.</note>²

---

¹ *On Fugitives* <note>original Latin title: De Profugis</note>. Volume 1, page 560, line 31.
² Ibid. page 566, line 9.

<summary>This page presents Philo Judaeus’s description of the divine Logos as the eternal fountain of wisdom and the source of immortality for those who seek it. The author emphasizes the importance of Philo's view that the Word of God provides the "prize" of everlasting life.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Logos, fountain of wisdom, Word of God, immortality, De Profugis, eternal life, Greek philosophy</keywords>

<page-num>124</page-num>

He was likely led to form this judgment—concerning the idea that only weak and simple beings are assigned to the Word <term>Logos</term>—based on the Christian doctrines with which he had become acquainted but did not perfectly understand. The Word <term>Logos</term>, in the character of Christ, did indeed extend his saving help particularly to those who were in a state of spiritual weakness <note>original: "debility"</note>, but not to the exclusion of others.

## XX.

The Logos <term>the divine Word</term> is the fountain of wisdom; everyone should most diligently turn to it, so that by drinking from that sacred Spring, they may obtain everlasting life instead of death.

"He encourages anyone who is capable of running swiftly to strain without stopping toward the highest divine Word, who is the fountain of wisdom; so that, having drawn from that stream, he may find eternal life as his prize instead of death." <note>original Greek: Προτρεπει δε ουν τον μεν ωκυδρομειν ἱκανον, συντεινειν απνευσι προς τον ανωτατω Λογον θειον, ὁς σοφιας εστι πηγη, ἱνα αρυσάμενος τε ναματος, αντι θανατου Ζωην Αϊδιον αθλον εὑρηται.</note> *It is of the greatest importance for every person who can run the course set before them to strive without ceasing to approach the divine Logos, the Word of God above, who is the fountain of all wisdom; so that, by drinking deeply of that sacred Spring, they may be rewarded hereafter with everlasting life instead of death.*¹ I quote this at length because it is so very remarkable.

"The utterance of God and the divine Word, from which all forms of education and wisdom flow eternally." <note>original Greek: Ρημα Θεου και Λογον θειον, αφ’ ὁυ πασαι παιδειαι και σοφιαι ῥεουσιν αενναοι.</note>²

### XXI.

## XXII.

He is the Advocate and Intercessor for mortal man. "And the same (the Logos) is a suppliant for the mortal." <note>original Greek: Ὁ δ᾽ αυτος (ὁ Λογος) ἱκετης μεν εστι τȣ θνητȣ. From Philo Judaeus, *Who is the Heir of Divine Things?*</note> <sup>1</sup>

## XXIII.

He ordered and disposed all things. "For the divine Logos separated and distributed all things in nature." <note>original Greek: ὁ τε γαρ Θειος Λογος τα εν τη φυσει διειλε και διενειμε παντα. From Philo Judaeus, *Who is the Heir of Divine Things?*</note> *The divine Logos separated and regulated all things in the world.*¹

"The sacred and divine Logos was the Person who distributed and appointed all things." <note>original Greek: Τομευς ἁπαντων ὁ Ἱερος και Θειος Λογος. Philo uses the term *Tomeus* (the divider or cutter) to describe how the Logos carves out the distinct parts of the universe to create order.</note>²

# XXVII.

## The Seal of God.

The Word of Him who made the world is Himself the seal, by which each of the things that exist has been shaped... as an impression and image of the perfect Word. <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ τοῦ ποιοῦντος (τὸν Κόσμον) Λόγος αὐτός ἐστιν ἡ σφραγίς, ᾗ τῶν ὄντων ἕκαστον μεμόρφωται . . . . ἅτε ἐκμαγεῖον καὶ εἰκὼν τελείου Λόγου.</note> *The Word <note>original: "Logos," referring to the divine reason or speech of God</note>, by whom the world was framed, is the Seal, after the impression of which everything is made . . . and is rendered the likeness and image of the perfect Word of God.¹*

The soul of man... has been stamped with a seal... of which the characteristic is the everlasting Word. <note>original Greek: Ἀνθρώπου ψυχήν . . . . τυπωθεῖσαν σφραγῖδι . . . ἧς ὁ χαρακτήρ ἐστιν ἀΐδιος Λόγος.</note> *The soul of man is an impression of a Seal, of which the prototype and original characteristic is the everlasting Word <note>original: "Logos"</note>.²*

---

John 6:27. For Him (Jesus) the Father has sealed. <note>original Greek: Τοῦτον γὰρ (Ἰησοῦν) ὁ Πατὴρ ἐσφραγίσεν.</note>

*Jesus, the Son of man . . . Him has the Father sealed.*

¹ *On Flight and Finding* <note>original title: De Profugis</note>, Volume 1, page 547, line 49 to page 548, line 2. <note>Philo uses the "seal" metaphor to explain how the physical world takes its form from a divine, spiritual pattern.</note>
² *On Noah’s Planting* <note>original title: De Plantatione Noë</note>, Volume 1, page 332, line 31.

### XXXI.

The happy consequences of humanity’s best endeavors. They are, by the same <term>Logos</term> <note>original: "Logos" — In this context, it refers to the divine Word or Reason, a concept used by both Hellenistic Jewish philosophers like Philo and Christian writers to describe the intermediary between God and the world.</note>, freed from all corruption and entitled to immortality hereafter. For then the Sacred Word, admiring the soul for its struggles, honored it by giving it a special prize: an immortal inheritance and a place among the incorruptible race. ¹ <note>original Greek: Τοτε γαρ αυτην (την ψυχην) των αθλων αγαμενος ὁ Ἱερος Λογος ετιμησε, γερας εξαιρετον δας, κληρον αθανατον, την εν αφθαρτω γενει ταξιν.</note>

<margin>1 Corinthians 15:52.</margin> <note>original Greek: Οι νεκροι (εν Χρισῳ) εγερθησονται αφθαρτοι.</note>
->*The dead (in Christ) shall be raised incorruptible.*<-

<margin>1 Corinthians 15:53.</margin> <note>original Greek: Δει γαρ το φθαρτον τȣτο ενδυσασθαι αφθαρσιαν.</note>
->*For this mortal must put on immortality.*<-

<margin>Romans 8:21.</margin> <note>original Greek: Ὅτι και αυτη ἡ κτισις ελευθερωθησεται απο της δουλειας της φθορας εις την ελευθεριαν της δοξης των τεκνων τȣ Θεȣ.</note>
->*Because the creation itself also shall be delivered from the bondage of corruption into the glorious liberty of the children of God.*<-

### XXXIII.

<margin>* *Who is the Heir of Divine Things* <note>original Latin title: "Quis Rer. Divin. Heres."; full title: "Quis Rerum Divinarum Heres Sit"</note>, Volume 1, page 482, lines 2–30.</margin>
He asks by what means a person may arrive at pure, incorporeal <note>incorporeal: existing without a physical body; purely spiritual.</note> happiness—or, as he expresses it, "to become the heir of incorporeal and divine things" <note>original Greek: γενέσθαι τῶν ἀσωμάτων καὶ θείων πραγμάτων κληρονόμος</note>—and he answers: "He who is inspired from above—the purest mind—he who is released from his bonds and set free." <note>original Greek: Ὁ καταπνευσθεὶς ἄνωθεν — ὁ καθαρώτατος νοῦς — ὁ λυθεὶς τῶν δεσμῶν, καὶ ἐλευθερωθείς</note> *He who is inspired from above—who has the purest mind—who is loosed from the fetters of this world and has gained his spiritual freedom—he alone can partake of this happiness.*

## XXXV.

### On the righteous man not being given over to total death, but raised by the Word of God. For through the Logos <note>term: The Greek word "Logos" means "Word" or "Reason," referring here to the divine instrument of creation and salvation.</note>, by whom all things were created, God will advance him to be near Himself in heaven.

"When, indeed, he was about to die, he did not pass away and join others as those before him did... but he was translated through the word of the Cause, by whom also the whole universe was created. By the same Word <note>original: Logos</note> He both creates the universe and leads the perfect man up from earthly things to Himself — placing him near Himself."
<note>original Greek: Ἡνικα γουν τελευτᾳν εμελλεν, ουκ εκλειπων προστιθεται, ὡσπερ οἱ προτεροι . . . αλλα δια ῥηματος του αιτιου μετανισταται, δι' οὑ και ὁ συμπας κοσμος εδημιουργειτο — Τῳ αυτῳ Λογῳ και το παν εργαζομενος, και τον τελειον απο των περιγειων αναγων ὡς ἑαυτον¹ — ² ἱδρυσας πλησιον ἑαυτου.</note>

¹ <note>original Latin: De Sacrificiis, V. i. p. 165. l. 7. This refers to Philo's treatise *On the Sacrifices of Abel and Cain*.</note>
² <note>original Latin: Ibid. l. 5.</note>

For when he is delivered to death, he does not cease to exist, nor is he merely added to the count of those who have gone before him. Instead, he is transferred <note>original: "translated"</note> to another state by the Word of that great Cause of all things (the *Logos*), by whom the world was created. For God, by his spoken Word, through which he made all things, will raise the perfect man from the lowly depths <note>original: "dregs"</note> of this world and exalt him near himself; he will place him close to his own person.

### XXXVI.
## THE LOGOS AS HIGH PRIEST <note>original Greek: ΛΟΓΟΣ ΑΡΧΙΕΡΕΥΣ</note>

Regarding the Logos being the true High Priest; regarding his being without sin, and his being anointed with oil.

The world, in which the firstborn Divine Word <note>original Greek: Θειος Λογος (Theios Logos)</note> is also High Priest.¹—For we say that the High Priest is not a man, but the Divine Word, who is free not only from all intentional, but also from all unintentional sins <note>original Greek: παντων ουχ ἑκουσιων μονον, αλλα και ακουσιων αδικηματων</note>...

---

¹ *On Dreams* <note>original Latin title: De Somniis</note>, Volume 1, page 653, line 23.

<summary>This page identifies the Divine Logos as the true, sinless High Priest of the world, drawing parallels between the Gospel of John’s portrayal of Christ and Philo’s philosophical descriptions of the Word.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, High Priest, John 14:6, John 12:26, Philo Judaeus, De Somniis, sinlessness, Divine Word, priesthood</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Logos (the Word) as the universal High Priest, emphasizing his absolute sinlessness and divine nature as described by Philo of Alexandria, and supporting these ideas with New Testament parallels from Hebrews and 1 Peter.</meta>

( 148 )

...free from sin—because his head has been anointed with oil.<note>original Greek: αμετοχον — διοτι την κεφαλην κεχρισται ελαιω.</note>*
It is the world in which the Word <note>term: Logos</note>, God's Firstborn, that great High Priest, resides. And I assert that this High Priest is no man, but the Holy Word of God: who is not capable of either intentional or unintentional sin—and hence his head is anointed with oil.

# XXXVII.
## THE LOGOS AS HIGH PRIEST AND MEDIATOR <note>original Greek: ΛΟΓΟΣ ΑΡΧΙΕΡΕΥΣ ΜΕΘΟΡΙΟΣ (Logos Archierus Methorios)</note>, OR THE LOGOS
### IN HIS MEDIATORIAL CAPACITY.

Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note> mentions the Logos as the Great High Priest and Mediator for the sins of the world.¹ And, speaking of the rebellion of Korah <note>A biblical event in Numbers 16 where Korah led a revolt against Moses and Aaron</note>, he introduces the Logos as saying:

"And I stood in the middle between the Lord and you." <note>original Greek: Καγὼ εισηκειν ανα μεσον Κυριε και ὑμων</note>²

For this role was delegated to him by God the Father:

"so that, by standing as a mediator <note>original Greek: μεθοριος (methorios) – literally "one who stands on the boundary"</note> between both, he might separate the creature from the Creator." <note>original Greek: ινα μεθοριος σας το γενομενον διακρινη τε πεποιηκοτος</note>³

He had said before:

"I also marvel at the Sacred Logos, who ran with breathless haste and intensity to stand in the middle between the dead and the living." <note>original Greek: Θαυμαζω και τον μετα σπεδης απνευσι δραμοντα συντονως ‘Ιερον Λογον, ινα ση μεσος των τεθνηκοτων και των ζωντων.</note>

---

¹ *On Dreams* <note>original Latin: De Somniis</note>, Vol. i. p. 653. line 14.
² *Who is the Heir of Divine Things?* <note>original Latin: Quis Rerum Divin. Hæres.</note> Vol. i. p. 502. line 1.
³ Ibid. p. 501. line 46.

<summary>This page explores Philo of Alexandria's description of the Logos as a boundary-standing mediator and High Priest who intercedes between the Creator and the created world. It specifically cites the Logos’s role during the rebellion of Korah as an example of this intercessory function.</summary>

I cannot help but view with *admiration* the *sacred Logos, pressing forward with such zeal and without stopping, so that he may stand between the dead and the living.* $^1$ The High Priest, who entered the *Holy of Holies* once a year, was a type <note>original: "type"—in this context, a "type" is a person, event, or institution in the Old Testament that is seen as a symbolic foreshadowing of something in the New Testament.</note> of someone greater who was to come. *Philo* describes this sacred apartment as—the very innermost recess of the temple—the holy Sanctuary, into which the High Priest enters once a year on the day of the fast <note>Yom Kippur, or the Day of Atonement</note>, only to offer up incense and to pray, according to the ancestral rites, for a bountiful harvest and for prosperity and peace for all people. $^2$ <note>original Greek: εσωτατω του Ἱερου — αυτα τα αδυτα, εις α απαξ του ενιαυτου ὁ Μεγας Ἱερευς εισερχεται, τη νηστεια λεγομενη, μονον επιθυμιασων, και κατα τα πατρια ευξομενος φοραν αγαθων, ευετηριαν τε και ειρηνην άπασιν ανθρωποις</note> In this account, *Philo* must have been in some respects willfully mistaken. He must have known that the

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Philo of Alexandria’s interpretation of the High Priest's duties, arguing that Philo misrepresented the Mosaic law to the Emperor Caligula by claiming the High Priest prayed for universal peace and prosperity, when the law actually focuses strictly on atonement.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>

The duty <note>original: "office"</note> of the High Priest at this time was to perform an act of atonement. It was ***an everlasting statute to make atonement for the children of Israel for all their sins once a year.***¹ As for any prayers to obtain—*a harvest of good things* <note>original Greek: φοραν των αγαθων</note>—meaning plenty or produce, no such prayers were made; much less were there prayers for the universal peace and happiness of mankind. I do not believe that the word *pray* or *prayer* is to be found in any single law <note>original: "ordinance"</note> of Moses.

Philo therefore misled <note>original: "imposed upon"</note> the emperor Caligula when he made this declaration before him. What he says about the **Logos** <note>The "Word" or divine reason, which the author identifies here with Christ.</note> being the **Intercessor** for humanity and a **Mediator** for sin is true; however, it was the Logos acting in a capacity that Philo could not be brought to acknowledge <note>The author suggests Philo recognized the concept of a divine mediator but failed to recognize Jesus Christ as that mediator.</note>. This entire concept is very accurately described by **St. Paul**, who mentions Christ as both **High Priest** and **Mediator**—a High Priest who has entered the true Holy of Holies, Heaven, once and for all, and who makes intercession for us.

### XXXVIII.

Concerning the six cities of refuge <note>In Mosaic law, these were specific towns where those who had committed accidental manslaughter could claim the right of asylum from blood-avengers.</note>, to which people guilty of accidental homicide were to flee for safety; and regarding their return from exile upon the death of the High Priest.

> *And the Lord spoke to Moses: "You shall designate three cities on this side of the Jordan, and three cities you shall designate in the land of Canaan, which shall be cities of refuge—so that anyone who kills another person unintentionally may flee there. ... And he shall remain in it until the death of the High Priest, who was anointed with the holy oil."* — Numbers 35:10, 14, 25.

### XXX.

Philo's <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Hebrew Scriptures with Greek philosophy through allegorical interpretation.</note> opinion concerning these cities and the death of the High Priest.

alluded to was the divine¹ <term>Logos</term> <note>The "Logos" (from Greek Λόγος) is a philosophical and theological concept meaning "the Word" or "Divine Reason."</note>: and the state of exile was his withdrawing his influence from the soul of man.² We see that he <note>Referring to Philo of Alexandria.</note> came near the mark, but could not attain to it. He accordingly supposes it to consist in the falling away of man; when the divine <term>Logos</term> <note>The "Word" or "Divine Reason," a concept in Greek philosophy and Christian theology.</note>, the great High Priest, withholds his beneficial influence, and man is quite deserted. ¹<note>original: "Ἕως γὰρ ὁ ἱερώτατος οὗτος Λόγος ζῇ καὶ περίεστιν ἐν ψυχῇ" — "For as long as this most holy Logos lives and remains in the soul"</note> *For as long as the divine Logos lives and presides in the human soul, there is no guilt; no appearance of sin: but when he withdraws himself, then commences sin and corruption.* 

But this is the spiritual death of the man, and not the death of the Logos, who could neither suffer, nor die. He is represented by Philo himself as *the Son of God, existing before the angels, before all worlds,* and <term>eternal</term> <note>original: "ἀΐδιος" (*aidios*)</note>—the *Seal of God—whose impression is the eternal Logos* <note>original: "Σφραγὶς Θεοῦ—ἧς ὁ χαρακτὴρ ὁ ἀΐδιος Λόγος" — "Sphragis Theou—hēs ho charaktēr ho aidios Logos"</note>.

## XL.

### The necessity of a Redeemer, and ransom for sin.

Although Philo could not accept the idea of a crucified Messiah, he still acknowledges and adopts most of the beneficial doctrines relating to Christ in his human state. From this, we may learn how very reasonable these doctrines appeared to him. But at the same time, he misapplies them, referring them either to the <term>Logos</term> <note>The "Word" or Divine Reason.</note> in his heavenly state, or else to the supreme Deity, to whom they cannot be applied. We have seen that he speaks of sin and *the propitiation for sin* <note>original: "propitiation" — an act of atonement or a way to appease divine justice.</note>; he also speaks of "the price and ransom for iniquity" <note>original: "λύτρα καὶ σῶστρα" (*lytra kai sōstra*) — ransoms and rewards for deliverance or salvation.</note>—and the means of salvation, by which spiritual freedom is to be obtained here, and

<summary>The author argues that while Philo of Alexandria utilized high theological concepts regarding redemption and a ransom for sin, he failed to correctly apply them to the Messiah, instead misattributing them to the immortal Logos or God Himself.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Logos, Redeemer, Propitiation, Ransom, Salvation, Messiah, Atonement</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Philo of Alexandria’s theology, specifically how he reconciles the concept of spiritual freedom with the sacrificial requirements of the Mosaic Law.</meta>

everlasting life hereafter. But he supposes these blessings to arise from acts and religious laws <note>original: "ordinances"</note> which were not adequate—such as the sin-offerings and other sacrifices <note>original: "oblations"</note> that were presented in the Temple, but were not sufficient for that great purpose. He sometimes seems to acknowledge that these sacrifices were symbolic types <term>types</term> <note>In theology, a "type" is a person, event, or institution in the Old Testament that is seen as foreshadowing something in the New Testament.</note>, and that the High Priest himself, who made intercession <note>original: "intercession" — the act of pleading or mediating on behalf of others before God</note>, was merely a representative of a greater Personage from whom these blessings were to be derived. At other times, he thinks that mere repentance without satisfaction <note>original: "satisfaction" — the payment of a penalty to satisfy the demands of divine justice</note> is sufficient: ¹ This is forgiveness; this is freedom <note>original: "Τοῦτ᾽ ἔστιν ἄφεσις, τοῦτ᾽ ἐλευθερία"</note>. ² This affliction is an atonement <note>original: "Ἡ κάκωσις αὕτη ἱλασμός ἐστι"</note>. *To repent affords forgiveness <note>original: "remission"</note> of sins. Humility produces atonement <note>original: "propitiation"</note>.* Still, he acknowledges that there must additionally be some sacrifices made and some victims offered to divine justice. He bases our reconciliation with the offended Deity on these, as well as on the righteousness <note>original: "rectitude"</note> of the Priests and Levites by whom the offerings were made.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Philo of Alexandria’s theology, specifically his view that while the Mosaic sacrifices and the role of the Levites were necessary, they were ultimately symbolic and pointed toward a greater need for a true Mediator.</meta>

He calls these offerings atonement <note>original: "ἱλασμὸς"</note> and labels the altar the place of atonement <note>original: "ἱλαστήριον"</note>, or *the seat of mercy*, and propitiation <term>propitiation</term> <note>Propitiation refers to an action meant to regain the favor of a deity or to make amends for a wrongdoing.</note>. He also mentions the Levites as a ransom for all others <note>original: "Λυτρα των αλλων ἁπάντων"</note>—*a propitiation for all the people.* Both repentance and offerings were required, and the ministry of the Priests was necessary; however, these were only symbolic <note>original: "figurative"</note> and, by themselves, could not achieve atonement and reconciliation. Something of greater importance was missing.

In pursuing this line of thought, Philo seems to come close to the truth; however, his strong prejudices were a constant obstacle and would not allow him to accept it in its full force. Yet he sometimes makes remarkable concessions, as can be seen in many passages I have quoted from him, and especially in the following example. He is speaking about the necessity of a Mediator, whom everyone in the service of God should seek.

everlasting life hereafter. But he believes these blessings arise from religious acts and laws <note>original: "ordinances"</note> which were not sufficient for the task—such as the sin-offerings and other religious gifts <note>original: "oblations"</note> that were presented in the Temple, but were not enough to achieve that great purpose. He sometimes seems to acknowledge that these offerings were symbolic types <term>types</term> <note>In this context, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament believed to foreshadow a future reality, specifically Christ.</note>, and that the High Priest himself, who acted as a mediator <note>original: "made intercession"</note>, was merely a representative of a greater Person from whom these blessings were truly derived. At other times, he thinks that simple repentance without any payment for sin <note>original: "satisfaction"</note> is enough: ¹ "This is forgiveness, this is freedom" <note>original: "Τῦτ’ εϲιν αφεσις, τῦτ’ ελευθερια"</note>. ² "This self-abasement is itself an atonement" <note>original: "Ἡ κακωϲις αυτη ιλαϲμος εϲι"</note>. *To repent provides forgiveness of sins. Humility results in atonement.* Still, he acknowledges that there must additionally be some offerings made and some victims sacrificed to satisfy divine justice. He bases our reconciliation with the offended God <note>original: "Deity"</note> on these sacrifices, as well as on the moral uprightness <note>original: "rectitude"</note> of the Priests and Levites by whom the offerings were made.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Philo of Alexandria’s views on atonement. It explores his use of sacrificial language—terms like propitiation and ransom—and his realization that while the Mosaic rituals were necessary, they were ultimately symbolic "types" pointing toward a greater, missing reality.</meta>

He calls <note>original: "styles"</note> these offerings a means of atonement <note>original: "ἱλασμὸς" (hilasmos)</note>; and he calls the altar $^1$ the place of atonement <note>original: "ἱλαστήριον" (hilastērion)</note>, or *the seat of mercy*, and propitiation <term>propitiation</term> <note>An act meant to regain the favor of a deity or make amends for a wrong.</note>. He also mentions the Levites as $^2$ "a ransom for all the others" <note>original: "Λύτρα τῶν άλλων ἁπάντων" (Lutra tōn allōn hapantōn)</note>—*a propitiation for all the people*. Both repentance and offerings were required, and the ministry of the priests was necessary; but they were only symbolic <note>original: "figurative"</note>, and on their own could not achieve <note>original: "effect"</note> atonement and reconciliation. Something of greater importance was missing.

In pursuing <note>original: "prosecuting"</note> his opinion in this way, Philo seems to come close to the truth; however, his strong prejudices were a constant obstacle and would not allow him to accept it fully <note>original: "in full force"</note>. Yet he sometimes makes remarkable <note>original: "wonderful"</note> concessions, as can be seen in many extracts I have presented from his work, and especially in the following example. He is speaking about the necessity of a Mediator, to whom everyone in the service of God should turn <note>original: "apply"</note>.

<summary>This page discusses Philo's use of sacrificial terminology, such as "atonement" and "mercy seat," noting that he viewed Jewish rituals as symbolic necessities that nonetheless lacked the power to achieve true reconciliation on their own. "Send heads to the son of Cronus, and to the Father send men." <note>original Greek: "Και κεφαλας Κρονιδῃ, και τῳ Πατρι πεμπετε φωτα." The term "φωτα" (phota) is an archaic Greek word for "men" or "mortals."</note>

Through a misunderstanding, this became the basis for human sacrifices, a custom that Philo himself mentions. However, he believes that all true atonement <term>expiation</term> <note>The act of making amends or reparation for guilt or wrongdoing.</note> is centered within the grounds of the Jewish Temple and is completed through its rituals and offerings, by the intercession of the High Priest as the representative of the Word <term>Logos</term> <note>A Greek term meaning "Word" or "Reason," used by Philo to describe a divine intermediary, but interpreted by Christians as the pre-existent Christ.</note>. He paid no attention to Christ the Redeemer—the Word of God in human form. Neither the Levite, nor the High Priest of the Levites, could make atonement for the sins of the world. "For it is impossible for the blood of bulls and goats to take away sins." ² The blood of bulls and goats had no such effectiveness <note>original: "efficacy"</note>. They were types <term>types</term> <note>In historical theology, a "type" is a person, event, or ritual in the Old Testament seen as a symbolic foreshadowing of something in the New Testament.</note> of a greater offering to be one day made; and God himself had shown their insufficiency, and that there was no real dependence upon them.

*To what purpose is the multitude of your sacrifices to me, says the Lord. I am full of the burnt-offerings of rams, and the fat of fed beasts. I delight not in the blood of bullocks, or of lambs, or of he-goats: bring no more vain <term>vain</term> <note>Meaning empty, useless, or without spiritual value.</note> offerings.* ³

## XLII.

### PHILO’S NOTION OF THE RETURN OF THE DISPERSED JEWS.

He could not be persuaded that this great Personage had appeared and been rejected by the Jews. He shared in their blindness and was a silent supporter <note>original: "tacit abettor"</note> of their crime. Instead of fearing <note>original: "apprehending"</note> any evil that would follow, he anticipates much happiness. For the Scripture <note>term: "the Scripture" (to logion); literally 'the oracle' or 'holy word'</note> says that the Tabernacle was established in the midst of our impurity, so that we might have a means of being purified, washing and rinsing away the things that defile our wretched and disgraceful life. <note>original Greek: Βουληθεις μεντοι (ὁ Θεος) και της Θειας αρετης απ' Ουρανου την εικονα επι γην καταπεμψαι δι' ελεον του γενους ἡμων, ἱνα μη ατυχησῃ της αμεινονος μοιρας, συμβολικως την ἱεραν Σκηνὴν και τα εν αυτῃ κατασκευαζει, σοφιας απεικονισμα και μιμημα. ( 174 )

->*wisdom.*<- For he tells us that he placed this Tabernacle, the seat of his oracle, in the midst of our impurities, so that we might have a means <note>original: "wherewithal"</note> to cleanse ourselves and wash away all the filth and pollution of our wretched and disgraceful existence <note>original: "ignoble being"</note>.

Our weaknesses <note>original: "infirmities"</note> are very accurately described by Philo, as is the necessity of purification. But this was not to be achieved by a worldly Tabernacle, but by a great High Priest, whom Philo has noted elsewhere, and who has entered once and for all into a heavenly Tabernacle, of which this earthly one was only an emblem. The High Priest was Christ himself—*a minister of the sanctuary, and of the true Tabernacle, which the Lord pitched, and not man* <note>original Greek: των ἁγιων λειτουργος, και της σκηνης της αληθινης, ἡν επηξεν ὁ Κυριος, και ουκ ανθρωπος</note>.

### XLVI.

## On the nature of Faith and its very beneficial <note>original: "salutary"</note> consequences according to Philo.

In the Old Testament, we find that belief in God and trust in His providence, along with submission to His divine will, are continually recommended. But the duty of Faith and the blessings that accompany it were never described and emphasized as we find them later in the writings of the Evangelists and Apostles. These excellent people have taught us what faith consists of and the virtues that should accompany it, as well as the peace and comfort it brings in this life and the everlasting happiness it will produce in the next. This happiness depends on our faith in Christ, **THE WORD OF GOD**; it is explicitly founded upon Him. However, concerning this, we are granted no certain light from either the

## 46.

### On the nature of faith and its very beneficial <note>original: "salutary"</note> consequences according to Philo.

In the Old Testament, we find a belief in God and a trust in His providence, along with a submission to His divine will, continually recommended. But the duty of faith and the blessings which accompany it were never described and enforced as we find them to have been later by the Evangelists and Apostles. These excellent people have taught us what faith consists of and the virtues that should accompany it; the peace and comfort that attend it in this life; and the everlasting happiness which it will produce hereafter. This happiness depends on our faith in Christ, THE WORD OF GOD; it is expressly founded upon Him. But concerning this, we have no certain <note>original: "sure"</note> light provided <note>original: "afforded"</note> either from the

<vocab>Faith, Philo, Old Testament, Christ, Word of God, providence</vocab>

From the later sacred writers, we learn that without faith, and specifically faith in Christ—it is impossible to please God <note>original: *αδυνατον ευαρεστησαι (τω Θεω)*</note>.¹ By faith we are justified <note>meaning: declared righteous in the eyes of God</note>;² by faith we are sanctified <note>meaning: made holy</note>;³ by faith we are made wise enough to reach salvation;⁴ through faith we are saved;⁵ and the propitiation <note>meaning: the act of regaining God’s favor or atonement</note> for our sins is obtained through faith.⁶ By this faith in Christ, the disciples had the power to cast out demons <note>original: *εξουσιαν εκβαλλειν τα δαιμονια*</note>.⁷ “Lord, even the demons are subject to us in your name” <note>original: *Κυριε, και τα δαιμονια ὑποτασσεται ἡμιν εν τω ονοματι σου.*</note>.⁸ They were likewise enabled to improve themselves in all that was good, and to preserve themselves holy, blameless, and irreproachable <note>original: *ἁγιους, και αμωμους, και ανεγκλητους*</note>, if they remained well established <note>original: *τη πιστει τεθεμελιωμενοι*</note> in the faith—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the philosopher Philo's descriptions of faith mirror Christian doctrine, specifically examining a passage where he defines faith as the ultimate good and a defense against demonic forces.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>
<sig>M 4</sig>

The only true and secure good is faith toward God. It is the comfort of life, the fulfillment of beneficial hopes, the absence of evils, and the abundance of good things. It is the rejection of demonic influence <gloss>1</gloss>, the knowledge of piety, the inheritance of happiness, and the improvement of the soul in all things—resting upon the Cause of all things, who is able to do everything and wills only what is best. <note>original Greek: Μονον εν αψευδες και βεβαιον αγαθον ἡ προς τον Θεον πισις. παρηγορημα βιου, πληρωμα χρησων ελπιδων. αφορια μεν κακων, αγαθων δε φορα. κακοδαιμονιας απογνωσις, ευσεβειας γνωσις. ευδαιμονιας κληρος, ψυχης εν άπασι βελτιωσις, επηρεισμενης τῳ παντων αιτιῳ, και δυναμενῳ μεν παντα, βουλομενῳ δε τα αριστα.</note>

The only sure and well-founded blessing in which we can trust is faith in God. It is the comfort of life and encompasses every beneficial hope. It is the reduction of evil and productive of all good: the ruin of demonic influence,

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Some interpret *kakodaimonia* <note>original Greek: κακοδαιμονια</note> as *unhappiness*, and the author sometimes uses it with that meaning. But as it is contrasted here with the *knowledge of piety* <note>original Greek: ευσεβειας γνωσις</note>, I believe that in this passage it refers to foul illusions, and particularly to the influence of demons.

Aristophanes makes a character say to another: "Who would not think that this was madness, or rather a diabolical frenzy?" <note>original Greek: Τις αν ουκ ηγοιτ’ ειναι μανιαν; κακοδαιμονιαν γε ετι μαλλον;</note>
->Plutus, line 501.<-

In another passage, a man humorously says: "You did not steal it; you only snatched it." The other answers: "You are frantic"—or in English, "The devil is in you." <note>original Greek: κακοδαιμονεις</note>
->Plutus, line 372.<-

The only true and secure good is faith toward God. It is the comfort of life, the fulfillment of good hopes, the absence of evils, and the abundance of goods. It is the rejection of misery <note>original Greek: κακοδαιμονιας</note>, the knowledge of piety, and the inheritance of happiness. It is the improvement of the soul in all things, as it rests upon the Cause of all things, who is able to do all things and wills what is best. <note>original Greek: Μονον εν αψευδες και βεβαιον αγαθον η προς τον Θεον πισις. παρηγορημα βιου, πληρωμα χρηστων ελπιδων. αφορια μεν κακων, αγαθων δε φορα. κακοδαιμονιας απογνωσις, ευσεβειας γνωσις. ευδαιμονιας κληρος, ψυχης εν απασι βελτιωσις, επηρεισμενης τω παντων αιτιω, και δυναμενω μεν παντα, βουλομενω δε τα αριστα.</note>

*The only sure and well-founded blessing to which we can trust is faith in God. It is the comfort of life and comprehends every salutary hope. It is the diminution of evil and productive of all good: the ruin of demonic influence,*

## XLVII.

### On Repentance as a Result of Faith.

After hope, repentance for sins holds the second place in order.² <note>original Greek: Δευτεραν δ’ εχει ταξιν, μετα την ελπιδα, ἡ επι τοις ἁμαρτανομενοις μετανοια.</note>

*The next duty in order after faith is repentance for our sins.*

After the victory of hope, a second contest follows, in which repentance struggles.³ <note>original Greek: Μετα δε την ελπιδος νικην αγων δευτερος εσιν, εν ᾧ μετανοια αγωνιζεται.</note>

*When we have gained hope, the next conflict in which we are engaged is to establish repentance.*
