## XLVIII.

### Concerning Righteousness and good works, the result of repentance. <note>original: "Of Righteouſnefs and good works, the conſequence of repentance."</note>

After the struggles of repentance, the third prize is to maintain righteousness. <note>original Greek: Μετα δε τας της μετανοιας αγωνας τριτα αθλα τιθεται Δικαιοσυνης.</note>¹

*After repentance, the third conflict is to maintain righteousness.*

After hope <note>The author's English translation uses "faith," but the original Greek "ελπιδα" translates more literally as "hope."</note> comes repentance and improvement; as a result, we read of people who are converted from a bad life to a better one. <note>original Greek: Μετα την ελπιδα δευτεραν εχει ταξιν μετανοια και βελτιωσις˙ οθεν εξης αναγραφει τον απο χειρονος βιου προς τον αμεινονα μεταβαλοντα.</note>²

*After faith comes repentance and improvement; in consequence of which we read of persons, who from a bad life are converted to a better.*

### XLIX.

Concerning the mercies of God the Savior, and of humanity's relationship and kinship <note>original: "affinity"</note> to the divine Word, through the goodness of God, upon their repentance, good deeds, and confession of their sins.

If, however, they are moved by shame and change with their whole soul, blaming themselves for their wandering, and declaring and confessing all the sins they have committed against themselves—first with a mind purified for the sake of a conscience that is truthful and without deceit <note>original: "ανυπȣλον" - literally "without a hidden wound," meaning sincere or healthy</note>, and then with the tongue, to encourage the improvement of those who hear them—they will find favor from God the Savior and the Merciful One. He has provided a special and supreme gift to the human race: kinship with His Word, which served as the archetype <note>original: "αρχετυπον"</note> from which the human mind was created. <note>original Greek: Εαν μεντοι ....... καταιδεϲθεντεϲ ὁλῃ ψυχῃ μεταβαλωϲι, κακιϲαντεϲ μεν αυτȣϲ τηϲ πλανηϲ, εξαγορευϲαντεϲ δε και ὁμολογηϲαντεϲ ὁϲα ἡμαρτον καθ' αυτȣϲ, διανοια κεκαθαρμενῃ το πρωτον ειϲ το τȣ ϲυνειδοτοϲ αψευδεϲ και ανυπȣλον, επειτα και γλωττῃ, προϲ βελτιωϲιν των ακȣοντων, ευμενειαϲ τευξονται τηϲ εκ τȣ Σωτηροϲ και ἱλεω Θεȣ, τῳ γενει των ανθρωπων εξαιρετον παραϲχομενȣ και μεγιϲτην δωρεαν, την προϲ τον αυτȣ Λογον ϲυγγενειαν, αφ' ἡϲ καθαπερ αρχετυπον γεγονεν ὁ ανθρωπειοϲ νȣϲ.</note> <sup>I</sup>

*If then they have from their very souls a just contrition <note>original: "just contrition" refers to a sincere feeling of remorse or guilt for past sins</note>, and are changed, and have humbled themselves for their past errors, acknowledging...* <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the word "acknowledging" is derived from the catchword "knowledging" provided in the metadata.</note>

# L.

### Man the Temple of God.

Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note> speaks of people who are truly virtuous and holy as being the temples of God.

*The prophet says that God walks in the mind <note>original: "this (the mind)"</note> as if in a palace.*¹ <note>original Greek: Οὗτος (ὁ νους), ᾧ φησιν ὁ προφητης του Θεου εμπεριπατειν, οἱα βασιλειῳ.</note>

*For the palace and temple of the Great Being <note>original: "the Being" or "Self-existing Deity"</note> is the intellectual mind of a wise person.*² <note>original Greek: Και γαρ εστι τῳ Οντι βασιλειον και οικος Θεου, σοφου διανοια.</note>

*God... looking down, could find no more worthy temple on earth than the rational mind.*³ <note>original Greek: Ὁ Θεος .... νευων αξιοπρεπεστερον επι γης ουχ εὑρε λογισμου.</note>

<meta>catchword: Two <note>original Greek: Δυο</note></meta>

---

¹ *On Rewards and Punishments* <note>original Latin: De Præmiis et Pœnis</note>, Vol. ii. p. 428. l. 10.

² Ibid. l. 12.

³ *On Nobility* <note>original Latin: De Nobilitate</note>, Vol. ii. p. 437. l. 11.

<summary>This page cites the philosopher Philo of Alexandria to argue that the human intellect and the souls of virtuous people serve as the true earthly temples and palaces of God.</summary>

# LI.

## His account of the first created Man.

Philo mentions man as being formed after the "image of God" <note>original Greek: κατ’ εικονα Θεου</note>; and that he was also to be considered the image of the "archetypal and causative Word" <note>original Greek: αρχετυπον του αιτιου Λογου; the "Word" here refers to the Logos</note>.¹

He names the first man Adam and says that he was placed by God in Paradise. He describes Adam as being in a state of perfection and freedom—"For God made him unrestrained and free" <note>original Greek: Εἰργάσατο γὰρ αὐτὸν (ὁ Θεὸς) ἄφετον καὶ ἐλεύθερον</note>²—*For God created him to be free without control, in a state of full liberty.* But he disobeyed and was expelled, and so he forfeited his happiness.³ This is where sin began, and a curse was subsequently passed down to his descendants. He fell from his original radiance <note>original: "brightness"</note> and lost that likeness of the Deity who had created him, which he had previously held.

³ *Allegorical Interpretation of the Laws* <note>original Latin: De Legum Allegoriarum</note>, Vol. 1, p. 61, line 38; p. 63, line 10.

# LI.

## His account of the first created man.

Philo mentions man as being formed after the image of God—*according to the image of God* <note>original Greek: κατ’ εικονα Θεου</note>; and that he was also to be esteemed the image of the Logos—*an archetype of the causative Word* <note>original Greek: αρχετυπον του αιτιου Λογου</note>.¹

He refers to the first man as Adam and says that he was placed by God in Paradise; and that he was in a state of perfection and freedom—*For God created him to be free and independent* <note>original Greek: Ειργασατο γαρ αυτον (ὁ Θεος) αφετον και ελευθερον</note>²—*For God created him to be at large without control, in a state of full liberty.* But he disobeyed and was expelled, and forfeited his happiness.³ Here sin began, and a curse was consequently placed upon his descendants <note>original: "posterity"</note>. He fell from his original brightness and lost that likeness of the Deity who formed him, which he had previously held.

³ *Allegories of the Sacred Laws* <note>original Latin: De Legum Allegoriarum</note>, Vol. 1, p. 61, line 38; p. 63, line 10.

> "Therefore, he derived no benefit from his noble birth, having brought a curse upon himself and becoming the source of misery for all those who came after him." <note>original Greek: Τοιγαροῦν οὐκ ἀπώνατο ἀπὸ τῆς λαμπρᾶς εὐγενείας, ἐπάρατος γενόμενος, καὶ τοῖς μετ’ αὐτὸν ἀρχὴ κακοδαιμονίας. The author notes that the editor properly reads "οὐκ" (not) instead of "εκ".</note>¹

Here we have a faithful account of the fall of man, original sin, and its fatal consequences for the world—"For these reasons, he was justly doomed to a mortal life instead of an immortal one, and was made a perishable being." <note>original Greek: ἐφ’ οἷς εἰκότως θνητὸν ἀντ’ ἀθάνατου βίον ἀνθυπηλλάξατο</note>²

After Philo has mentioned this inherent evil in the human constitution, one would expect him to point out some remedy—some proper atonement—by which God’s favor might be regained and man might be justified in His sight. But, as we have seen, his recourse is only to confession, repentance, and the blood of victims <note>animal sacrifices</note>, which can never, of

--- Page 229 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that external rituals and animal sacrifices are insufficient for the forgiveness of sins, contrasting Philo’s Jewish perspective with the Christian doctrine of atonement through Christ.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>
<sig>N 2</sig>

themselves be an adequate compensation for guilt. When a man has risen in rebellion against his prince, has broken the most beneficial <note>original: "salutary"</note> laws, and been guilty of theft, murder, and accumulated wickedness, he may say that he is sorry for it and wish it had not been done. He may even present a bull or a goat as compensation for the people he has robbed or killed. But this will not suffice before an earthly presiding officer <note>original: "president," used here to mean a high-ranking judge or governor</note>; much less will it suffice before the great Judge of the world, the God of all justice and truth. 

Something more was therefore required to achieve pardon and atonement. Philo, given his circumstances, could not help but know the central tenet of the Christian creed—*Salvation through Christ*—and that He was *the propitiation <note>the means of removing guilt or gaining favor</note> for sin*. He should also have known that all the sacrificial offerings for atonement appointed by the Jewish Law were ineffective; they were only symbols <note>original: "figurative"</note> of the great atonement to come. His own prophets had told him so, and their words had been fulfilled. However, he has acknowledged some truths of great importance which are well worth our consideration.

<meta>catchword: LII. Of</meta>

### LII.
## Of the Holy Spirit.

We have seen that Philo held a very high opinion of the Logos, or Word of God, and fell very little short of the truth. Whether he held the third person, the Spirit of God, in the same regard <note>original: "esteem"</note> and had as accurate <note>original: "just"</note> an idea of it, requires some consideration. In his account of the creation, where it is said that the Spirit of God moved upon the waters, he makes it exist only as long as <note>original: "coeval with"</note> light and describes it as nothing more than the element of air.<sup>1</sup> But in other places, he provides a very different description. For when he speaks of this divine Spirit resting upon the seventy elders,<sup>2</sup> he describes it as infinite and indivisible, and calls it the "spirit of all wisdom" <note>original: "pansophon Pneuma"</note>.<sup>3</sup> He then continues and says,

<sup>2</sup> Numbers 11:17.
<sup>3</sup> *On the Giants* <note>original: "De Gigantibus"</note>, Vol. i. p. 266. l. 2.

Now the Spirit that is upon him is the wise, the divine, the indivisible, the inseparable, the excellent, and that which is entirely and completely filled throughout all things. While it benefits others, it is not harmed; though shared with another, it does not decrease in its understanding, knowledge, and wisdom <note>original: Νυν δε το επ’ αυτω Πνευμα εστι το σοφον, το Θειον, το ατμητον, το αδιαιρετον, το αστειον, το παντη δι’ ολων εκπεπληρωμενον· οπερ ωφελουν, ου βλαπτεται, μεταδοθεν ετερω, ουδ’ αυ προστεθεν ελαττουται την συνεσιν, και επιστημην, και σοφιαν.</note>.

Therefore, while the divine Spirit can reside in the soul, it is impossible for it to remain there permanently, as we have said <note>original: Διο δη πνευμα Θειον μενειν μεν δυνατον εν ψυχη, καταμενειν δε αδυνατον, ως ειπομεν</note>—

*Now this Spirit of God is a being of wisdom and of a divine nature, indivisible, inseparable, beautiful, and in every respect completely whole. When it benefits others, it is not impaired; when given to another, it receives no loss in perception, knowledge, or wisdom. For this reason, this divine Spirit, though it may reside in the human soul, cannot remain there continually, as I have mentioned.* He gives a reason for the Holy Spirit not always remaining with men, on account of their impurity—because they are fleshly, the divine Spirit is unable to remain <note>original: δια το ειναι αυτους σαρκας, μη δυνασθαι το θειον Πνευμα καταμειναι</note>—*The divine Spirit cannot dwell with them always, because they are carnal <term>carnal: focusing on physical or worldly desires rather than spiritual ones</term>.* But the

The most detailed description of the Holy Spirit is found in his observations on the words of Moses.¹ An account is given there of three Angels appearing to Abraham, which Philo mentions as the sacred **Trias** <note>original: "Trias"; a Greek term for a group of three, often used to describe the Trinity.</note>. He describes the great reverence of the Patriarch Abraham at the sight of them:

*For indeed Abraham, coming with all haste, speed, and eagerness, gave instructions to Virtue (Sarah)... when God, escorted by two of the highest Powers—namely Sovereignty and Goodness—Himself being the one in the middle, produced three distinct impressions in the soul of Abraham, who beheld them.* <note>original: "Και γαρ Αβρααμ, μετα σπουδης και ταχους και προθυμιας πασης ελθων, παρακελευεται τη αρετη Σαραι .... ἡνικα ὁ Θεος δορυφορουμενος ὑπο δυειν των ανωτατω Δυναμεων, αρχης τε αυ και αγαθοτητος, εἰς ων ὁ μεσος, τριττας φαντασιας ενειργαζετο τη ὁρατικη ψυχη (τε Αβρααμ)"</note>—*For it was with great earnestness, speed, and zeal that Abraham went and gave directions to his wife Sarah—when God, escorted on each side by two Personages from on high, whose attributes were Power and Goodness (the Divinity in the middle being in union with the other two), impressed a threefold appearance upon the soul of Abraham, who beheld them.*²

He has, to some degree, obscured these truths with his <term>sophistry</term> <note>sophistry: clever but potentially misleading or overly complex philosophical reasoning</note>, which I will omit.

His opinion is nonetheless clear: the representation of these divine personages who attended the Deity consisted of two powers from heaven, whom he distinguishes by their authority and dominion as well as by their goodness. He further adds—"being the one in the middle" <note>original: "εις ων ὁ μεσος"</note>. By this, it appears manifest to me that he means the ¹ unity of the third with the two preceding. And although he seems to grant supremacy to God, yet he speaks of all three as "uncircumscribed" <note>original: "απεριγραφοι"</note>. By this, he means *unlimited* and *infinite*, and consequently *not to be restricted*. He writes, "none of these can be measured in any way; for God is uncircumscribed, and uncircumscribed [also are...]" <note>original: "ὡν ἑκάστη μεμετρηται μεν ουδαμως · απεριγραφος γαρ ὁ Θεος, απεριγραφοι [και]"</note>
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

---

¹ Otherwise, to say that there were three persons and that the one in the middle was one of them would appear pointless and unnecessary. His meaning can be understood from a previously quoted passage concerning the Logos <note>Logos: The "Word" or divine reason, a central concept in Philo’s philosophy often equated with the Son in Christian theology.</note>. 

"But the divine Logos, being above these... himself being the image of God, the eldest of all intellectual things, and the closest—with no intervening distance—to the only one who truly exists, is firmly established." <note>original: "‘O δ’ ὑπερανω τουτων Λογος θειος . . . . αυτος εικων ὑπαρχων Θεου, των νοητων ἀπαξαπαντων ὁ πρεσβυτατος, ὁ εγγυτατω, μηδενος οντος μεθοριου διαστηματος, του μονου, ὁ εστιν αψευδως, αφιδρυμενος."</note> *On Flight and Finding* <note>original: "De Profugis"</note>, Volume 1, page 561, line 16. *For the divine Logos, being the very image of God, is above all other intellectual beings whatever. And he is placed the nearest, without the slightest interval, to that great Monad* <note>Monad: A term for the "One" or the ultimate unity of God.</note> *who can only be said to truly exist and be self-existing.*

"...and His Powers." <note>original: "καὶ αἱ Δυνάμεις αὐτοῦ"</note> ^1 — Philo adds that the whole matter was a mystery, which was not to be treated lightly: "And having been initiated into the perfect rites, do not blurt out the divine mysteries to anyone; but treasure them up and, keeping silence, guard them in secret." <note>original: "Καὶ τῶν τελείων μύσις γενομένη τελετῶν, μηδενὶ προχείρως ἐκλάλει τὰ θεῖα μυστήρια· ταμιευομένη δ’ αὐτά, καὶ ἐχεμυθοῦσα, ἐν ἀπορρήτῳ φυλάττῃ"</note> *For when a person has been, as it were* ^2 *initiated and has partaken of these extraordinary mysteries, he should not be too forward to disclose such sacred articles; but like a good Steward, he should preserve them in silence and conceal them among those things which ought not to be divulged.* ^3 

To this latter part, I cannot by any means subscribe. Whatever divine Truth is afforded to us, we must accept it and bear witness to it before the world. Although it may contain something above human conception, it must still be admitted if it is delivered from an undoubted authority; otherwise, we act contrary to reason and to general practice. For we allow thousands of things,

...for which we cannot account, yet we act as if their properties were well known. We may therefore safely proclaim our faith and maintain the doctrine provided, even though it may in some respects be beyond our understanding <note>original: "apprehension"</note>.

### CONCLUSION.

If we then accept these doctrines of Philo, and overlook his prejudices and misinterpretations <note>original: "misapplications"</note>, we shall find that some wonderful truths are provided. These could not have been borrowed from his fellow Jews; for whatever knowledge they had of these mysteries, it was by no means equal to the information <note>original: "intelligence"</note> which he has given. This must have been obtained from the source to which I have attributed it—from the fountain of all truth, the Gospel, and from those excellent people, the immediate disciples of Christ, in whose time he lived. It came particularly from those by whom some of the first churches were founded, and most especially from

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>The author concludes that Philo’s sophisticated theological insights were too advanced to have originated in contemporary Jewish thought alone; instead, he argues they must have been derived from the Gospel and early Christian disciples.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Gospel influence, early Church, divine mysteries, Christian origins, Jewish theology</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that Philo of Alexandria's writings reflect the influence of early Christian teaching, specifically suggesting that his terminology mirrors that of the Apostles and the church founded by St. Mark in Alexandria.</meta>

( 202 )

the founder of the church of Alexandria, where he lived. I must therefore repeat what cannot be emphasized enough: that in his writings, we read the views of the earliest Christians and of the Apostles themselves.

Where else could he have obtained so many terms that bear such a close resemblance to the expressions and doctrines found in the Apostolic writings? These include the Son of God <note>original Greek: Υιος Θεου</note>, the First-born Word <note>original Greek: Λογος πρωτογονος</note>, the most ancient <note>original Greek: πρεσβυτατος</note>, the eternal <note>original Greek: αϊδιος</note>, the Word as High Priest <note>original Greek: Λογος Αρχιερευς</note>, the mediator <note>original Greek: μεσος</note>, the boundary <note>original Greek: μεθοριος</note>, the petitioner for the mortal <note>original Greek: ἱκετης τε θνητου</note>, the creator <note>original Greek: δημιουργος</note>, the Shepherd of the holy flock <note>original Greek: Ποιμην της ἱερας αγελης</note>, the Viceroy of God <note>original Greek: Ὑπαρχος Θεου</note>, the seal <note>original Greek: σφραγις</note>, the image of God <note>original Greek: εικων Θεου</note>, the light <note>original Greek: φως</note>, the spirit of God <note>original Greek: πνευμα Θεου</note>, and the all-wise spirit <note>original Greek: πνευμα πανσοφον</note>. We read further concerning Redemption and—*the price and ransom for the soul* <note>original Greek: λυτρα και σωστρα</note>—eternal life instead of death <note>original Greek: αντι θανατου ζωην αϊδιον</note>, and the idea that the mind of man is the temple of God <note>original Greek: νυς [νους] ανθρωπου ναος Θεου</note>. 

Other equally significant examples could be added to these. Few of them are to be found in the Greek Version <note>The Septuagint, the early Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> or in any Jewish doctrines—at least not in the sense <note>original: "acceptation"</note> used here. They were obtained either from conversations with or from the writings of the first Christians, or rather from both.

# REGARDING THE MANNA, OR FOOD FROM HEAVEN

<margin>* Exodus 16:15.</margin>
The account Philo gives of the manna mentioned by Moses is extraordinary. Our Savior, the Word of God, took notice of it as a "type" <note>a symbolic foreshadowing</note> of himself and tried to explain its hidden meaning to the Jews: *"I am that bread of life. Your fathers ate manna in the wilderness and are dead. This is the bread which comes down from heaven, so that a person*

<summary>This page concludes a list of Philo's theological doctrines, including his views on the Holy Spirit and human virtue, and begins an analysis of manna as a symbolic type of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, manna, Moses, Word of God, Logos, Holy Spirit, Triad, Exodus, bread of life, typology</keywords>

--- Page 243 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of manna as a symbolic type of Christ, drawing parallels between the Gospel of John and the writings of Philo of Alexandria regarding the "Logos" or Divine Word as spiritual nourishment.</meta>

( 207 )

...may eat of it and not die. I am the living bread which came down from heaven. If anyone eats of this bread, he will live forever; and the bread that I will give is my flesh, which I will give for the life of the world.¹

Philo speaks of it in the same mysterious but significant manner: "This is the bread—the nourishment which God appointed to be offered to the soul—His own utterance and His own Word." <note>original Greek: Ουτος εστιν ὁ αρτος, ἡ τροφη, ἡν εδωκεν ὁ Θεος τη ψυχη προσενεγκασθαι, το ἑαυτου ῥημα, και τον ἑαυτου Λογον</note>²

"For man indeed lifts his eyes toward the heavens, looking upon the manna—the Divine Word—the heavenly and incorruptible nourishment of the soul that seeks after God." <note>original Greek: Ὁ μεν γαρ (ανθρωπος) τας οψεις ανατεινει προς αιθερα, αφορων το Μαννα, τον Θειον Λογον, την ουρανιον φιλοθεαμονος ψυχης αφθαρτον τροφην. The term "philotheamonos" specifically refers to a soul that loves the contemplation of divine things.</note>³

### OBSERVATIONS UPON THE OPINION OF PHILO.

We find that Philo explains the meaning <note>original: "purport"</note> of this heavenly Manna by saying it was Bread—"This is the bread that is food" <note>original Greek: Ὁυτος εστιν ὁ αρτος τροφη (Houtos estin ho artos trophē)</note>. And this bread, he says, is that divine food which God has sent for the nourishment of the human soul, specifically—"his own utterance and his own Word" <note>original Greek: το ἑαυτου ῥημα, και τον ἑαυτου Λογον (to heautou rēma, kai ton heautou Logon)</note>—*his divine doctrine and his* Logos, *or Word*.

It is represented as "the soul’s incorruptible food" <note>original Greek: ψυχης την αφθαρτον τροφην (psychēs tēn aphtharton trophēn)</note>, *the incorruptible food of the soul*; which "the Divine Word distributes to all equally" <note>original Greek: Λογος Θειος διανεμει πασι εξ ισου (Logos Theios dianemei pasi ex isou)</note>—*the heavenly Logos distributes impartially to all*. In another place, he tells us in a similar way that it was not only a doctrine but also a person who was alluded to under this symbol of bread and heavenly food—"Manna... the most ancient of beings, the Divine Word" <note>original Greek: Μαννα . . . τον πρεσβυτατον των οντων Λογον Θειον (Manna ... ton presbytaton tōn ontōn Logon Theion)</note>. By this Manna was signified *the most ancient of beings, the sacred Logos*, whom he has elsewhere styled "the second Divinity" <note>original Greek: ὁ δευτερος Θεος (ho deuteros Theos)</note>.

<summary>This page explores Philo of Alexandria's interpretation of manna, arguing that he viewed it not just as physical food, but as a symbol for the Divine Logos, the "second Divinity" and the spiritual nourishment of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Logos, Manna, Word of God, Divine Word, spiritual nourishment, second Divinity, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Philo of Alexandria's writings, arguing that his interpretation of the "heavenly bread" (manna) as a symbol for the Divine Word was likely influenced by early Christian teachings or the Gospel of John.</meta>

Such is the explanation <note>original: "solution"</note> of the mystery concerning the heavenly bread—the food of the soul—which was provided to the Israelites in the wilderness. From this interpretation, I think it is clear that he [Philo] was indebted to the account of our Savior's words given by some of the disciples and apostles; the same account which appears in ^1 St. John, chapter 6. The similarity in thought seems to prove it, even though he may have deviated in some ways from the true intent <note>original: "scope"</note> of the doctrine. The following extracts will perhaps clarify what I am saying and show that—
<meta>catchword: Philo</meta>

---

^1 It may possibly be doubted whether Philo obtained this account from the Gospel of St. John, as he might not have lived long enough to have seen it. However, although this doctrine is only passed down to us by St. John, we must not imagine that it was known to him alone. These are the words of Christ which he spoke publicly, and which must have been known to all who heard him. Anyone who consulted his disciples and apostles could easily have become acquainted with them. Philo perhaps learned of them from St. Mark. St. John's Gospel was written before the destruction of Jerusalem, at which time Philo was probably about sixty-eight years old; and he describes himself as living to be old and gray. It is, therefore, not impossible that he might have even seen the Gospel of St. John.

## THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE DOCTRINES OF THE APOSTLES AND OF PHILO BRIEFLY STATED.

It is manifest <note>evident</note> that Philo held the same high opinion of the second Person, the <term>second God</term> <note>original Greek: "deuteros Theos"</note>, as the Apostles and disciples of Christ, and he attributes to him the same qualities. His only failure is in not admitting that the <term>Logos</term> <note>a Greek term meaning "Word" or "Reason," used by both Philo and the author of the Gospel of John to describe a divine intermediary</note> appeared in the flesh. Consequently, Philo did not recognize that he had two natures—a divine and a human one—and two distinct roles <note>original: "characters"</note> which should not be confused. 

Instead, Philo takes all the attributes of each role and applies them to only one being. Therefore, he identifies the <term>Logos</term> not only as the Image of God and the Creator of the world, but also as the Mediator and Redeemer of mankind, through whom the ransom for sin and the price of redemption were paid. He is the same figure who provided heavenly food to the soul and served as the Shepherd of God’s chosen flock. Lastly, he imagines him to have been the great High Priest through whom intercession was made, and sin <note>The text continues on the next page with the word "expiated," meaning to atone for or make amends.</note>

<summary>This page compares Philo's philosophical "Logos" with the Christian Apostles' understanding of Christ, noting that while Philo shares many views on his divine attributes, he fails to recognize the incarnation of the Word into human flesh.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Logos, Christology, Mediator, Redeemer, High Priest, Greek philosophy, Early Church doctrine</keywords>

--- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Philo Judaeus’s theology, specifically focusing on how he describes the "Logos" as a High Priest who atones for sin, while comparing this to the Christian understanding of Jesus Christ.</meta>

...expiated <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "...and sin expiated." To expiate means to atone for or make amends.</note>; and of whom Aaron, with his censer, was a symbolic representation <note>original: "type"</note>. He therefore, as we have seen, tells us—"We say that the High Priest is not a man, but the Divine Word" <note>original Greek: "Λεγομεν τον Αρχιερεα ουκ ανθρωπον, αλλα Λογον Θειον ειναι"</note>—and adds—"without any share in wrongdoing" <note>original Greek: "παντων αδικηματων αμετοχον"</note>—*I maintain that this High Priest is not a man, but the divine Word of God, the Logos, and that he is free from all sin.* 

But he would have expressed this doctrine more accurately by saying, "We say then that the High Priest is not strictly the Divine Word, but Christ Jesus, the Son of Man, and the God-man, who is free from all wrongdoing" <note>original Greek: "Λεγομεν ουν τον Αρχιερεα ου κυριως τον Λογον Θειον ειναι, αλλα Χρισον Ιησουν, τον υιον του ανθρωπου, και τον Θεανθρωπον, παντων αδικηματων αμετοχον"</note>. *I say then, that this High Priest (of whom Aaron is represented as a symbolic representation) was not properly the divine Logos, but Christ Jesus, the Son of man, both God and man, who did no sin; but sinless as he was, died for the sins of the world.*

The entire nature and role <note>original: "character"</note> of our Savior is acknowledged by Philo, but it is transposed and misapplied.

<summary>This page argues that while the philosopher Philo correctly identified the "Logos" as a sinless High Priest, he failed to recognize that this figure is specifically Jesus Christ, who is both divine and human.</summary>

### THE GREAT SIGNIFICANCE OF THE EVIDENCE PROVIDED BY PHILO.

I have shown that Philo was probably born around the time of our Savior’s coming into the world. It is certain that about eight years after the death of Christ, he was sent from Alexandria as an ambassador to Rome, and he lived until the time of Nero. I repeat this now to prove that he had opportunities to see and converse with some of the first disciples of the Apostles, and even with the Apostles themselves. Based on his age and background (for he was born in Jerusalem), we might even imagine that he could have seen their great Master <note>Referring to Jesus Christ</note> in person. I would judge from many passages <note>original: "articles"</note> in his writings that he was familiar with the first three Gospels; he either borrowed from them or owed much of his knowledge to the Christians of his time. It is not improbable that

<summary>This page establishes Philo's historical timeline, suggesting that because he lived during the time of the Apostles and was born in Jerusalem, he likely had direct contact with early Christian teachings or perhaps even Jesus himself.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Chronology, Alexandria, Nero, Apostles, Jerusalem, Gospels, Early Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for Philo's importance as a historical witness. It suggests that Philo is a more reliable source than the later Greek Fathers because he wrote before any potential doctrinal corruptions entered the early Church and because his status as a non-Christian makes him an unbiased witness.</meta>

( 216 )

...that he had access to both. Therefore, his testimony regarding many major and significant points <note>original: "articles"</note> is of great importance, for he uses the same terminology <note>original: "speaks the language"</note> as the Apostles and the earliest teachers of the Church.

The testimony of the early Greek Fathers has always been held in high regard. However, Philo's evidence carries even more weight and certainty. Because he lived many years before them, he wrote many of his treatises before any doctrinal errors had entered the early Church <note>original: "infant Church"</note>. Furthermore, since he was not a follower of Christianity, he could not be biased in its favor. We have seen that the points he recorded—which have been presented here—align with the teachings of the Apostles, except for a few misinterpretations already mentioned. Therefore, we cannot find more convincing proof of the meaning <note>original: "purport"</note> of these doctrines. Philo was captivated by their truth and majesty <note>original: "sublimity"</note>, and he adopted them as his own. We receive these teachings through him, just as we receive the light of the

sun

<summary>This page argues that Philo’s writings provide uniquely authoritative evidence for early Christian doctrine because he was a contemporary of the Apostles and an unbiased, non-Christian witness. The author claims Philo's works preserve the "sublime" truths of the early Church before any later errors could emerge.</summary>
<keywords>Philo, Greek Fathers, Early Church history, Christian doctrine, Apostles, historical testimony, bias, sublimity</keywords>

sun reflected from a mirror—though not so abundant, nor so powerful, as from the original source; yet very genuine, and sufficient to show the fountain of light from which they are derived.

# CONCLUSION.

Let me then conclude with the words of the Apostle Saint Paul, when he gave some very significant advice to the people and Church at Colosse <note>Colosse: an ancient city in Phrygia, in modern-day Turkey</note>.¹

> That you might walk worthily of the Lord, being pleasing in all things, being fruitful in every good work and increasing... Giving thanks to the Father, who has qualified us to share in the inheritance of the saints in the light. He has delivered us from the power of darkness and transferred us into the kingdom of the Son of his love. In whom we have redemption through his blood, the forgiveness of sins. He is the image of the invisible God, the firstborn <note>original Greek: Περιπατησαι ὑμας αξιως τȣ Κυριȣ εις πασαν αρεσκειαν, εν παντι εργῳ αγαθῳ καρποφορȣντες και αυξανομενοι ...... ### THE SENTIMENTS OF DR. ALLIX CONSIDERED.

Since I wrote this treatise, I have found that what the learned editor of Philo has said concerning the birth and age of his author is taken entirely from the work of Dr. Allix <note>Peter Allix (1641–1717) was a prominent French Protestant theologian who wrote extensively on the history of the Church and the Trinity.</note> titled *The Judgment of the Ancient Jewish Church Against the Unitarians*. I am obliged to differ with this very respectable writer, just as I have previously differed with the aforementioned editor who borrowed so heavily from him. His effort is to portray Philo as being very advanced in years during the reign of Caligula—which is the very time, as Photius¹ tells us, when he was in his prime. However, I have already said so much on this point that there is no need to make any further addition. He admits that Philo could not have borrowed his opinions from Plato, nor could he have obtained them through reason, for they were beyond the wisdom of unassisted man. He 

¹ Chapter 105, page 278, line 29. "Philo flourished in the times of Gaius Caesar <note>original: Εν τοις χρονοις (Φιλων) ηκμασε τα Γαιου του Καισαρος. Gaius Caesar is the Roman Emperor Caligula.</note>."

<meta>catchword: therefore</meta>

<summary>The author disputes Dr. Allix’s claim that Philo was elderly during Caligula’s reign, arguing instead that Philo was in his intellectual prime and that his theological insights were too advanced to have originated from Plato or human reason.</summary>
<keywords>Dr. Peter Allix, Philo of Alexandria, Caligula, Photius, Plato, Unitarianism, Jewish Church history</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Peter Allix’s chronological arguments regarding Philo of Alexandria's age. It specifically examines the evidence from Josephus and Jewish tradition used to claim Philo was seventy years old during his embassy to the Emperor Caligula.</meta>

<page-num>( 222 )</page-num>

[Dr. Allix] therefore concludes that these ideas were inherited by Philo from the Jews; he tries to prove that the Jews fully possessed this treasure of knowledge. He cannot believe that Philo had access to any of the Apostles or first Christians because of his advanced age, for he supposes him to have been seventy years old at the time of his *embassy.*

---

->*The Author’s mode of argument.*<-
> “Josephus, in his *Antiquities* <note>original: *Antiquities Lib. xviii. c. 10.* Josephus’s history of the Jewish people.</note> Book 18, Chapter 10, assures us that Philo was the leader and most significant of the Jews employed by the people of Alexandria in the embassy to Caligula. This man, says Josephus, being eminent among those of his nation, appeared before Caligula [before] his death, which occurred in <term>A.U.C.</term> <note>original: *Anno Urbis Conditae*, Latin for "in the year of the founding of the city" (Rome). A.U.C. 793 is approximately 40 AD.</note> 793—that is to say, in the fortieth year of our Lord. Now Philo, in the history of his mission to Caligula, says of himself that he was at that time all grey with age, which means 70 years old according to the Jewish understanding of a man with grey hair, as seen in the *Pirke Avoth*, <note>original: *Pirke Avoth c. 5.* A collection of ethical teachings and maxims from the Rabbis of the Mishnaic period.</note> Chapter 5. Suppose then that he was 70 years old when he appeared before Caligula; it follows that he was born in the year of Rome 723. Suppose also that he began to write at 30 years old; that would fall in the year of Rome 753—that is to say, 30 years before Christ preached in Judea. For Jesus Christ did not begin to preach until the year of Rome 783.” — Dr. Allix, page 80.

---

The whole of this argument depends upon one point taken for granted: that Philo was 70 years old when he went upon this embassy. For this claim, there is not the least foundation.

<summary>The author critiques Dr. Allix's chronological calculations, which use Jewish tradition and Josephus to argue that Philo was seventy years old during his embassy to Caligula and therefore too old to have been influenced by Christianity.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Dr. Peter Allix, Josephus, Caligula, Pirke Avoth, A.U.C., chronology, Jewish history, Early Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Peter Allix’s arguments. The author is refuting the idea that Philo of Alexandria's silence regarding Jesus Christ proves he did not borrow ideas from early Christians.</meta>

<page-num>( 223 )</page-num>

I have shown before that almost every page in the *Treatise* on which he bases his argument demonstrates the opposite.

Another reason he urges to prove that *Philo* was not indebted <note>original: "beholden"</note> to Christians is that he never mentions the name of Christ. But why should this be surprising if, after everything he borrowed, he still remained a Jew? It should be considered that although he was of that race, he never once introduces the name of Jehovah, nor of the Messiah, who was widely expected by the Jews at that time. Nor does he take notice of several books or writers of the Old Testament. When he first went to Rome, it was to counter all the slanders <note>original: "calumnies"</note> with which *Apion* of Egypt had burdened the Jewish nation. Yet in his account of that event, he never once mentions Apion's name. We cannot, therefore, rely on conclusions drawn from the silence of *Philo*.

*Justus of Tiberias* <note>original: *Justus Tiberiensis*. A Jewish historian and rival of Josephus.</note> was from Galilee and lived during the time of our Savior; yet he also made no mention of Christ or Christianity.

<summary>The author argues that Philo’s silence regarding Jesus Christ does not prove he was unaware of Christian teachings, noting that Philo similarly omitted other major figures and names from his writings. He points to Justus of Tiberias as another contemporary historian who remained silent on the rise of Christianity.</summary>

<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Dr. Peter Allix, Christ, Messiah, Apion of Egypt, Justus of Tiberias, Jewish history, argument from silence</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Dr. Peter Allix’s chronological errors regarding Philo of Alexandria. The author argues that Allix wrongly attributed Philo's ideas to earlier Jewish traditions rather than recognizing potential Christian influence.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>

As a result of this original mistake regarding the age of Philo, Dr. Allix continues to show throughout his work that all these profound <note>original: "weighty"</note> truths found in this author were obtained from his fellow Jews and can be seen in their Mishna, Targums, and other books. Because of this, he constantly appeals to the compilers of those writings to prove that they held the same opinions. However, although he quotes extensively based on his vast learning, there are many great truths in Philo that are neither mentioned by Dr. Allix nor found among those Jewish writers. Furthermore, this appeal is misdirected and of little significance. For almost every ¹paraphrase, along with—

---

¹ The earliest of these writings is the Chaldean <note>original: "Chaldee"</note> Paraphrase of Onkelos, and the next is the Targum of Jonathan, which are supposed to have been composed a few years before Christ. But this claim rests merely upon Jewish traditions, which are not at all uniform and are therefore very doubtful. The other Talmuds <note>original: "Talmudim"</note> were much later. In the year 120 after the burning of the Second Temple <note>original: "Anno a Templi Secundi incendio cxx"</note>—the Mishna. In the year 300 <note>original: "Anno ccc"</note>—the Jerusalem Talmud. Finally, in the year 436 <note>original: "Anno denique ccccxxxvi"</note>—the Babylonian Talmud. See Pietro Galatino <note>original: "Galatini"</note>, book 1, chapter 5, page 13. See also Brian Walton’s *Polyglott Bible*, Introduction <note>original: "Prolegomena"</note>, pages 82, 83.

<summary>The author critiques Dr. Allix for claiming Philo's theological ideas were derived from early Jewish texts like the Mishna and Targums, arguing that these texts were actually composed much later than Allix suggests.</summary>

<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Dr. Peter Allix, Mishna, Targums, Onkelos, Targum of Jonathan, Talmud, Pietro Galatino, Brian Walton, Jewish tradition, chronology</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument against Dr. Peter Allix, asserting that Philo of Alexandria’s writings preceded the major Jewish commentaries and could have been influenced by early Christian figures like Saint Mark.</meta>

<page-num>( 225 )</page-num>

<margin>
However, Jacob Bryant <note>Jacob Bryant (1715–1804) was a prominent English scholar and mythographer who argued that Philo’s logos was influenced by Christianity.</note> neglects the Essenes, and the ultimate proof <note>original: "proof of the pudding"</note> of this is found in the Dead Sea Scrolls.
</margin>

The Gemara, Mishna, Talmuds, and Targums <note>These are the primary collections of Jewish civil and ceremonial law and biblical commentary.</note>—regardless of who wrote them or what they are called—were written after Philo’s time. Because Philo was held in such high regard, these later writers might have copied from him, but he certainly could not have borrowed from them. The Jewish people were definitely familiar with some significant truths relevant to our current topic; these can be found in their own Sacred Writings <note>Referring to the Hebrew Bible/Old Testament.</note>. However, there are other truths of equal importance <note>original: "moment"</note> that could only have been known through a later revelation. These are found in great abundance within Philo's works. As for the objection that he could not have had any interaction <note>original: "intercourse"</note> with Saint Mark or any of the disciples of Christ because he lived too early, his own evidence has already shown that this is a baseless <note>original: "ill-grounded"</note> idea.

<summary>The author argues that Philo of Alexandria wrote before the major Jewish Talmuds and Targums, suggesting he did not borrow from them, and asserts that Philo was contemporary enough to have met Christ's disciples.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Gemara, Mishna, Talmud, Targum, Revelation, St. Mark, Jacob Bryant, Essenes, Dead Sea Scrolls</keywords>

# PART III.

### SOME OBSERVATIONS UPON PART OF A TREATISE WRITTEN BY THE REV. CHARLES HAWTREY, M.A.<gloss>1</gloss>

---

I have, and I think very rightly, recommended this treatise.<gloss>2</gloss> However, there is one section where I cannot agree with the author. In that section, he attempts to prove that Christ, in his human form <note>original: "state of manhood"</note>, was the original Son of God, and that the *Logos* <note>The "Word" or divine reason, a term used in the Gospel of John to describe the pre-existent Christ.</note>, or Word, previously <note>original: "antecedently"</note> was not his Son. Accordingly, he says, "Therefore it appears to be

---

<gloss>1</gloss> This treatise is entitled *The God-Man of the New Testament* <note>original Greek: Θεανθρωπος της καινης διαθηκης</note> and was published in 1794.

<gloss>2</gloss> On page 57 of this work.

<summary>The author begins a critique of a 1794 theological work by Charles Hawtrey, specifically disputing Hawtrey's argument that Christ only became the Son of God during his incarnation rather than being the Son eternally.</summary>
<keywords>Charles Hawtrey, Christology, Logos, Son of God, Incarnation, 18th-century theology, Theanthropos, Trinity</keywords>

<page-num>( 230 )</page-num>

be the clear <note>original: "express"</note> doctrine of the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>, however it may have been overlooked, that the sonship <note>original: "filiation"</note> consisted—and consisted only—in the Word's becoming flesh."$^1$ Again—"The *Logos* <note>The "Word" or divine reason</note>, also, in uniting himself with human nature, became the Son of God;$^2$" and was not the Son of God, as it is suggested, prior to that union. For the Author had said before (on page 40), "That the sonship <note>original: "filiation"</note> consisted in the birth of the *Logos* and in the union with human flesh <note>original Greek: σαρξ ανθρωπινη</note>."

According to this doctrine, the Divine *Logos* <note>original Greek: Λογος</note>, or Word of God, must not be regarded as the Son of God until his appearance upon earth.

But how can we reconcile this with the various passages from the sacred Writers, where the contrary seems to be maintained? It is said—that *God sent his only-begotten Son into the world, so that we might*

<meta>catchword: live</meta>

<meta>This page continues the argument against the idea that Christ only became the Son of God at the Incarnation, focusing on scriptures that describe the Son being "sent" into the world.</meta>

*live through him.*<sup>1</sup> If he was the only-begotten Son of God when he was sent, he must have held that status <note>original: "character"</note> before he arrived; his sonship <note>original: "filiation"</note> came before <note>original: "was antecedent to"</note> his appearance on earth.

It is said again—*And we have seen, and do testify, that the Father sent the Son to be the Savior of the World.*<sup>2</sup> I must therefore repeat the same argument—if the Son of God was appointed and sent for a particular purpose, he must have existed in that role <note>original: "character"</note> before that purpose began. Whoever is sent must exist before the act of sending; this appears from the words of our Savior himself—*I proceeded forth and came from God; I did not come of my own accord, but he sent me.*<sup>3</sup> And who was the person sent? We have seen before that it was the *Son of God* by his proceeding—

<sup>2</sup> Ibid <note>original Latin: "Ibidem," meaning "in the same place."</note> 4:14.

<sup>3</sup> John 8:42.

<summary>The author argues that because the New Testament describes the Son being "sent" by the Father, Christ must have already been the Son of God prior to his birth on earth.</summary>
<keywords>pre-existence, Son of God, filiation, incarnation, Savior, 1 John 4:9, John 8:42, Christology</keywords>

--- Page 268 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Son of God existed as a divine person prior to the Incarnation, citing specific verses from Romans and the Gospel of John to show that Christ's earthly appearance was a subsequent state to his eternal nature.</meta>

<page-num>232</page-num>

from the Father. The passages in Scripture for this purpose are many. *God sent his Son in the likeness of sinful flesh*;<sup>1</sup> that is, in a new role <note>original: "character"</note>. It is plainly suggested <note>original: "intimated"</note> that there was a time when the Son was not in the flesh, but was a divine person without anything human. There is a remarkable instance in the Gospel of St. John<sup>2</sup> where he mentions that they beheld *the glory of Christ*; he illustrates this by repeating the word *glory* and saying, *as of the only-begotten Son of the Father*. We find that the glory of Christ was like that of the only Son of God.

Christ in the flesh, therefore, came long after <note>original: "was far posterior to"</note> the divine Person <note>original: "Personage"</note> to whom he is compared. His appearance was what one would expect from the Logos <note>original: "Logos," referring to the "Word" or divine reason of God described in John 1</note>, with whom he was united; he shared in the brightness of the Logos as far as flesh and blood could participate. When it is said—<sup>3</sup>*All things that the Father has are mine*—<sup>4</sup>*And now, O Father, glorify me,*

<sup>1</sup> *Romans* 8:3.
<sup>2</sup> *John* 1:14.
<sup>3</sup> *John* 16:15.

<summary>The author uses New Testament verses to argue that Christ's earthly existence in human flesh was a later state for a divine Person (the Logos) who previously existed in glory with the Father.</summary>

( 233 )

*...with your own self with the glory which I had with you before the world existed*—can we suppose that this fatherhood <note>original: "paternity"</note> is to be dated from Christ's time at Bethlehem or Nazareth, or that it is to be limited to the age of Augustus Caesar? Since it was given by the Father before the creation, and the gift was to the Son, the sonship <note>original: "filiation"</note> must have commenced at that early time <note>original: "season"</note> when the Word <note>original: "Logos"</note> proceeded from the Father; *and being in the form of God, did not consider it robbery to be equal with God; but made himself of no reputation, and took upon himself the form of a servant, and was made in the likeness of men.*¹

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that Christ’s identity as the Son of God is not limited to his human life on Earth, but originates in his eternal existence before creation.</meta>

### CONCERNING OUR SAVIOR BEGOTTEN BEFORE ALL WORLDS.

The author says on page 43, "I do not see how the <note>original Greek: γεννηθεντα προ παντων των αιωνων</note> *begotten before all worlds* is to be supported by anything in the New Testament." This seems extraordinary, because it is said that Christ, in his divine character, was the <note>original Greek: πρωτοτοκος πασης κτισεως</note> *first-born of all creation*¹ and existed before all worlds—for they were made by him.² He is also styled <note>original Greek: μονογενης</note> *only-begotten*, or *only-begotten Son*. But Adam is called the "Son of God" by St. Luke (3:38). Therefore, this title of "Son" cannot be attributed to Christ solely in his human state, for there were others called "sons" as men before him. Instead, it relates to the "only-begotten" who existed before all worlds. If, therefore, Christ in the flesh is ever alluded to as the "first-begotten" or "only-begotten" of God, it arises merely from his intimate union with the <term>Logos</term> <note>The "Word"; the eternal, divine nature of Christ that existed before his human birth.</note>, to whom this title primarily belonged.

---
¹ Colossians 1:15. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ² Hebrews 1:2.

<summary>The author defends the doctrine that Christ was "begotten before all worlds," arguing that his title as the "Son of God" refers to his eternal divine nature rather than just his human life.</summary>
<keywords>Christology, Logos, pre-existence, Son of God, Adam, New Testament, St. Luke, Colossians 1:15, Hebrews 1:2, only-begotten, first-born</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of an author's view on the titles of Christ, specifically arguing that the term "First-born" must imply Christ's existence before his physical incarnation on Earth.</meta>

The learned author is aware that the passage in Colossians 1:15 works against his opinion. Accordingly, he states in the Appendix (page 184) that "the words <note>original Greek: μονογενης</note> *only-begotten* and <note>original Greek: πρωτοτοκος</note> *first-born* are never used anywhere in Scripture to refer to the <term>Logos</term> <note>The "Word"; the eternal, divine nature of Christ</note>, but solely to the <note>original Greek: Υιος</note> *Son*." However, if the Logos and the Son are the same, this objection carries no weight. But how can—<note>original Greek: πρωτοτοκος πασης κτισεως</note> *the first-born of all creation*—be a title attributed solely to Christ in his human form? He was revealed in the flesh so long after that creation in which he had already been the great agent of the work.

The author continues to try and avoid this difficulty by suggesting that <note>original Greek: πρωτοτοκος</note> *first-born* signifies his pre-eminence or superior status here, rather than his *priority in time* (page 185). But the word *first-born* cannot be made to mean anything more or less than *first-begotten* or *first-produced*. And when it is said of a person that he was not only first-begotten, but begotten before all created things, it must refer to priority of existence.

<summary>The author argues that the titles "only-begotten" and "first-born" in Colossians 1:15 refer to Christ’s eternal pre-existence before creation, refuting the idea that they only describe his human life or social rank.</summary>
<keywords>Colossians 1:15, first-born, only-begotten, Logos, Christology, pre-existence, incarnation, Greek translation, theology</keywords>

...as well as to pre-eminence. There is no evading the force of the Apostle’s words.

<margin>Hebrews 1:2</margin>
But the Author adds: “If it signifies priority in point of time, or of existence, will it not be to blend Jesus Christ with the mass of creation? To make him thereby the first created of the works of God?”

**Answer.** The Author seems to suspect that there is great uncertainty in his arguments; and he therefore tries to force us into his opinion by the dread of the consequences. But the alarm is vain: and no such consequences follow. He should remember that the <term>Logos</term> <note>The "Word"; the eternal, divine nature of Christ</note>, or Word of God, was not created. He was the instrument of the Deity, through whom also he made the worlds. <note>original Greek: δι’ οὗ καὶ τοὺς αἰῶνας ἐποίησεν; from Hebrews 1:2</note> He produced all things both *visible* and *invisible*. Why is it imagined that this all-productive power must necessarily be blended with the works of his own hands? How does his priority connect him with any subsequent matter or Being? He proceeded from the Father; but we must not from hence suppose that he was first created, or created at all. The

<summary>The writer argues that Christ’s priority in time does not mean he is a created being, explaining that as the Logos, he was the instrument through whom God created the entire universe.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, Christology, creation, Hebrews 1:2, pre-existence, deity, theology, visible and invisible</keywords>

The author does not consider that the Word was united with the Deity alone, and not with any finite or perishable being at this creation. He was not created, but begotten. Surely this is to kick against the goads. <note>original Greek: προς κεντρα λακτιζειν; a Greek idiom meaning to struggle uselessly against a superior power or fate</note>

The Author goes to great lengths toward his conclusion in order to support his favorite notion. He accordingly says (on page 184), “If the terms first-begotten, or only-begotten, had in Scripture been applied to the <term>Logos</term> <note>original Greek: Λογος; the "Word," referring to the divine nature of Christ</note>, the doctrine of Arius, I believe, should not have been objected to.” This is surely said with too little caution. On the next page, he gives a reason for his opinion that has a dangerous tendency: “*The doctrine of eternal generation, if I may be permitted to give my own opinion of it, strongly favors the cause of Arianism.*” <note>Arianism: a 4th-century theological view that Christ was a created being, subordinate to God the Father, and not co-eternal with him</note> 

Regarding eternal generation, I can say nothing, as there is no such doctrine in Scripture; nor could I ever comprehend the notion. It seems to be an expedient devised to avoid some imagined difficulties. But suppose we were to grant that such a

<summary>The writer critiques the opposing author for suggesting that the terms "first-begotten" or "only-begotten" support Arianism, while also rejecting the non-scriptural concept of "eternal generation."</summary>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of a theological argument regarding "eternal generation" and whether the status of Christ as a "Son" necessitates his inferiority to God the Father.</meta>

generation has existed, how does it at all favor Arianism <note>Arianism: the theological belief that Jesus Christ was a created being and is subordinate to God the Father</note>—the very doctrine it seems to have been introduced to oppose? He explains this through a hypothesis: “For, if it is true, then Christ—by virtue of being a Son—was always subordinate to the Father,” and so on.

But why is it assumed to be true that, because he is called a Son, he is subordinate or in subjection? This point, however, is maintained more than once; it is argued that “<term>filiation</term> <note>filiation: the fact of being a son, or the relationship of a son to a father</note> implies inferiority.” But in this notion, I fear that the author pays more attention to words than to the actual nature of things.

It is true that in this world, a helpless child depends on its parents from birth because of its weakness and the nature of its existence, remaining in subjection to them for a long time. But we must not assume that this same dynamic exists in heaven. For there is not the slightest analogy between the birth of a human child and the "production" of the <term>Logos</term> <note>original Greek: Λογος; "the Word," referring to the divine nature of Christ as described in the Gospel of John</note>. Therefore, no fair comparison can be made between the relationship and union of the Logos and the Father, and the relationship of a child to an earthly parent, where there is neither the same spiritual union nor a direct bodily connection.

<summary>The author argues against the idea that Christ’s title as "Son" implies he is inferior or subordinate to God the Father, dismissing earthly parent-child analogies as inapplicable to the divine nature of the Logos.</summary>

## THE AUTHOR SEEMS TO RUIN HIS OWN PURPOSE.

Throughout his entire treatise, the author has been trying—with great learning and much success—to prove the union and unity of the Godhead <note>Godhead: the essential nature or essence of God, usually referring to the Trinity</note>, as well as the divinity of the Logos <note>original Greek: Λογος (Logos); "the Word," referring to the divine nature of Christ as described in the Gospel of John</note>. However, he believes all this must be abandoned if we admit that the original Logos, or Word, was the Son of God. It is as if these established doctrines could be discarded simply because of a name, title, or way of describing him.

When we are told by the Evangelist <note>Evangelist: refers here to John the Apostle, the author of the Gospel of John</note>—*In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. All things were made by him*, etc.—if after this he is called the *Son of God*; *his first-begotten*; or *his only-begotten*; where is the inconsistency? Even if our own biases create a perceived difficulty, how can that invalidate those clear and essential truths mentioned above? Therefore, we can accept that Christ, in his divine nature, is the Son of God and still be far removed from the notions of *Arius* <note>Arius: a 4th-century priest whose teachings, known as Arianism, claimed that Jesus Christ was a created being and therefore subordinate to God the Father</note>. We need not be worried on that account.

<summary>The author argues that calling the Logos the "Son of God" does not contradict his divinity or the unity of the Godhead, dismissing fears that such titles lead to Arianism.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, Godhead, Trinity, Arius, Arianism, Son of God, divinity, Gospel of John, Christology</keywords>

Saint Paul, speaking of the insufficiency of the law, tells us that this failure was made up in Christ. — *For what the law could not do, because it was weak through the flesh <note>original: "flesh," here referring to the limitations of human nature</note>, God, sending his own Son in the likeness of sinful flesh, and to deal with sin, condemned sin in the flesh.*¹ We find that Christ in the flesh was only a likeness of the Son of God, who was sent from heaven. — *Who, being in the form of God, did not consider it robbery to be equal with God; but made himself of no reputation, and took upon himself the form of a servant, and was made in the likeness of man.*² The Son of God, therefore, was in the form of God before he took upon himself the likeness of man—that is, before he was either sent or came; before he was conceived and took on human flesh. *For God so loved the world that he sent his only-begotten Son, so that whoever believes in him should not perish but have everlasting life.*³

## NOTHING IN THE DOCTRINE CONTRADICTORY TO REASON.

I am convinced, and have for a long time been of the opinion, that this doctrine—though complex <note>original: "abstruse"</note> and a mystery—can be shown from the evidence of Scripture to be perfectly consistent <note>original: "consonant"</note> with reason and by no means incomprehensible. The manner in which the process <note>original: "operation"</note> was carried out may be beyond human understanding; but the great work itself, as described by the sacred writers, is, I believe, easy to understand <note>original: "apprehended"</note>.

I believe, therefore, that there is one God from eternity to eternity—that is, of endless duration, without beginning or end—from whom all things originated <note>original: "proceeded"</note>. This is beyond my comprehension, because I cannot grasp eternity, nor do I have a precise knowledge of anything infinite. But my reason tells me most certainly that there

<summary>The author argues that while the inner workings of divine mysteries may be beyond human grasp, the doctrines themselves are consistent with reason and clearly presented in Scripture. He reflects on the human inability to fully comprehend infinite concepts like eternity despite their logical necessity.</summary>
<keywords>reason, divine mystery, Scripture, eternity, infinity, nature of God, logic, theology</keywords>

...must have been something through all of boundless duration. For (as I have said elsewhere), if there had originally been nothing, there could have been no result <note>original: "produce"</note>—no byproduct <note>original: "derivative"</note>, whether good or evil. Nothing could have been brought about if there were no <term>efficient cause</term> <note>The primary agent or force that produces an effect.</note>, for an effect without a cause is inconceivable. Existence <note>original: "Being"</note> cannot emerge from <term>non-entity</term> <note>Non-existence or "nothingness."</note>.

There must, therefore, have been an original power, without beginning or end, which was the cause of all other beings. The first creation <note>original: "production"</note> of the <term>most High</term> was his Son, who came forth <note>original: "proceeded"</note> from him and who shares in the <term>divine nature</term>. He is called the <term>first-begotten</term> of God and of all creation. Through him, all things that exist were made; every subsequent being was the work of his hands and bears witness to his divine wisdom. Was the second person of the Trinity then co-existent with the <term>Deity</term>? Certainly in respect to his <term>essence</term> <note>The fundamental nature or "whatness" of a being.</note>, though not in terms of his <term>personality</term> <note>The distinct individual "personhood" of the Son as distinguished from the Father.</note>. For this <term>essence</term>, which he possessed as the Son, was made of the same spiritual and eternal <term>substance</term> <note>The essential reality or divine "matter" that constitutes God.</note> as

<summary>The author argues that existence must stem from an eternal "efficient cause" rather than nothingness, identifying the Son of God as the first creation who shares God's eternal essence but possesses a distinct personality.</summary>

the Father’s, before the <term>personality</term> <note>The distinct "personhood" or individual identity of the Son as the second person of the Trinity, as distinguished from the Father.</note> commenced. Was this personhood then produced in time? Undoubtedly; for whatever is brought into effect must be brought about in time. Some earlier power must produce it.

However difficult it may appear to man’s limited understanding, every effect—however remote—must be surrounded by a boundless duration in both directions: both before and after. An eternity must have passed, and an eternity must follow. Should the <term>Logos</term> <note>original: "Logos"; a Greek term meaning "Word," used in Christian theology to refer to the second person of the Trinity (Jesus Christ) as the eternal Word of God.</note> not then be considered eternal? Not in respect to his personhood, for 그 specific form <note>original: "modification"</note> took place only before creation. But the <term>essence</term> <note>The fundamental nature or divine substance of a being.</note> from which he originated was certainly eternal. He is eternal because of his participation in the divine nature, which had no beginning.

At this point, I am obliged to differ from <term>Dr. Eveleigh</term> <note>John Eveleigh (1748–1814), an English clergyman and Provost of Oriel College, Oxford, known for his Bampton Lectures on the Trinity.</note> in his excellent discourse upon this subject, where he introduces the following words of our Savior:^1 *“And now, Father, glorify me in your own presence with...”*

*“...the glory I had with you before the world began.”* <note>A quotation from the Gospel of John 17:5.</note> Regarding this, it is said that his existence as God with God is here called the glory which he had with the Father; and the time when he had this glory, instead of being called *“in the beginning,”* is said to have been *“before the world was.”* *Both are expressions covering the same duration; both imply eternity.* He had previously said (on page 10), quite rightly, that the divine nature was eternally possessed by the Son. the Father, was produced at a particular period, and his <term>personhood</term> <note>original: "personality"; referring to the distinct identity of the Son as the second person of the Trinity.</note> had a beginning. I believe that many errors and destructive <note>original: "fatal"</note> disputes have resulted from this truth not being properly observed. I therefore repeat that this modification of the divine nature was not, nor could it be, from all eternity. When the sacred Writers speak of the Word as the second person, I believe it will be found that they never speak of him in that role <note>original: "character"</note> as being from everlasting, nor do they suppose him to have existed <note>original: "subsisted"</note> in this way from all eternity.

<margin>* John 1:1-2.</margin>
*“In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. He was in the beginning with God.”* Eternity has no beginning. Therefore, there is no reference to eternity here. Every beginning must start from a specific point, however distant and unknown that point may be. For this reason, we may be certain that the <term>Logos</term> <note>original: "Logos"; the Greek word for "Word," used in the Gospel of John to describe the pre-existent Christ.</note>, or Word, was only the first-born in relation to the creation that followed. Our Savior suggests <note>original: "intimates"</note> as much in his prayer to God: *“And now, O Father,”*

<summary>The author argues that biblical references to "the beginning" regarding the Word (Logos) indicate a specific point in time when the Son's distinct personhood began, rather than referring to absolute eternity. He suggests that the Son is the "first-born" in relation to subsequent creation but did not exist as a distinct person from all eternity.</summary>

### THE CONCEPT OF ETERNAL GENERATION CONSIDERED AGAIN

Those who hold the idea of "eternal generation" <term>eternal generation</term><note>A traditional theological doctrine stating that the Son is eternally "begotten" of the Father, having no beginning in time despite being a derivative of the Father.</note> seem to be misled by a term for which they can have no definite knowledge. It was introduced merely as a help toward solving a supposed difficulty which, I think, never existed. In short, it is a greater mystery than the one it is brought to explain. A person might just as reasonably insist upon an "eternal creation," and it would appear just as plausible to many. But at this rate, it would

<vocab>self-existent, eternal generation, divine Nature, mystery, eternal creation</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that while the Son is divine and united with the Father, he was "produced in time" rather than being eternally begotten. He critiques the doctrine of "eternal generation" as an unnecessary and confusing mystery that is no more logical than the idea of an "eternal creation."</summary>
<keywords>Christology, Eternal Generation, Trinity, Divine Nature, Self-existence, Creation</keywords>

--- Page 290 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of "eternal generation," arguing that the concept is a logical contradiction and that the Logos (the Son) must have had a beginning in time as a derivative of the Father.</meta>

...it would be found that the world was formed by divine wisdom and yet never had a beginning—which is as absurd as it is untrue. These thinkers push the object of their study as far out of sight as possible, ostensibly to get a better view of it. But the entire argument is a fallacy. It is therefore useless for them—just like the schoolmen <term>schoolmen</term><note>Medieval theologians and philosophers who used highly technical, often abstract logic to analyze Christian doctrine.</note> of the past—to use terms with no precise meaning, and especially words with no meaning at all, to explain what they do not understand. We can never find clarity by returning to darkness, nor can we resolve one difficulty by introducing another that is much greater.

This is proven by the previously mentioned doctrine of "eternal generation," which seems designed to confuse rather than to teach, and implies a logical contradiction. We have seen that the Logos <term>Logos</term><note>original: "λόγος"; a Greek term meaning "Word" or "Reason," used in theology to refer to the second person of the Trinity.</note> proceeded from God and was begotten by the Father. But how could he have been begotten, or have proceeded, if he never had a beginning? I do not know who first created this line of reasoning, but it seems to be based on nothing more than metaphysical sophistry <term>metaphysical sophistry</term><note>The use of overly complex or abstract reasoning to create a deceptive or flawed argument.</note>.

<summary>The author dismisses the doctrine of eternal generation as an illogical fallacy, comparing it to the abstract and confusing language used by medieval schoolmen. He argues that if the Logos was "begotten" or "proceeded" from God, he must have had a definitive beginning.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, eternal generation, schoolmen, metaphysical sophistry, divine wisdom, Christology, logical contradiction</keywords>

## AN OBJECTION STATED.

It may be asked, *Why could there not be an eternal generation of the Son, just as there is an everlasting duration of the Father? Has not God existed through all eternity?* The Deity most certainly has always existed and will endure forever. But there is a great and irreconcilable difference between these two points of doctrine <term>points of doctrine</term><note>original: "articles"</note>; therefore, we cannot form any accurate analogy between them. 

The great, everlasting, and self-existing God does not owe His being to any power or to any prior <term>prior</term><note>original: "antecedent"</note> cause; for it is completely <term>completely</term><note>original: "to the last degree"</note> absurd to suppose anything exists before what is eternal. There was, therefore, no process <term>process</term><note>original: "operation"</note> involved in His production, for He was not produced—being, as was previously observed, self-existent and prior to all things. 

But in respect to the Word of God—the Logos <term>Logos</term><note>original: "λόγος"; the Greek term for "Word" or "Reason," used here to refer to the Son of God.</note>—there was a prior purpose and an action <term>action</term><note>original: "operation"</note> involved in His production. He was begotten by the Father, which suggests a specific event:

<meta>and</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while God the Father is self-existent and uncaused, the Son (the Logos) cannot be "eternally begotten" because the act of being begotten implies a prior purpose and a beginning.</summary>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that because the Son was "begotten," this event must have occurred at a specific point in time, rather than being an eternal state without a beginning.</meta>

( 256 )

<meta>catchword: AN</meta>

...and as I asserted before—and I believe it is beyond contradiction—every fact must have been completed in time. For this reason, it is said, "*This day have I begotten you*" <note>A reference to Psalm 2:7, later quoted in the New Testament (Hebrews 1:5) to describe the status of Jesus.</note>, which plainly proves that this act <term>act</term><note>original: "operation"</note> could not have happened in any way other than within time. This is further suggested in our Savior’s address to God, when He says: <margin>John 17:5.</margin> "*And now, Father, glorify me in your own presence with the glory that I had with you before the world existed.*" 

He does not say "the glory which I had always, throughout all eternity," but only "prior to the Creation." When this was accomplished, we do not know; we only learn this much: that before all things were created, Christ was begotten of His Father, and that was when the relationship of Father and Son <term>relationship of Father and Son</term><note>original: "filiation"</note> began. Therefore, we may agree with the words of the Nicene Creed <note>The Nicene Creed, formulated in 325 AD, is a foundational statement of Christian belief regarding the nature of God and Jesus Christ.</note>, where these doctrines are very accurately set forth and require our attention and faith:

> "I believe in God . . . And in one Lord
> Jesus Christ, the only begotten Son of God:
> Begotten of the Father before all worlds:
> God..."

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of the Nicene Creed, specifically focusing on the relationship between God the Father and the Son, and the author's rejection of the concept of "eternal generation."</meta>

( 257 )

"God from God, Light from Light, true God from true God <note>original: "Very God of very God." In this context, "very" means "true" or "actual," derived from the Old French *verai*.</note>, begotten, not made, being of one substance with the Father, by whom all things were made." Christ is the same as the Father and eternal in respect to his essence and original divinity; but he is posterior <note>Coming after or later in time.</note> in respect to his sonship <term>sonship</term><note>original: "filiation"</note> and adoption. This adoption and sonship must have occurred within time.

I believe, therefore, that those who argue for "eternal generation" <note>The traditional Christian doctrine that the Son is eternally begotten by the Father, having no beginning in time.</note> run into very unnecessary difficulties, if not outright absurdities. For they assume a fact can be accomplished without a beginning, or a wonderful operation can occur without any primary efficient cause <note>In philosophy, the "efficient cause" is the agent or force that actually brings something into being.</note>—that is, without an operator—which is impossible. This trouble is, I say, unnecessary, as everything mentioned in Scripture about the *Logos* <note>original: "Logos" (Greek: λόγος), meaning "Word." In Christian theology, it refers to the second person of the Trinity, Jesus Christ.</note>, or the Word of God, can be more clearly proved using much better principles.

Regarding the Word <note>original: "Logos" — a Greek term used in the Gospel of John to describe the second person of the Trinity, Jesus Christ, as the eternal expression of God.</note> proceeding from God and partaking of His divine nature, I cannot see anything in it more difficult to believe than in the conception and birth of a human being, or in the growth of fruit in a field. The process, whether on earth or in heaven, is equally mysterious to me and beyond my understanding. Yet I would have to abandon my senses if I did not believe the one, and my reason and religion if I denied the other; for it has been passed down to me with the highest sanction and the most unquestionable authority. If there is any difficulty, it arises from faulty reasoning.
