For, as I have previously suggested, can it be more extraordinary for God in His infinite wisdom and power to produce from Himself a Divine Being—the exact image of His person and glory <note>original: "brightness" — an allusion to Hebrews 1:3, describing Christ as the "brightness of [God's] glory and the express image of his person."</note>—than for an animal, by blind instinct, to create a likeness of itself and produce its own species? It may be said that both the instinct and the act of reproduction are ultimately from God. This is very true. Why then do we presume in any respect to limit the Almighty, and think that for Omnipotence <note>original: "Omnipotence" — the quality of having unlimited or very great power.</note> one operation is more difficult than another?

### CONCERNING MELCHIZEDEK, KING OF SALEM.

It is said that after Abram returned from the *defeat <note>original: "slaughter"</note> of Chedorlaomer and the four kings in the Valley of Shaveh <note>original: "Shavah"</note>, Melchizedek, King of Salem—who was also the priest of the Most High God—brought out bread and wine and blessed* Abram*, saying:*

*“Blessed be* Abram *of the Most High God, possessor of heaven and earth; and blessed be the Most High God, who has delivered your enemies into your hand.”*

*And he* (Abram) *gave him a tenth <note>original: "tithes" — a traditional religious offering consisting of one-tenth of one's possessions or earnings.</note> of everything.¹*

There have been many theories <note>original: "conjectures"</note> concerning this passage and a wide variety of opinions. However, I believe that if we consider the context and the words of the Apostle Paul, this passage will not seem mysterious <note>original: "obscure"</note> at all and will present no great difficulty.

representation of him in human form. It is accordingly said of him that *he was made like the Son of God*, a symbolic image of the Christ to come.¹

All of this would have appeared very clear if not for a mistake that has persisted in almost all translations; it was first introduced by the authors of the Greek version <note>original: "greek Version" — referring to the Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note>. The words in the original Hebrew are *Melchi zedec* and *Melech Salem*. Although two of these terms vary slightly in their form, they signify *the King of Righteousness* and *the King of Peace*. This is well known, and we have the additional authority of Saint Paul, who was an excellent judge of their meaning.² Now, the first two terms are kept in the standard versions without any interpretation of their meaning; meanwhile, the other two terms are partly translated and partly left as they were in the original. The latter is rendered in our version <note>likely the King James Version</note> as *The King of Salem*; this "Salem" is generally supposed

---

¹ Hebrews 7:3. &emsp;&emsp; ² Ibid. Hebrews 7:2.

<summary>The author argues that Melchizedek’s titles "King of Righteousness" and "King of Peace" identify him as a divine figure, but notes that these meanings were obscured when early Greek translators treated the Hebrew descriptors as proper names.</summary>

<meta>The author continues to argue that the failure to translate the Hebrew titles "Melchizedek" and "Salem" has led to historical confusion, specifically by turning descriptive attributes into geographical names and personal identities.</meta>

( 264 )

to signify Jerusalem. It is hard to imagine how much confusion has been cast upon this history by the fact that these words—which are sufficiently clear in the original Hebrew—have been left without an explanation. By introducing the person’s character and office as if they were a proper name, one of his divine attributes is instead represented as the name of a place in Canaan.

<margin>In Hebrew Traditions <note>original: "*In Tradit. Heb.*"</note></margin>
It may be worthwhile to take note of the false interpretations which have resulted from this, and the inconsistencies that have been maintained. In the first place, because the words "Melchizedek" <note>original: "Melchi zedec"</note> have been accepted as a man's proper name, many have taken great pains to find out who that man could have been. Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 342–420 AD), the scholar responsible for the Vulgate, the primary Latin translation of the Bible</note> says that some supposed he was Shem, the son of Noah. But who can believe that the Patriarch Shem, even if he were ever a king, would have reigned in the idolatrous region of Canaan?

<summary>The author argues that mistaking Melchizedek’s titles for proper names has led to the historical error of identifying him as the Patriarch Shem ruling in Canaan.</summary>

<keywords>Melchizedek, Jerusalem, Salem, Jerome, Shem, Noah, Canaan, biblical translation, etymology, Hebrew Traditions</keywords>

--- Page 303 ---
<meta>The author continues to argue that treating "Melchizedek" as a personal name has led to various contradictory and historically impossible genealogies being attributed to him.</meta>

<margin>* Pages 49, 50.</margin>
According to the author of the *Easter Chronicle* <note>original: "*Chronicon Paschale*", a 7th-century Byzantine universal chronicle</note>, he was a descendant of Ham. It is equally unbelievable that anyone from the lineage of Ham should be a priest of the Most High God. Suidas <note>The *Suda* is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia, formerly attributed to an author named Suidas</note> follows the same reasoning and tells us that he was the son of Side, the son of Egyptus <note>original: "Ægyptus"</note>, King of Libya; and that he was himself King of Canaan and reigned in Jerusalem, then called Salem. He says further that he was King of the Jews, and <note>original Greek: "εκ Ιουδαιων μονον," meaning "of the Jews only"</note> not only of the Jews, but of the Gentiles in general—and all this in the time of Abraham <note>original: "Abram"</note>, before Abraham even had a child.

There has hardly ever been such a collection of absurdities. How could a son of Side, or a son of Ham, or a son of Noah, be a person who had *neither father nor mother, who was of no lineage <note>original: "descent"</note>, and had neither beginning of days nor end of life?* <note>The author is quoting Hebrews 7:3 to point out the theological contradiction of assigning a human genealogy to Melchizedek</note> And how could he reign over the Jews before any of the family of Judah or Jacob even existed?

Similar mistakes occur concerning Salem, which is represented as a city. Josephus says that Melchizedek reigned there, and that it was the same as Solyma, which was afterward called Jerusalem.¹

<margin>
² See Joshua 10:1 and Judges 1:21. It is called Jerusalem all through the Old Testament.

This is a great mistake; for it was called Jerusalem before the Israelites were in possession of it, and the name is continually repeated throughout the Scriptures.² And what is very extraordinary, it was never called Solyma—at least the name does not once occur in the Sacred Writings, neither in the original Hebrew nor in the Greek Version <note>The Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>. It was a name formed by the Greeks afterward, who changed Jerusalem <note>original Greek: Ἱερουσαλημ</note> to Hierosolyma <note>original Greek: Ἱεροσολυμα</note>; and who would persuade the world that it was a compound word formed from the Greek word *hieros* <note>original: ἱερος, meaning "holy" or "sacred"</note> and Solyma. The same is observable in the etymology of the former name, which has been in like manner deduced by some from *hieros* and Salem.

<meta>The author continues to argue that Salem and Jerusalem are two distinct locations, criticizing ancient and medieval scholars who conflated them based on faulty Greek etymologies.</meta>

It is stated in the *Etymologicum Magnum* <note>original: "Etymologicum Magnum," a major Byzantine Greek encyclopedia and dictionary compiled around 1150 AD</note> that *"Jerusalem was first called Salem; but when Christ appeared there, it was named Hier-salem <note>a combination of the Greek word "hieros" (holy) and "Salem"</note>, the holy City of Salem."* This shows us the level of absurdity to which some people will go.

However, there is not a single instance in the Bible where Salem is used as a substitute for Jerusalem, except in those passages in Genesis where it is translated that way by a great mistake. The only place where it seems to have been considered a proper name is in some versions of the seventy-sixth Psalm, verse 2, where it says: *"In Judah is God known—and his tabernacle is in Salem."* But here the ancient Greek version <note>The Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>² differs and provides the meaning more accurately: *"And his place of residence was established in peace"* <note>original Greek: και εγεννηθη εν ειρηνη ὁ τοπος αυτȣ</note>—or

...which to this day is called Salem; and the Palace of Melchizedec is shown there, showing its magnificence by the scale of the ruins of the ancient work. <note>original Latin: "quod usque hodie appellatur Salem : et ostenditur illic Palatium Melchizedec, ex magnitudine ruinarum veteris operis ostendens magnificentiam."</note> Even if this palace—which must have existed in the time of Abraham—had been ever so splendid, and its ruins as magnificent as Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the primary translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible</note> would persuade us, we may still be well assured that Melchizedec never reigned there. What he mentions regarding Salim, others refer to Zion, just as their fancy directs—"on the mountain called Zion." <note>original Greek: *Εν τω ορει τω λεγομενω Σιων*</note> Suidas <note>a massive 10th-century Greek encyclopedic lexicon</note>.

--- Page 309 ---
<meta>The author continues to argue that Melchizedek is a divine figure rather than a human king, addressing a potential counterargument regarding similar names found in the Bible.</meta>

<page-num>271</page-num>

However, regarding Melchizedek <note>original: "Melchizedec"</note>, a learned friend* suggested to me that there is an instance of a man being named in this manner. This can be found in the name Adoni-zedek <note>Hebrew: "Lord of Justice"</note>, *the Lord of Justice*, which was the name given to a King of Canaan in the days of Joshua. There is certainly a perfect parallel between them, though with some differences. For we see that the person with whom Abraham <note>original: "Abram"</note> had an interview was not only described as the King of Justice, or *righteousness*, but also as the <margin>Salem</margin> Prince of *Peace*. This renders his character far more specific and extraordinary. 

Therefore, I believe that I may still venture to say that no mortal man was ever so highly distinguished. In addition to this, as I have observed before, these marks of distinction are not properly names but significant and prophetic titles. They belong to a divine Personage and are unique to the character of one whose kingdom was to be founded in righteousness and maintained in peace.

---

* The Reverend Mr. Peter Roberts of Eton.

<margin>
It is nonsense to suggest that receiving tithes is an act that can only be performed by a physical, mortal man.
</margin>

We can therefore be certain that this magnificent figure, who appeared to Abraham <note>original: "Abram"</note> and who was *without beginning of days or end of life*, and also *without ancestors*, could be no other than the Divine Logos <note>Greek: *logos*, meaning "Word"</note>, or Word of God. They were, therefore, both the same divine Person appearing in a similar form, but at two different times. The first appearance in a human form was introduced to give Abraham a hint of the actual, eternal High Priest who was to come; the first was merely a temporary type <note>In theology, a "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament that foreshadows someone or something in the New Testament</note> of him. For even though he came before, it is expressly said *that he was made like the Son of God*. Consequently, he, just like the latter, is said—*to remain a Priest forever*.¹

I believe, therefore, that the account given by Moses might be translated in the following way:

> *And the King of righteousness (the same as the Prince of peace) brought forth bread and wine; and he was a Priest of the Most High God.*

<meta>This page continues the argument that the Apostles and St. Paul often used common language and names familiar to their audience, even if those names were technically titles or had deeper linguistic meanings, in order to avoid distracting from their primary message.</meta>

they preached was not harmed by their acceptance of common usage. They never worried about whether it was Balaam the son of Beor or of Bozar; whether it was Joshua or Jesus <note>In the Greek New Testament, the names Joshua and Jesus are written identically</note>; Elijah or Elias; Eleazar or Lazarus; Quirinus or Cyrenius; *Jerusalem* <note>original: Ἱερουσαλὴμ</note> or *Hierosolyma* <note>original: Ἱεροσόλυμα</note>. They mentioned such names as were in use among the people to whom they addressed themselves and which were best understood.

For this reason, St. Paul accepted Melchizedek being treated as a proper name because it was so highly regarded by the Hebrews to whom he wrote. Yet he clearly hints that it ought properly to be understood in another way, for the significance <note>original: "purport"</note> of the history depended upon the true interpretation. And if this is so, the words that make up those supposed names should be interpreted accordingly, and thus accepted for the sake of spiritual instruction <note>original: "edification"</note>.

and Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize Greek philosophy with Jewish scripture</note> maintained—and as Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius, a 17th-century Dutch legal scholar and theologian</note>, Le Clerc <note>Jean Le Clerc, an influential Genevan theologian</note>, and others have since supposed—it was for the refreshment of his small army. The former was created in conformity to the latter, and therefore was "another and yet the same" <note>original Latin: "alius et idem"</note>—the very Word <note>original Greek: "Logos"</note> in a human form, and a representation of Christ, who was to come in the flesh. This Person, I say—being like the Son of God, and indeed being the very Son of God—remains a Priest forever.*³

It is because of this description that I have been led to assert that this King of Righteousness, who appeared to Abraham <note>original: "Abram"</note> in a human shape, was the Word of God, called

### CONCERNING A METHOD OF EXPLANATION USED BY SOME MISSIONARIES.

It is said that when Spanish missionaries in America wanted to explain the divine hypostasis <term>hypostasis: the fundamental reality or substance that constitutes the distinct persons of the Trinity</term>, they used the image of a tree with two branches as a symbol <note>original: "emblem"</note>. This was meant to show that unity can coexist with different levels of division and distinct personhood. 

Through this appropriate analogy, they propose and then solve all the objections and difficulties that have been raised regarding the primary subject—the Trinity—by using this specific model <term>type: a symbolic representation or foreshadowing</term>. They ask, therefore, whether this magnificent tree is one or many. The answer given is that there are certainly three portions—divided but not separated—existing in a strict union: Three in One. 

If their audience objects that the reverse must also be true, and that "One must be Three" (which would seem to be a contradiction), this objection is overruled by the physical object shown before...

<meta>catchword: before</meta>

<summary>This page describes an analogy used by Spanish missionaries in the Americas, who used a tree with two branches to explain the concept of the Trinity—illustrating how three distinct parts can exist as one unified whole.</summary>
<keywords>Spanish Missionaries, America, Trinity, divine hypostasis, analogy, Three in One, religious education, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 320 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the tree analogy used by Spanish missionaries to illustrate the Trinity, specifically focusing on how the branches (representing the distinct persons) are inseparable from the trunk (the divine essence).</meta>

<page-num>282</page-num>

before their eyes. There, they see a clear demonstration that unity can be expanded <note>original: "dilated"</note> into plurality <term>plurality: the state of being more than one; in this context, the three persons of the Trinity</term> while the connection and union are preserved. They are further taught that two of these portions are derivatives <note>referring to the branches stemming from the main trunk</note>, which are in strict union with the tree itself. They remain firmly connected and, in a sense, part of the same body, even though they are distinct in their arrangement and purpose.

Therefore, they exist alongside <note>original: "co-existent with"</note> the parent tree, for they share the same origin as the main body from the start. Although they appear later in time regarding their growth <note>original: "protrusion"</note> and division, they collectively form one and the same object.

I have mentioned that the Jews certainly had traces of the Supreme Hypostasis <term>Supreme Hypostasis: the fundamental substance or "personhood" of the Godhead; in this context, it refers to the Trinity</term>. Their Jehovah, the Angel of the Covenant, was no other than the second Person in that Triad <note>original: "Triad"; refers to the Holy Trinity</note>. Under the authority of the Father, He was the great operator in the work of creation. He appeared to their ancestors <note>original: "Fathers"</note>, and they looked up to Him as their guardian Deity. They were likewise not ignorant of the Holy Spirit, who co-operated in all things, *by which God adorned <note>original: "garnished"</note> the heavens. You send forth your Spirit, and they are created: and you renew the face of*

*the earth.* <note>original: "Psalm civ. 30"; In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 104:30: "You send forth your Spirit, they are created: and you renew the face of the earth."</note> The Jews were acquainted with these first principles of divine knowledge long ago. I believe they brought these ideas, along with other information from the History of Moses, into China when they were occasionally admitted into that country—particularly into the province of Henan <note>original: "Honan"</note>. Their entry may have occurred not long after their first captivity <note>The "first captivity" likely refers to the Assyrian or Babylonian captivities of the Jews in the 8th or 6th centuries BCE.</note>. Although some of these points may not be directly relevant to my current topic, I will not omit them, as I believe the reader will find them very satisfying.

Some of these extracts are taken from the Chinese historian Luo Bi <note>original: "Lo-pi"</note>, who lived during the *Song Dynasty* <note>original: "Dynasty of Song"; the Song Dynasty ruled from 960 to 1279 CE.</note> about eight hundred years ago. However, the books he consulted for information, and from which he quotes, are of a much earlier date. They are of the greatest antiquity and are said to have been written many ages before the Christian Era.

Others are taken from the *Shujing* <note>original: "Chou-king"; also known as the *Classic of History* or *Book of Documents*</note>, which is considered to be the most sacred book among the Chinese. It is held in the same reverence as the Pentateuch of Moses <note>The first five books of the Hebrew Bible</note> is among the Jews, and it is supposed to be of even greater antiquity. The books *Yijing* <note>original: "L' Y-king"; the *Book of Changes*</note> and *Dazhuan* <note>original: "Ta-tchouen"; the *Great Commentary* on the Yijing</note> are as old as Confucius, who lived five hundred years before Christ.

What I have mentioned—that the Spanish missionaries represent a certain concept by a tree with two arms—the ancient Chinese represented by an emblem which bears a strong analogy to it. This was a figure like the Greek Upsilon, **Y**, which they called *u*, or rather *y*. And the book in which this mystery is explained has the name *Yijing*—*the Book of Y*—which is extraordinary.

¹ Among the ancient Chinese characters that have been preserved, one finds this one: △. According to the *Kangxi Dictionary* <note>original: "Dictionaire de Kang-hi"; the standard dictionary of the Qing Dynasty, published in 1716</note>, this character signifies *Union*. Let us listen to what the Chinese say regarding its analysis. According to the *Shuowen* <note>original: "Choue-ouen"; the *Shuowen Jiezi*, an influential dictionary from the 2nd century AD</note>, this highly praised book states that △ **is three united in one**.

The *Liushutong* <note>original: "Lieou-chou-tfing hoen"; a scholarly work on the origins and forms of ancient characters</note>, which is a reasoned and scholarly explanation of the most ancient characters, expresses it this way: △ **signifies intimate union, harmony, the primary good of man, of heaven, and of earth. It is the union of the three Cai.** (*Cai* <note>original: "Tsaï"; likely referring to the *Sancai* or "Three Powers": Heaven, Earth, and Humanity</note> signifies principle, power, or ability within the *Tao*:) for when united, they lead together, create, and nourish. **Note:** Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>, who does not mention Jesus, is in full possession of the Alexandrian Logos <term>Logos</term> doctrine—the divine creative Word. <note>In ancient philosophy, the "Logos" refers to the principle of cosmic reason or the "Word" of God used in the creation of the universe.</note> This was a constant and ancient feature of Egyptian doctrine. Therefore, the Logos doctrine is merely one element within Judaism, but in Egypt, it existed for many centuries before the Jews resided there—and it was the most characteristic part of Philo's theology <gloss>the theology of Philo</gloss>. Consequently, the Logos doctrine of the Apostle John and the Christian Gnostics <term>Gnostics</term> <note>Early Christian groups who sought "gnosis" (special knowledge) and often blended Christian teachings with Greek and Egyptian philosophical traditions.</note> came to them from Egypt. page 3

# Notes

10 — <term>Gnostics</term> <note>Gnostics were members of various early Christian and pre-Christian religious sects who believed that "gnosis" (secret spiritual knowledge) was the key to salvation.</note> affirmed the divinity of <term>Christ</term>; they denied the humanity of <term>Jesus</term>. <note>Many Gnostic groups held a "docetic" view, believing that Jesus was a purely divine being who only appeared to be human, rather than being flesh and blood.</note>

21 throughout — <term>Philo</term> <note>original: "passim" — a Latin term meaning "throughout" or "in various places" in a text.</note> on the Word. <note>Referring to Philo’s development of the "Logos" (the Word) as a mediator between God and the world.</note>

41 — Pre-Christian <term>Christianity</term>. <note>This refers to the idea that many "Christian" doctrines, such as the trinity or the savior-god, existed in other forms before the formal establishment of the religion.</note>

130-1 — Compare with <note>original: "cf." — an abbreviation for the Latin *confer*, meaning "compare."</note> Humanist doctrine (compare with <term>Tammuz</term> the Shepherd Savior). <note>Tammuz was an ancient Mesopotamian god of fertility and shepherds whose mythical death and resurrection were later compared by scholars to the story of Christ.</note>

191 and following — <note>original: "f." — and the following page or pages.</note> Compare with the idea that the "Temple of God is within you."

206 and following — <note>original: "f." — and following.</note> On manna. <note>In the Bible, manna is the miraculous food provided by God to the Israelites in the desert; Philo and other philosophers often interpreted it allegorically as divine wisdom.</note>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which featured a list of topical notes and page references dated 1770, this page provides a specific theological observation regarding the origins of the "Logos" doctrine.</meta>

**Note:** Philo <note>original: "Philo"; Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE) was a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>, who does not mention Jesus, is in full possession of the Alexandrian Logos doctrine—the divine creative word—which was a <unclear>constant</unclear> and ancient feature of Egyptian doctrine. 

Therefore, the Logos doctrine <note>The "Logos" (Greek for "Word" or "Reason") refers to the principle of divine reason and creative order.</note> is nothing but a hint in Judaism; however, in Egypt, it was part of the most characteristic <unclear>part of the</unclear> theology for many centuries before the Jews lived there. Thus, the Logos doctrine <insert>of Philo</insert>, of John <note>Referring to the author of the Gospel of John, which begins: "In the beginning was the Word (Logos)."</note>, and of the Christian Gnostics <note>Early Christian sects that emphasized secret knowledge and often blended Greek, Jewish, and Egyptian philosophical elements.</note> came to them from Egypt. g3

<summary>This note argues that the "Logos" doctrine, central to the Gospel of John and Gnosticism, originated in ancient Egyptian theology rather than Judaism, reaching Christian thinkers via Philo of Alexandria.</summary>
<keywords>Philo of Alexandria, Logos doctrine, Egyptian theology, Gnosticism, Gospel of John, Hellenistic philosophy, religious syncretism</keywords>
